Officer After Landing Sequence. ......................................................................................... 04-14.4

PURPOSE
The ERJ 175 Standard Operating Procedures Manual (SOPM) SP 3700 contains the standard
operating procedures used by flight deck crewmembers for the operation of SkyWest Airlines’
ERJ 175 aircraft.
The objective of this SOPM is to provide standardized procedures and processes for flight deck
crewmembers to perform their duties and responsibilities with the highest degree of safety
and standardization.
COMPLIANCE
Compliance with applicable Federal Aviation Regulations (FARs), Operations Specifications (Ops
Specs), and foreign regulations is mandatory.
The SOPM does not contradict any FAR, Ops Spec, or applicable foreign regulation. When a
conflict occurs between its contents and the compliance with any FAR, Ops Spec, or foreign
regulation, the latter takes precedence. However, when SkyWest policy is more restrictive,
SkyWest policy takes precedence. Any such conflict should be reported to the Manager Flight
Standards.
Any differences between the SOPM and procedures provided by the aircraft manufacturer are
based on improvements in safety and operational practicality.
14 CFR Part 121.133(a) requires SkyWest Airlines to prepare and keep current manuals for the
use and guidance of employees to conduct daily operations. The ERJ 175 SOPM complies with
14 CFR Parts 119 and 121. Source documentation for the ERJ 175 SOPM includes:
• Airplane Operating Manual (AOM)
• Embraer Standard Operating Procedures Manual
• FARs, Airworthiness Directives, Aeronautical Information Manual (AIM), and Advisory
Circulars (ACs)
• Flight Operations Safety Management System (SMS) Manual SP 3000
The SOPM is applied in all line operations, training, and checking events.

RESPONSIBILITY
The Director Aircraft Operations is responsible for the quality of flight standards processes.
In the absence of the Director Aircraft Operations, the Manager Flight Standards, assumes
responsibility.
AUTHORITY
The Manager Flight Standards has authority to establish and modify all processes related to the
ERJ 175 Standard Operating Procedures Manual (SOPM) SP 3700.
CONTROLS
The FAA-approved SkyWest Pilot Training Program requires all pilots to be trained and evaluated
to determine their knowledge of the structure, use, and content of the SOPM. This knowledge is
evaluated during ground and flight training, to include operating experience and line checks.
Pilots must pass all required training and evaluation before being allowed to conduct line
operations.
CrewQual ensures that no SkyWest pilot is assigned a flight operations function for which that
pilot has not been trained and fully qualified.
ERJ 175 Standard Operating Procedures Manual (SOPM) SP 3700 is continuously evaluated,
revised, and improved by a Standard Operating Procedures Committee (SOPC) designated by
the Manager Flight Standards. The SOPC meets periodically to review established procedures
and make recommendations and changes, as necessary. Human factors principles are
incorporated in the design of SOPM checklists and associated procedures.
The SOPM procedures must be conducted as outlined herein and validated complete using
the prescribed checklists.
This manual must be available and immediately accessible in the flight deck so each flight deck
crewmember can perform their assigned duties and responsibilities. Each assigned user must
comply with the content provided in this manual.
New or updated standards, policies, and procedures are communicated via published bulletins
or temporary revisions. These changes must be adhered to, until permanently placed in the
manual by a permanent revision.
Flight deck crewmembers are encouraged to report any difficulty related to human factors before
they result in incidents or accidents. This is fundamental to identifying potential flight standard

deficiencies. This may be confidentially accomplished through voluntary reporting (e.g., ASAP,
IOR, SCR) or directly to any Flight Operations manager or line check airman.
PROCESS MEASUREMENT
The Director Aircraft Operations is responsible for determining objectives for the ERJ 175
Standard Operating Procedures Manual (SOPM) SP 3700. The Director Aircraft Operations does
this with the aid of the Manager Flight Standards.
The Manager Flight Standards is responsible for the development of procedures to meet the
objectives of this program.
The Manager Flight Operations Safety measures the effectiveness of the ability of the procedures
to meet the objectives of this program. This position is also responsible for ensuring procedures
included in this program are accomplished as they are designed.
Data collection processes to measure the effectiveness of the procedures in the SOPM are
outlined in Flight Operations Safety Management Systems (SMS) Manual SP 3000 Chapter 03
Flight Operations Safety Assurance.
The Manager Flight Operations Safety reviews the data as outlined in Flight Operations Safety
Management Systems (SMS) Manual SP 3000 Chapter 03 Flight Operations Safety Assurance.
SAFETY OWNERSHIP
Safety Ownership is defined, outlined, trained, and measured for Flight Operations within Flight
Operations Safety Management Systems (SMS) Manual SP 3000 Chapter 01 Flight Operations
Safety Management Policy (Safety Ownership paragraph) (Flight Safety library on EFB).

– Chapter 03 Human Factors
– Chapter 05 Flight Operations
– Chapter 06 Airworthiness
– Chapter 07 Adverse Weather
– Chapter 11 Emergency/Irregular Operations

MANUAL OUTLINE
The SOPM is divided into sections:
• Manual Management
• Introduction
• Limitations
• Operating Procedures
• Emergency Procedures
• Definitions
MANUAL REVISION PROCEDURES
The SOPM is:
• Available electronically:
– On SkyWest Online
– Through the Company Manuals application on the Electronic Flight Bag (EFB) (as
applicable)
Revisions are used to communicate and implement changes to policy and/or procedure. Bulletins
are used to quickly communicate and implement critical or operationally pertinent changes to
policy and/or procedure. Bulletins are sent as either an Alert bulletin (FOAB), or Information
bulletin (FOIB). Bulletins are used as operational guidance until revisions are issued.
The Aircraft Operations Department publishes manuals in support of Aircraft Operations,
Flight Operations, Flight Training, and the Operational Control Center (OCC) and does so
in coordination with the Customer Service, Maintenance, InFlight Operations, and Safety &
Quality Departments.
The Manager Flight Standards, or their designee, is responsible to compile and prepare
necessary changes or additions to the SOPM and to disseminate changes to the Flight
Operations Manager Technical Publications for revising, editing, proofing, and gaining
acceptance from the SkyWest Certificate Management Office (CMO) or other necessary parties.

Refer to Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 01 Manual Management for detailed

The Manager Flight Standards receives proposed changes to the SOPM through internal and
external sources (e.g., regulatory, manufacturer, safety programs, industry). The changes are
distributed among the SOPC for review and inclusion in the SOPM. Procedural changes are
routed through the Flight Operations Manager Technical Publications into Workflow where
external departments review, risk assess, and comment. All comments and concerns are
addressed and finalized using the Workflow process prior to forwarding to the Corporate
Technical Publications and Standards (CTPS) Department for CMO submission.
Changes to the procedures and processes of the SOPM are evaluated using the risk analysis
program outlined in Flight Operations Safety Management Systems (SMS) Manual SP 3000.

INTRODUCTION................................................................................................................. 02-01.1
INTRODUCTION
The ERJ 175 aircraft must be operated in accordance with the limitations presented in this
chapter.
In the event a limitation in this manual disagrees with the Airplane Flight Manual (AFM), the
AFM takes precedence.
In the event a placard or instrument marking disagrees with the limitations shown in this manual,
the more restrictive limitation takes precedence.
All limitations are AFM limitations, unless otherwise noted.
A SkyWest imposed limitation is designated by the use of (SW) and may be more restrictive
than the AFM limitation.

OPERATION IN ICING CONDITIONS
There is no temperature limitation for anti-icing system automatic operation.
On ground:
• The TO DATASET MENU on the MCDU must be set to ENG when OAT is from 5°C to
10°C and:
– When there is any possibility of encountering visible moisture up to 1,700 ft AFE.
– When operating on ramps, taxiways, or runways where surface snow, ice, standing
water, or slush may be ingested by the engines, or freeze on engines, nacelles, or
engine sensor probes.
• The TO DATASET MENU on the MCDU must be set to ALL when OAT is less than 5°C and:
– When there is any possibility of encountering visible moisture up to 1,700 ft AFE.
– When operating on ramps, taxiways, or runways where surface snow, ice, standing
water, or slush may be ingested by the engines, or freeze on engines, nacelles, or
engine sensor probes.
In flight:
• The engine and wing anti-ice systems operate automatically, in case of ice encounter when
the ice protection mode selector is in the AUTO position.
• When either one or both ice detectors are failed, the crew must set the mode selector to
ON when icing conditions exist or are anticipated below 10°C TAT with visible moisture.
NOTE:
• Icing conditions may exist whenever the Outside Air Temperature (OAT) on the ground or
for takeoff, or Total Air Temperature (TAT) in flight, is 10°C or below and visible moisture
in any form is present (such as clouds, fog with visibility of one mile or less, rain, snow,
sleet, and ice crystals).
• Icing conditions may also exist when the OAT on the ground and for takeoff is 10°C or
below when operating on ramps, taxiways, or runways where surface snow, ice, standing
water, or slush may be ingested by the engines, or freeze on engines, nacelles, or engine
sensor probes.

INSTRUMENT, NAVIGATION, AND COMMUNICATIONS
NAVIGATION EQUIPMENT
TAS, TAT, and SAT information are only valid above 60 kt.
While transmitting in VHF1, the standby magnetic compass indication is not valid.
Barometric altimeter minimums must be used for all CAT I approaches.
Prior to joining a published segment of an instrument approach, verify the NAVAID identification
visually displayed matches the chart being used. When the identification is not visually displayed,
verify the ID matches the chart being used.
AUDIO PANELS
Do not plug in dual prong and ANR (5-pin) headsets to the same jack panel simultaneously. This
may cause damage to the digital audio panels.
ENHANCED GROUND PROXIMITY WARNING SYSTEM (EGPWS)
Navigation is not to be predicated on the use of the Terrain Awareness System.
The use of predictive EGPWS functions should be manually inhibited when landing at an airport
that is not in the database to avoid unwanted alerts.
The use of predictive EGPWS functions must be manually inhibited during QFE operations,
when GPS data is unavailable or inoperative. Press in the GND PROX TERR INHIB button to
inhibit predictive EGPWS functions.
FMS LIMITATIONS
RNAV (GPS) LP minima are not authorized.

OPERATING PHILOSOPHY
Page: 03-01.2 of 19

PHILOSOPHY OF OPERATION
SAFETY PRIORITY
Passenger and public safety are the highest priority. Safety is achieved through standardization
of flight deck procedures. The primary goal of the SOPM is safety and standardization.
This manual provides mandatory guidance. Flight deck crewmembers are expected to know
and adhere to the procedures and guidance contained herein, unless alternative procedures
are provided (e.g., Special Airport Briefing Guide), or immediate alternative action is required to
maintain the safety of flight.
CREW RESOURCE MANAGEMENT (CRM)
Crewmembers must coordinate their actions, so the airplane is operated safely and efficiently.
Crew resource management (CRM) is the utilization of all available resources, information,
equipment, and people to achieve safety first and efficiency second. Basic prioritization of CRM
can be reduced to “aviate, navigate, and communicate.” A sense of collaboration and mutual
help must prevail to ensure each flight is completed safely and efficiently. For more detail, refer
to the Crew Resource Management paragraph in Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 03
CRM / TEM / FPM (Crew Resource Management).
THREAT AND ERROR MANAGEMENT (TEM)
Threat and Error Management (TEM) involves all crewmembers and is the process the crews
use to proactively identify and manage threats and errors before they impact the safety of flight.
Refer to Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 03 CRM / TEM / FPM (Threat and Error
Management).
CAPTAIN’S AUTHORITY
The captain is ultimately responsible for the flight under their command.
The captain must use crew resources in the most effective way and must encourage other
crewmembers to engage in teamwork by allowing them to participate and give suggestions,
whenever useful for the execution of the flight.
The captain must always treat crewmembers with respect and consideration.

COMMAND
Command is the legally mandated responsibility, authority, and accountability of each PIC. It is the
exercise of duties associated with this position and resides with the PIC for the duration of a flight.
The PIC is the final authority, ultimate decision-maker, and is responsible for the overall safe
conduct of the flight.
In cases where two captains are assigned to a flight, one must be designated as the PIC and
must assume command.
FLIGHT PATH MANAGEMENT
Managing the flight path of an airplane, including trajectory, energy state, power settings, and
automation, is a basic function and responsibility of the flight crew. Managing the flight path,
however, by simply controlling the flight path leaves the task(s) incomplete. Feedback that the
correct inputs have been made and the correct flight path-energy level is being followed is as
important. For more detail, refer to Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 03 CRM / TEM /
FPM (Flight Path Management (FPM) paragraph).
MONITORING
Monitoring is a systematic observation and active participation process that involves
cross-checking and feedback. It is the responsibility of both pilots. Proper monitoring and timely
intervention prevent distractions, inattention, and threats from developing into an undesired
situation. For more detail, refer to Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 03 CRM / TEM /
FPM (Flight Path Management (FPM) paragraph).
DARK AND QUIET COCKPIT CONCEPT
The design philosophy of the airplane states that under normal flight operation of its systems, the
pushbuttons, and annunciators on the overhead, main, glare-shield, and control pedestal panels
must be dark. No visual or aural warnings indicate normal operation of the systems.
BUTTONS CONCEPT
The normal operating position of the push in/out buttons is identified with no light bar illuminated,
regardless of switch position.
Some knobs on the overhead panel have detent protection and must be pulled out to allow knob
rotation. Knob stationary positions are marked with a white rectangle, and knob momentary
positions are marked with a white triangle. To ensure proper signal transmission when using
the selector knobs that have momentary positions, hold the knob for at least two seconds at the
positions before releasing it.

CHECKLIST PHILOSOPHY
Checklists are designed to assist the pilots to ensure they do not forget actions that, when not
carried out, may result in a risk to safe operations. The use of the onboard checklist is based on
the assumption that both pilots have been properly trained on the airplane and therefore have a
thorough knowledge of the airplane's systems and procedures. It further assumes they know the
consequences of not performing the right actions at the right time and in the correct order.
The normal checklist has been divided and named according to its specific phase of operation.
When a checklist is interrupted, it must be started again from the beginning.
The checklists contained in the SOPM supersede all other checklists.
NORMAL CHECKLISTS
Normal checklists outline procedures that must be performed but are not considered to be
comprehensive for all procedures that may be required.
Checklist protocol is defined in the expanded checklist description. A checklist may include
multiple protocols for certain items within that checklist.
There are three checklist protocols used in the SOPM:
READ AND DO
Checklist items must be completed in order while the checklist is read.
TASKS
Tasks are memorized and to be completed prior to reading the checklist. There is no prescribed
sequence to be followed, and items should be accomplished at a logical time prior to the checklist.
FLOW PATTERN
Flow patterns are used to accomplish a series of tasks from memory in a prescribed order
prior to reading a checklist.

CHECKLIST CONDUCT
All checklists must be announced "complete" by the appropriate flight deck crewmember.
SILENT
Checklists are read and completed silently.
CHALLENGE AND RESPONSE
Challenges must be read, and responses stated aloud as described in the expanded checklist.
ON THE GROUND
The captain calls for the appropriate checklist. The checklist is read by the first officer.
IN FLIGHT
The pilot flying (PF) calls for the appropriate checklist. The checklist is read by the pilot
monitoring (PM).
→
CHECKLIST RESPONSE
When a checklist item requires action from both captain and first officer, the notation is CP/FO
("/" equals "and"). When a checklist item requires action and response from the captain or first
officer, the notation is "CP OR FO."
When a response is required from both flight deck crewmembers for an individual checklist item,
the response is given in the order listed on the checklist.
Checklist items preceded by “LFOD” should only be stated on the last flight of the day. “LFOD” is
not stated when reading the checklist.
EMERGENCY / ABNORMAL CHECKLIST
The Emergency/Abnormal Checklist provides the procedural steps required to resolve situations
on the ground or in flight. Refer to Chapter 5 Emergency/Abnormal Procedures.
CREW COORDINATION
COMMUNICATIONS
Non-essential conversation must be avoided during high workload flight phases, especially
during sterile flight deck and high areas of vulnerability. Normal communication between the
airplane and ATC is recommended to be performed by the FO (on ground) and the PM (in flight).
Communications from the flight deck crew to the passengers and between the flight deck crew
and flight attendants (FAs) may be performed by any flight deck crewmember, as directed by the
checklist or at the captain’s discretion.

CREW COMMUNICATION PROTOCOL
The FWD FA is the primary contact when there is communication with the flight deck. The AFT
FA may communicate with the flight deck when necessary. Both flight attendants may pick up
and listen on the interphone. When the AFT FA does not pick up and listen, the FWD FA passes
any pertinent information on to the AFT FA.
CHAIN OF COMMAND AUTHORITY IN THE CABIN
• PIC, FO, FWD FA, AFT FA
• FWD FA, primary communication with flight deck
• FWD FA oversees all duties in the cabin
• AFT FA provides the PIN and passenger count to the PIC
NORMAL OPERATIONS
FLIGHT DECK DOOR
Prior to engine start, the FWD FA verbally states “READY TO TAXI” and gives a thumbs-up
signal and closes the flight deck door.
The flight deck door must be locked prior to engine start and remain so until the aircraft is parked
at the gate and the engines are shutdown, except as necessary to permit access and egress
from the flight deck.
CABIN TAKEOFF BRIEFING / FA NOTIFICATION
Prior to the Before Takeoff Checklist, as flight deck duties allow, the first officer performs a cabin
takeoff briefing. This announcement is conducted as outlined in the Passenger Announcements
section.
As part of the Before Takeoff Checklist first officer flow, the first officer calls the FA using the
interphone and verifies the cabin is secure and ready for departure. The FA answers and
advises the crewmember that:
• The cabin is secure, or
• The cabin is not secure, and the approximate additional time required to prepare the cabin.
Once the cabin is secure, the FA advises the flight deck crew using the interphone that the
cabin is secure and ready for departure.
STERILE FLIGHT DECK SIGNAL
During the crewmember preflight briefing, the captain discusses the following sterile flight
deck protocol:
Takeoff and reaching 10,000 ft or reaching cruise altitude when below 10,000 ft.
• Select the Sterile light switch to OFF. This indicates the sterile flight deck no longer exists. The
FAs may begin cabin duties as conditions permit, but no sooner than five minutes after takeoff.
NOTE: When the flight deck crew feels it is not safe to begin cabin duties, notify the FAs as
soon as possible, and specify the reason for and the length of the anticipated delay.

EMERGENCY WARNING SIGNALS
Flight deck to FA:
In a potential emergency with no time to prepare, the flight deck crewmembers contact the FA via
the interphone. When this occurs, the FA must communicate with the flight deck crewmembers
immediately.
FA to flight deck:
To inform the flight deck crewmembers of an emergency onboard, the FA presses the EMG
button once. When this occurs, the flight deck crewmembers must communicate with the FA
as soon as possible.

The EMG button should not be used to notify the flight deck crewmembers of an evacuation.
To alert the flight deck crewmembers that an evacuation is underway, the FA turns on the
emergency lights.
EMERGENCY COMMUNICATION
EMERGENCY CALL
AURAL / VISUAL SIGNAL REMARKS
THREE HI / LO CHIMES
FLASHING RED LIGHT on FA Indicator
Panel, Changes to SOLID RED after
Call Is Answered
• From the flight deck. Chime(s) heard
in the cabin. Indicates unusual or
hazardous situation. FA communicates
with the flight deck immediately to
determine reason for warning alert. USE
CAUTION.
• From cabin to flight deck. Chime(s)
heard in the flight deck. Indicates
unusual or hazardous situation. The
flight deck crewmember not flying
answers the interphone immediately.
(This is not to be used to alert pilots of
an evacuation. In an evacuation, the FA
turns on the emergency lights.) When no
answer, the flight deck crewmember not
flying observes cabin activity through
the viewing port to determine the reason
for the warning alert. USE CAUTION.
• May include hijacking, terrorist activity,
or any hazardous type of situation,
including medical emergencies
BRIEFINGS
All crewmembers must be informed regarding matters affecting their functions.
The captain must ensure all briefings are conducted in accordance with the outlined procedures
herein.
Only pertinent items specific to each topic in the briefing needs to be briefed.
Refer to Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 03 Human Factors [Threat and Error
Management (TEM)] for situations and examples of TEM countermeasures used during briefings.
CREWMEMBER PREFLIGHT BRIEFING
The crewmember preflight briefing occurs after reporting for duty and prior to pushback. The
captain facilitates this briefing, and all crewmembers should be present and involved. The
crewmember preflight briefing consists of two parts and can be combined: Initial Crewmember
Briefing and All Flights Briefing.

INITIAL CREWMEMBER BRIEFING
The Initial Crewmember Briefing occurs after reporting for duty and prior to pushback, on the
first day of the trip, or when a crewmember change occurs. Topics are listed according to the
acronym ICE:
• Introduction - Crewmember verification (e.g., ensure all crewmembers have required
documents, as applicable; are fit for duty)
• Communication - Cabin/flight deck emergency notification and communication (e.g., distress
or evacuation signal, breach of security or suspicious activity, sterile flight deck). In the event
of suspicious activity or a breach of security, the FA uses plain English without code words or
phrases, using their full name when notifying the flight deck.
• Entry Protocol - The PIC and FAs establish the flight deck entry protocol, and any other
items necessary for the security of the flight (e.g., Pilot Out Of Flight Deck/Flight Attendant
Access To Flight Deck Protocol)
ALL FLIGHTS BRIEFING
The All Flights topics are briefed for each flight leg, always focusing on anticipated threats.
• MX items (Review MEL / CDL / NEF, as appropriate, for operations/performance procedures
and limitations.)
• Taxi time/deice/ground delay potential
• Weather (departure, enroute, turbulence, destination)
• Flight time and cruise altitude
• Diversion potential
• Customer service items
• FAM / LEO / FFDO
• Jumpseat / ACM brief
• Unfamiliar airports or airports with specific procedures
All briefings only need the pertinent items from each list below.
DEPARTURE WAANT BRIEFING
Weather –
• ATIS / ASOS, etc.
• Takeoff minimums/alternate requirements
• Low visibility taxi/takeoff procedures
• Windshear/gusty wind considerations and limitations
• Cold weather operations (deice/anti-ice)
NOTE: When visibility is less than RVR 1,200, review the Surface Movement Guidance &
Control System (SMGCS) Low Visibility Taxi Routes Chart, when published.

Airport Departure –
• Runway of departure (e.g., runway length, surface condition, special considerations)
• Taxi plan/Hot Spots
• SID / ODP / RNAV DPs
• Engine failure after V1 procedures (standard/simple/complex)
→
• Navigation radio management
• Automation (level of automation to be used during departure and climb)
• *Emergency/Abnormal procedures management.
• *Rejected takeoff plan. This includes reasons for rejecting the takeoff and crew duties.
NOTE: * The captain normally conducts these briefing items. These items need only be
briefed on the FFOD for the crew.
Area Terrain Awareness –
• Surrounding Terrain Considerations
• CFIT / EGPWS threats for departure and emergency procedures
• Minimum safe altitudes
• Terrain mitigation strategies
NOTAMS –
• ATIS advisories
• Flight release messages and remarks
Threats –
• Discuss highest threat (e.g., complex taxi procedure, contaminated taxiways, MEL / CDL
limitations, departure procedure, terrain)
DEPARTURE BRIEFING EXAMPLE
The ATIS reports 2,000 ft overcast, winds 270/8 knots. It’s 8 degrees and raining, so be sure
to select ENG in the MCDU for takeoff. We will plan a single engine taxi out to runway 30R
via Charlie, November, and Delta.
This will be my takeoff. The runway is 10,000 ft, dry, and the Autobrake is set to RTO. If we have
an engine failure or emergency prior to V1, we will execute a rejected takeoff procedure and
assume appropriate crew duties, as outlined in the Emergency Memory Items or QRC.
After V1, the special departure procedure is the SIMONS 1 and we will return here, requesting
the ILS to 30R.
The departure clearance is the MOXAM 3 RNAV procedure. We will use the autopilot, per the
RNAV DP protocol with VNAV. Remaining on the departure procedure will keep us clear of the
rising terrain to the north and east.
The highest threat is night, and I am unfamiliar with the airport. Let’s work together and slow
down to prevent any taxi issues.
Do you have any questions or anything to add?

ARRIVAL WAANT BRIEFING
An arrival briefing is conducted as soon as adequate information is available and while pilot
workload is at a minimum (before TOD). MCDU programming and approach set up should be
accomplished before the briefing.
When an IAP is to be flown, or when referencing an IAP for a visual approach at night, a
complete approach plate briefing must be conducted.
Any change to an approach or runway assignment, after the initial briefing has been completed,
requires a re-brief of the changes using WAANT. If time does not allow a re-brief, required FMS
inputs and flightdeck setup, ask for extended vectors or holding until briefing/set up can be
accomplished. Considerations may be given to declining the revised clearance.
Weather –
• ATIS
• Windshear/gusty wind considerations and limitations
• Low visibility taxi
Airport Arrival –
• Identify applicable arrival transition and expected crossing altitudes
→
• Expected runway information (e.g., minimum landing distance assessment, length, surface
condition, approach lighting system, autobrake setting, thrust reverser usage).
• Approach briefing
– Visual
∗ Final approach course
∗ Electronic guidance
∗ Obstacle clearance
∗ Navigation/Radio management
∗ Automation (level of automation to be used during arrival and approach)
∗ Go Around procedures, actions, and callouts
∗ Planned descent transition from arrival to final approach segment
– Charted or RNAV Visual Flight Procedure
∗ All visual approach items
∗ Approach chart number and revision date
∗ Briefing strip
∗ Specific chart notes
∗ Vertical descent Angle/Glide Path Angle
∗ Planned descent rate/ground speed.
∗ Intermediate step-downs and altitudes – minimums
∗ Approach minimums
∗ Missed approach procedures
∗ Attention All Users Chart, if included

ARRIVAL BRIEFING EXAMPLE
The weather is 2,000 ft OVC with winds out of the West. We have the ALEXX1 arrival into ORD
with just one speed restriction, XYZ. Based on the ATIS, we will plan the ILS to 22R.
The runway is 10,000 ft, dry, so I don’t intend to use thrust reversers. Autobrake is set to LOW.
I’ll plan on exiting the runway to the right and taxiing on ‘W’ then ‘B’ to the ramp. It’s ORD, so let’s
plan a two-engine taxi and keep our heads up and out looking for traffic.

There are no terrain considerations for our arrival and approach into ORD, and no NOTAMs for
our runway or expected taxi route.
The highest threat is the non-standard taxi activities at ORD; no stopping once we exit the
runway. Let’s be vigilant and verbally communicate our taxi progress and all taxi instructions.
Don’t be afraid to challenge anything. Let’s brief the ILS for runway 25.
Do you have any questions or anything to add?
DEBRIEF
A debrief is conducted for each flight segment. It may be accomplished at any time after a threat
has been encountered and when:
• Out of sterile flight deck,
• In a low AOV, and
• Workload is low.
At a minimum, a debrief should be conducted at the termination of each flight and may be
initiated by either crewmember.
CALLOUTS
NOTE: Throughout the manual, spoken callouts are formatted in quotations, bold, and caps.
ALTITUDE
When receiving a clearance involving an altitude change, pilots:
• Read back full clearance for altitude changes
• Contact ATC immediately to clarify any discrepancies
• PF briefs the PM when or where a pilot’s discretion climb/descent begins
The pilot who sets an altitude point to the selected altitude readout on their respective PFD and
states the altitude selected. The other pilot points to their respective PFD, confirms the settings
agree with ATC and the other pilot, then reads the setting aloud. Once complete, the vertical
mode change is initiated by the appropriate pilot.
The PF makes an altitude callout at 1,000 ft prior to level off during climb or descent:
PF: “1,000 TO GO”
The PM verifies and states the altitude leaving and the altitude for intended level off:
PM: "ONE SIX THOUSAND (DESCENDING or CLIMBING) ONE FIVE THOUSAND"
During the last 1,000 ft to a climb/descent altitude assignment, both pilots monitor the level-off,
as well as visually scan outside for traffic in VMC.

AUTOPILOT
The PF asks the PM to engage the autopilot. The PM engages the autopilot and confirms
the action. The crew states:
PF: “AUTOPILOT ON”
PM: “AUTOPILOT ON”
EXCHANGE OF FLIGHT CONTROLS
The exchange of flight controls callout establishes positive control of the aircraft and who is the
PF and PM. Any time an exchange of flight controls is necessary, it should be initiated by the
captain. The first officer advises the captain when there is a need to relinquish controls. The
captain then initiates the exchange.
Normal exchanges of flight controls occur:
• When aligned with the runway centerline prior to commencing takeoff
• During the landing roll, as dictated by the captain
• During pilot out of flight deck protocol
• During approach setup, study and briefing if needed
When the captain gives the flight controls to the first officer:
Captain: “YOUR CONTROLS”
First Officer: “MY CONTROLS”
When the captain takes the controls from the first officer:
Captain: “MY CONTROLS”
First Officer: “YOUR CONTROLS”
When in flight, the exchange must include aircraft flight status of heading, altitude, and speed.
Example:
“YOUR CONTROLS, HEADING ONE TWO ZERO, LEVEL THREE SIX ZERO, SPEED POINT
SEVEN EIGHT.”
or
“YOUR CONTROLS, HEADING THREE SIX ZERO, DESCENDING ONE ZERO THOUSAND,
SPEED TWO HUNDRED FIFTY KNOTS.”

SPEED
When calling out speeds for the purpose of airspeed indicator crosschecking and pilot
incapacitation, ensure the actual speed is verbalized. For example, on the takeoff roll, the PM
is responsible for the “80 knots” callout. When the callout is made 10 knots late, the callout
would be “90 knots.”
Airspeed deviations, outlined in the Climb, Cruise, Descent, and Approach Deviations and
Callouts tables, must be announced and corrected. Refer to the respective section for deviations
and callouts.
PASSENGER ANNOUNCEMENTS
Refer to the Cabin Communications and Passenger Announcements paragraphs in Flight
Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 05 Flight Operations (Cabin Communications and
Passenger Announcements) for situations and example announcements.
FLIGHT AUTOMATION
USE OF AUTOMATION
The procedures of this manual assume the full use of all automated features of the airplane.
The flight deck crew is encouraged to use the full capabilities of the Auto Flight Control System
(AFCS) to help reduce workload and improve safety. Pilots should use the available automation
at the level most appropriate to achieve these objectives and to comply with Ops Specs and
airspace requirements. This is not intended to prevent pilots from flying the airplane manually.
Manual flight is encouraged to maintain pilot proficiency, but only when conditions and workload
for both the PF and PM are such that safe operations can be maintained.
Pilots must be alert and avoid "automation complacency" generated by the highly automated
airplane systems. Pilot monitoring is essential and, as an active role, is placed on the same level
of importance as flying, especially when task sharing.
NOTE: When the autopilot causes the airplane to deviate from the desired flight path, use TCS
or promptly disconnect the autopilot and manually return to the desired condition.
The FMA must be continually monitored and cross-checked.
USE OF THE FLIGHT DIRECTOR (FD)
The flight director (FD) is used and updated at all times. The PF can request the PM to perform
the tasks required to maintain the FD update with the maneuvers performed. When the FD is not
synchronized with the actual airplane’s maneuver, it must be updated or turned off.

USE OF THE MULTIFUNCTION DISPLAY CONTROL UNIT (MCDU)
The criticality of proper FMS inputs and settings cannot be overstated. FMS usage must be
coordinated in all phases of flight. All flight plan inputs, including waypoints, speeds, and
altitudes, must be visually confirmed and cross-checked by both pilots before executing.
Before executing, the pilot that makes input sequences through each fix to ensure proper
input and states “CONFIRM.” When the input is confirmed acceptable, the other pilot states
“ACTIVATE.”
On the ground prior to taxi, the PF is the primary manipulator of the FMS. During taxi, the FO
assumes this role.
When the autopilot is engaged, the PF is the primary manipulator of the FMS. During critical
phases of flight, high workload, or task saturation, however, the PF commands flight plan
changes and the PM makes the changes, as instructed, except the PF may select manual speeds
or speed intervention and ACTIVATE APPROACH SPEEDS on their respective MCDU. When
hand flying, the PF commands flight plan changes and the PM makes the changes, as instructed.
USE OF THE AUTOPILOT
Autopilot use is encouraged to help reduce workload and improve safety.
USE OF THE GUIDANCE PANEL (GP)
When the autopilot is engaged, the PF has control of the GP. However, during high workload
conditions, the PF may ask the PM to make the desired mode and altitude selections.
During manual flight, the PF commands selection of the desired modes on the GP, and the PM
performs the mode and altitude selections.
The pilot making guidance panel changes verbally states the change. The other pilot visually
confirms the change.
NOTE: AFCS mode selections must be visually confirmed by both pilots in the Flight Mode
Annunciator (FMA) section.
USE OF SPEED INTERVENTION
CAUTION: It is possible to overspeed the flaps when using speed intervention.
To activate speed intervention, enter the desired speed at LSK 1R on the active flight plan page.
To delete speed intervention, delete the speed at LSK 1R.
Speed intervention is automatically cancelled and resumes FMS constraints when transitioning
between different flight phases (i.e., climb to cruise or cruise to descent).

Date: 11 DEC 23
Upon initial flap selection, speed intervention is cancelled, and the airplane reverts to the green
dot or fixed speed schedule, whichever is selected on the APPROACH SPEEDS page. If speed
intervention is activated again after the initial flap selection, it will remain active regardless of
further flap selections until cancelled by the flight crew. The crew must delete speed intervention
no later than the FAF and allow the airplane to slow according to the landing speed schedule.
USE OF THE AUTOTHROTTLE
Pilots must always be alert and monitor the autothrottle operation, checking the movement
of the thrust levers in the correct direction.
USE OF WEATHER RADAR
The weather radar should be used any time weather is present. The radar should be operated
in STBY mode on the ground and not turned on unless clear of any ground personnel,
equipment/structures, and ready for takeoff. In heavy weather areas, the display refresh rate
may be improved by turning the PM side to SLAVE mode.

GENERAL
Preflight activities are the direct responsibility of the PIC. The PIC may delegate any of these
activities to other crewmembers but is ultimately responsible for ensuring all preflight items
have been correctly and completely performed.
The PIC may not divide the responsibilities of any of the principal functions, (e.g., the external
inspection of the airplane, completion of the manifest); they must be delegated in their entirety or
not at all.
Preflight activities must include at least the following:
• Crewmember briefings
• External inspection
• Security inspection
• Airworthiness acceptance
• Flight attendant responsibilities (the FA reports all cabin safety items and cabin equipment
discrepancies directly to the PIC).
• Obtain WX, clearances, coordinate with ground personnel/pushback crew
• Review of the dispatch release for accuracy, including:
– Remarks
– Flight plan
– Fuel load
– Performance data
– Aircraft "N" number
– Applicable NOTAMS
– Maintenance items, to include deferrals, CDL, and NEF items
– Weather, to include reported and forecast conditions and alternate requirements

SAFETY AND POWER UP EXPANDED CHECKLIST
The Safety and Power Up Checklist must be performed for every crew’s first flight of the day
on a particular airplane, or when a power down was required.
When receiving the airplane already powered up, verify compliance with all items and perform
FIRE EXTINGUISHER panel check.

Required documents and equipment are found on the Required Flight Deck
Documents/Equipment list, EFB Company Manuals application. Reference this list to ensure
required items are onboard and accessible. Required items are either located in the flight deck
and/or on the EFB. If not on the EFB, they must be onboard and located in a manner that
provides access to the flight crew.
When a document or equipment specified to be in the flight deck is missing, a replacement must
be obtained by the PIC before flight. Documents accessed from the EFB Company Manuals
Application must be up-to-date as outlined in Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 05
Flight Operations [Electronic Flight Bag (EFB), Report for Duty Requirements paragraphs].

NOTE: For aircraft with Ram Air Doors installed and activated, make sure the ground
service mode is OFF before powering up the aircraft. This will prevent a RAM AIR FAULT
advisory message.
Ensure airplane is not moved until IESS is initialized. Initialization occurs within 90 seconds
after power-up.
Verify that only displays 2 and 3 are available. When more than displays 2 and 3 are
available, the airplane must not be dispatched.
Check the battery voltages on the MFD Status page.
Step Flight Deck Crewmember
� One or both battery voltage levels is less than 22.5 volts.
Determine lowest battery voltage.
If... Then...
Battery voltage is
between 21.0 and
22.5 volts...
• Recharge the batteries prior to takeoff, through any
AC source (including engines during taxi) for:
– Battery Temp >= 0°C – 30 min
– Battery Temp >= -5°C and < 0°C – 35 min
– Battery Temp >= -10°C and < -5°C – 40 min
– Battery Temp < -10°C – 50 min
1
Battery voltage is
below 21.0 volts...
• Report irregularity as outlined in Flight Operations
Manual SP 3100 Chapter 06 Airworthiness
(Mechanical Irregularities paragraph)
• DO NOT depart

GENERAL GROUND OPERATIONS
ESTABLISHING ELECTRICAL POWER / AIR CONDITIONING
The APU is used to supply AC power in the absence of external AC power services.
The APU is started approximately 10 minutes prior to pushback, unless required for passenger
comfort.
GROUND POWER
Verify AVAIL light is illuminated before pushing in the GPU Button to select ground power. When
ground power is not available, or is not being used, ensure the GPU Button is pushed out.
NOTE: When the source of AC power is lost, power down the airplane utilizing the Leaving the
Airplane Checklist then perform power up utilizing the Safety and Power Up Checklist.
AUXILIARY POWER UNIT (APU)
The following procedure is accomplished to start the APU whenever necessary throughout the
course of normal operations.
NOTE: BATT 1 must be ON and BATT 2 must be in AUTO for the APU to start.
Step Flight Deck Crewmember
1 EMER STOP Button .................................................................................. Guarded
2 APU SELECTOR KNOB ...................................................ON, START then release
POWER DOWN WITH PASSENGERS ON BOARD
When a power down is required with passengers on board, comply with the following:
• Do not accomplish a power down during the boarding or deplaning process.
• Coordinate with cabin crew and inform them of the need to power down the aircraft.
• Accomplish a passenger announcement explaining the need to power down the aircraft for
approximately (insert expected number of minutes). Explain the aircraft cabin will be dark,
and all window shades should be opened.
• The flight attendant informs the crew when they are ready to proceed with the power down.
• Power down must be accomplished using the shutdown and leaving the airplane checklist.
• Established flows, checklists, and procedures must be used to re-establish power to the
aircraft.
NOTE: Use of emergency lighting shall not be used during this procedure. In case of an
evacuation or emergency, lighting activation must be initiated by the crew.

FLIGHT CONTROL POWER UP BUILT IN TEST (PBIT)
The PBIT consists of two different tests:
• Electrical PBIT (E-PBIT)
• Hydraulic PBIT (H-PBIT)
The E-PBIT is automatically performed when an initial AC source is introduced. Allow the E -
PBIT to finish before turning on any hydraulic pumps. Interruption of the E - PBIT may result in a
FLIGHT CONTROL NO DISPATCH Caution EICAS message.
The test is interrupted when:
• Any hydraulic pump is running
• The Flight Control Mode Panel switches are cycled
• The AC power supply is interrupted
A FLT CTRL TEST IN PROG Status message is displayed to indicate the PBIT is in progress.
The H-PBIT is performed when all three hydraulic systems are pressurized for at least one
minute and the reservoir temperature is greater than +10ºC.
The H-PBIT is interrupted when any flight control surface is moved while the test is running.
A FLT CTRL BIT EXPIRED EICAS message is displayed when the last successful PBIT was
more than 50 hours old while the aircraft is on the ground and the EICAS messages are no
longer inhibited.
When FLT CTRL BIT EXPIRED caution message is displayed, complete an E-PBIT or H-PBIT to
reset.
ELECTRICAL PBIT (E-PBIT)
E-PBIT if interrupted and about to expire or expired:
Step Flight Deck Crewmember
�
1 Power down the aircraft using the Leaving the Airplane Checklist.
2 Wait a minimum of one minute.
3 Perform the power up procedure utilizing the Safety and Power Up Checklist.
4 Wait three minutes for the E-PBIT to run.
5 Perform a H-PBIT when required.

HYDRAULIC PBIT (H-PBIT)
H-PBIT if interrupted and about to expire or expired:
Step Flight Deck Crewmember
�
1 Do not move any flight control surface.
Confirm the STEER OFF EICAS message is illuminated.
If.... Then....
STEER OFF EICAS message
is not illuminated...
• Press the Steering Disengage Switch on the
control yoke
• Proceed to Step 2
2
STEER OFF EICAS message
is illuminated...
Proceed to Step 3.
3 Select HYD Synoptic Display. Confirm all three hydraulic systems are below 250
psi and reservoir temperatures are above +10°C before proceeding.
4 Post Mod Load 27.2: turn the hydraulic system ELECT PUMP knobs within 6
seconds to ON in the following sequence: 3A, 1 and 2
5 Wait 60 seconds.
6 At this point, the FLT CTRL BIT EXPIRED EICAS message extinguishes.
7 Turn the electrical hydraulic pumps 1 & 2 to AUTO and 3A to OFF when they
are no longer needed.
HYDRAULIC SYSTEMS WARM-UP
The Hydraulic Systems Warm-Up must be accomplished before starting the engines when
reservoir temperatures are below -18°C.
Step Flight Deck Crewmember
� Hydraulic Systems Warm-Up required.
1 HYDRAULIC ELEC PUMP SYS 1, 3A ................................................................ ON
2 PTU ..................................................................................................................... ON
3 Wait 30 seconds.
4 PTU .................................................................................................................AUTO
5 HYDRAULIC ELEC PUMP SYS 2 ....................................................................... ON
6 Verify all reservoir temperatures warmer than -18°C.
7 HYDRAULIC ELEC PUMP SYS 1, 2 ...............................................................AUTO
8 HYDRAULIC ELEC PUMP SYS 3A .................................................................. OFF

AIR DATA SMART PROBE (ADSP) HEATER
When a parked aircraft has been exposed to freezing conditions, moisture can freeze inside the
ADSPs and create an undesired indication on the ground, usually during the first power-up
of the day.
When airspeed indication is present on the PFDs, any, or all of following messages from the
AFCS system may be displayed on EICAS:
• AFCS FAULT
• AT FAIL
• AP FAIL
• FD FAIL
• YD FAIL
• APPR 2 NOT AVAIL
• Air Data Systems (ADS) messages may be displayed on the EICAS also
When the smart probes are contaminated with ice or snow, accomplish the following procedure
after AC Power is established on the aircraft and verify the pushbutton illuminates.
ADSP Heater Switch .................................................................................................................ON
Flight Control PBIT. ................................................................................................. ACCOMPLISH
• Verify the clock on the Flight Control Synoptic page resets to 50 hours after the flight
control PBIT is complete.
• When the flight control PBIT does not run or when the clock on the Flight Control
Synoptic page does not reset to 50 hours, leave the aircraft powered for a minimum of 3
minutes with the ADSP Heater Switch on. After a minimum of three minutes and after
any accumulated ice in the Smart Probes has had a chance to melt, power down the
aircraft, wait one minute, and power the aircraft back up using the normal procedures for
establishing electrical power. The flight control PBIT should now run properly.
ADSP Heater Switch ...............................................................................................................OFF
After heating the ADSPs, ensure there is no airspeed indication on both PFDs and all
spurious AFCS and ADS EICAS messages are no longer displayed.
If there was a frozen probe during the initial power-up, the Electrical PBIT may not run.
Therefore, check the PBIT remaining time on the MFD Flight Controls synoptic page and
consider performing a power down/power up procedure when there is a need to reset the
Electrical PBIT countdown clock.
If the spurious AFCS and ADS EICAS messages do not clear after following this procedure,
call Maintenance.

RAM AIR FAULT EICAS ADVISORY MESSAGE
NOTE: For aircraft with Ram Air Doors installed and activated, make sure the ground service
mode is OFF before powering up the aircraft. This will prevent a RAM AIR FAULT advisory
message.
When a RAM AIR FAULT advisory message remains displayed for more than one minute after
power up, it can be cleared as follows
PACK 1 and PACK 2 ....................................................................................................PUSH OUT
Wait 5 seconds
PACK 1 and PACK 2 ........................................................................................................PUSH IN
This fault message is considered a nuisance and no further action is required when the fault
clears. When the fault message does not clear, it indicates an actual RAM AIR failure
requiring a report to maintenance as outlined in Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter
06 Airworthiness (Mechanical Irregularities paragraph).
APU FUEL SOV FAIL CAUTION MESSAGE
APU SWITCH.............................................................................................................................ON
APU SWITCH...........................................................................................................................OFF
NOTE: A two-second delay must be observed between switch selection.
This fault message is considered a nuisance and no further action is required when the fault
clears. When the fault message does not clear, it indicates an actual mechanical failure
requiring a report to maintenance as outlined in Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 06
Airworthiness (Mechanical Irregularities paragraph).
EXTERNAL INSPECTION
The external inspection procedures are usually performed by the first officer prior to and following
each flight; however, the captain maintains responsibility for ensuring the external inspection is
complete.
While conducting the external inspection, pilots must be aware of moving vehicles around the
airplane and surroundings.
Items with *** preceding them must be accomplished when icing conditions exist or are
suspected. Perform a visual inspection as outlined in Ground Deicing Program Manual SP 2800
Chapter 04 Flight Operations Deice/Anti-ice Procedures (Deice/Anti-ice Policy, Cold Weather
Preflight Inspection paragraph), as applicable.

BIRD STRIKE DAMAGE – VISUAL DAMAGE
A visual inspection for bird strike damage shall be performed when, during flight operations, a
bird strike is observed. Following an observed bird strike, a maintenance log write-up is required
and must also be reported to Maintenance.
COLD WEATHER OPERATIONS
Takeoff is prohibited with frost, ice, snow, or slush adhering to any aircraft critical surface.
Reference Ground Deicing Program Manual SP 2800 for all deicing/anti-ice procedures and
limitations.
Conditions conducive to icing exist on the ground and for takeoff when the OAT is 10°C or below,
when operating on ramps, taxiways, or runways where surface snow, ice, standing water, or
slush may be ingested by the engines or freeze on engines, nacelles, or engine sensor probes.
A Cold Weather Preflight inspection must be performed by a flight deck crewmember during the
external inspection to determine the wings are free from frozen contaminants when:
• The OAT is 5°C or less, or
• The wing fuel temperature is 0°C or less, or
• Atmospheric conditions conducive to icing exist, or
• The aircraft has remained overnight and may have frozen contaminants due to exposure to
frost or precipitation, which could involve the entire airframe, or
• On any through flight where residual ice from the inbound flight may have accumulated on
airframe surfaces or components, or
• Cold soak ice is suspected in the fuel tank area.
NOTE: Anytime the temperature is 5°C or colder, the preflight inspection must take place
within 30 minutes of pushback.
The leading edge and upper wing and tail surfaces must be free of ice, and snow. A thin layer of
hoarfrost is permitted on the fuselage provided the layer is thin enough to distinguish surface
features underneath, such as painting and markings.
NOTE: A 3 mm (1/8 in) frost layer is permitted on the underwing surfaces.
Clear ice is most likely to form when the aircraft is operated in an environment of high humidity
and the upper surfaces of the wing are in contact with the cold fuel in the wing tanks. The
formation of clear ice on the wing upper surfaces is very difficult to detect visually, especially
when the wing is wet. The aircraft wing surfaces must be inspected for cold soak ice or frost
when the following conditions exist:
• Frost is detected below the wing tank area
• The aircraft was exposed to low temperatures during the inbound leg and the aircraft returns
to flight with little delay on the ground, when the surface temperature is near freezing with high
humidity (temperature/dew point spread of less than 3°C)

Page: 04-01.26 of 42
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
Pilots must be aware of the effect cold fuel in the tanks may have on moisture present on the
wing upper and lower surfaces. When fuel temperature is 0°C (32°F), or below, it is possible to
have clear ice on the wing with the temperature above freezing.
Wing frost caused by cold soaked fuel can form on the upper and lower surfaces of the wing
even at temperatures significantly above freezing. Frost or ice on the upper surface of the
wing must be removed.
NOTE: For a list of all critical surfaces and deicing/anti-icing procedures, refer to Ground
Deicing Program Manual SP 2800.
POTABLE WATER SYSTEM
Stations are required to accomplish one of the following actions when the temperature during a
RON was at or below freezing (0°C / 32°F):
• Drain and purge the potable water system; or
• Apply heated air for the duration of the RON (APU or external); or
• Hangar the aircraft in a heated environment
NOTE: Obtain the official overnight low temperature from the OCC when necessary.
The ERJ 170/175 Potable Water Draining/Purging Verification Form will confirm that the potable
water tank was drained, and the lines were purged. The form is placed in the flight deck by
station personnel for FFOD flights.
When the form is missing, have the station check their log to verify one of the above actions
was accomplished. When verified, the form is not required. Without verification, the aircraft
is considered not airworthy. Report the mechanical irregularity to Maintenance Control (e.g.,
“Aircraft potable water cannot be verified drained/purged in freezing conditions.”)
The form is not required when the temperature remained above freezing during the RON.
Inquiry
For all FFOD flights, use the ACARS Inquiry to document:
• Y (YES) – Airplane was drained/purged, heated, or hangared.
• N (NO) – Airplane was not drained/purged, heated, or hangared. Maintenance required.
• N/A (NOT APPLICABLE) – RON above freezing.
Extreme Cold (at or below -7°C (19°F)
Extreme cold may flash freeze the potable water servicing port causing the system to malfunction.
To avoid maintenance delays, stations are advised not to service the potable water system in
extreme cold. The station should advise the crew.
The aircraft may be dispatched without water since the lavatory(s) still functions.

ORIGINATING EXPANDED CHECKLIST
GENERAL
The Originating Checklist is initiated by the Captain and read by the First Officer. This is
completed early enough to ensure Maintenance has sufficient time to correct any irregularities.
The checklist is performed under any of the following circumstances:
• First flight of the day (either aircraft or crew)
• Change in crew
• Aircraft swap
• Aircraft has been left unattended. The captain and first officer accomplish the flow only
(switches only: tests are not required, e.g., O2 mask and trim check).
• Maintenance has been performed on the aircraft
(Flow - Challenge and Response - CP/FO)
AIRWORTHINESS ACCEPTANCE ............................................................... COMPLETE CP/FO
The captain and first officer verifies the airworthiness of the aircraft as outlined and in
accordance with Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 06 Airworthiness.
GEAR / RAT PINS ............................................................................................REMOVED CP/FO
Verifies the landing gear and RAT safety pins are removed and stowed on the flight deck
(done during the Interior/Exterior Preflight Inspection).
PANEL PREP ........................................................................................ COMPLETE L / R CP/FO
Verifies all aircraft systems are operational and the captain/first officer flow items are
completed.

AIRWORTHINESS ACCEPTANCE ........................................................................... COMPLETE
Ensure the aircraft is airworthy as outlined and in accordance with Flight Operations Manual
SP 3100 Chapter 06 Airworthiness.
The steps outlined in the FOM may be performed any time prior to conducting the Originating
Check. However, it is recommended they are performed early enough in the preflight duties
to ensure an on-time departure (see Preflight section).
By completing this, both the PIC and SIC verify overall airworthiness and accept the aircraft
as airworthy.
When the airworthiness changes (e.g., maintenance performed, mechanical irregularity),
after this check has been completed, repeat/review the actions outlined in Flight Operations
Manual SP 3100 Chapter 06 Airworthiness, to ensure the aircraft is airworthy and properly
returned to service.

CB Function Key .................................................................................................. Select

available.

COCKPIT DOOR CONTROL .......................................................................................... Checked
• LOCK .................................................................................................................... Guarded
This check is accomplished on the first flight of the day for the aircraft.
– Close the flight deck door
– Press and hold the TEST button
– Check the alarm and UNLOCKED indication ON
– Push in the LOCK button and check the electromechanical latch normal operation
– Press the EMERG ENTRY pushbutton on the door control panel in the passenger
cabin. Check the UNLOCKED indication is flashing and check normal operation of
the alarm sequence according to system logic.
– Push out the LOCK button
Alarm Sequence
Actuating the EMERG ENTRY pushbutton starts the chime alarm sequence. The chime remains
on for four seconds and is repeated three times at nine-second intervals during which the
sound chime remains paused. After the third chime cycle ends, the door unlocks if the INHIB
pushbutton is not pressed within 30 seconds after EMERG ENTRY pushbutton actuation.

FIRST OFFICER’S ORIGINATING CHECKLIST EXPANDED FLOW
AIRWORTHINESS ACCEPTANCE ........................................................................... COMPLETE
Ensure the aircraft is airworthy as outlined and in accordance with Flight Operations Manual
SP 3100 Chapter 06 Airworthiness.
The steps outlined in the FOM may be performed any time prior to conducting the Originating
Check. However, it is recommended they are performed early enough in the preflight duties
to ensure an on-time departure (see Preflight section).
By completing this, both the PIC and SIC verify overall airworthiness and accept the aircraft
as airworthy.
When the airworthiness changes (e.g., maintenance performed, mechanical irregularity),
after this check has been completed, repeat/review the actions outlined in Flight Operations
Manual SP 3100 Chapter 06 Airworthiness

BEFORE START EXPANDED CHECKLIST
GENERAL
The Before Start Checklist is used to prepare for starting the engines.
Accomplish the Before Start Checklist when:
• Fueling is complete
• ATC clearance is received (unless ATC clearance is not available until taxi out)
• Tasks and flows are complete
Approximately 10 minutes prior to departure, start the APU and give the command/signal
when ready to disconnect the air and/or ground power. Accomplish Jumpseat Briefing, when
appropriate. Accomplish a welcome aboard briefing, time permitting.

BEFORE START EXPANDED CHECKLIST
(Flow Pattern - Challenge and Response - CP/FO)
PREFLIGHT CHECKS .......................................................................... COMPLETE L / R CP/FO
Both pilots ensure preflight duties are complete, including the airworthiness checks outlined
in Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 06 Airworthiness.
FUEL QTY...................................................................... (___), (___) / (___) CONFIRMED CP/FO
The captain compares the release fuel to the fuel onboard and states:
“RELEASE FUEL (___), (___) onboard.”
The first officer responds with fuel onboard: “(___) CONFIRMED.”
MCDU ................................................................................................................. SET L / R CP/FO
Prior to conducting the Departure WAANT briefing, one pilot reads the ATC clearance in
its entirety.
The other pilot simultaneously verifies the route on the ROUTE page, the ADS-B call sign,
the clearance altitude limit, and squawk code.
The PM reads the fixes, speeds, and altitudes from the departure, enroute, and arrival
charts through the top of descent (TOD).
Simultaneously, PF verifies on the FLIGHT PLAN page through the TOD:
•
• Waypoint names and sequence,
• Speed restrictions,
• Crossing altitudes,
• Appropriate discontinuities are present.
Both pilots visually cross-check the flight plan by sequencing through each waypoint on
the MFD PLAN page.
This should be accomplished prior to reading the Before Start Checklist.
CREW O2 .................................................................................................CHECKED L / R CP/FO
Mask flow and EICAS quantity are checked.

ENGINE START EXPANDED CHECKLIST
GENERAL
Engine start may be performed simultaneously with the pushback procedure, requiring the
parking brake to be released and steering disengaged.
NOTE: In case of a static engine start, ensure the parking brake is set.
Refer to the QRH for the following engine start procedures:
• Engine Ground Pneumatic Start
• Engine Battery Start
Accomplish the Engine Start Checklist when:
• Manifest is complete and Takeoff Data has been computed.
• Engine Start tasks and flows are complete.
• FA reports “ready to taxi.”
• All doors are closed.

PUSHBACK
The Before Start Checklist must be completed prior to the pushback.
NOTE: Power back is not allowed.
NOTE: When the pushback speed is higher than 5 kt and either hydraulic system 1 or 2 is
depressurized, the autobrake automatically disarms and EICAS messages BRK LH (RH)
FAULT or BRK LH (RH) FAIL display, until the wheel speed is reduced to less than 5 kt,
or the hydraulic systems are pressurized.
NOTE: When deicing/anti-icing is required, leave the flaps up and accomplish the AFTER
START CHECKLISTS once deice/anti-ice is complete.
PRIOR TO PUSHBACK
Prior to pushback and engine start, the FA reports “READY TO TAXI,” gives the thumbs-up
signal, and closes the flight deck door.
PUSHBACK PROCEDURES
Prior to aircraft movement:
• Obtain necessary clearance
• Establish and maintain audio or visual communication with the pushback crew. Refer to the
Jeppesen Airway Manual Air Traffic Control Section Paragraph 5 ICAO Marshaling Signals
and Station Operations Manual SP 5500 Chapter 04 Hand Signals.
CAUTION: Do not apply brakes while the aircraft is being pushed back
CAUTION: Ensure nosewheel steering is OFF prior to pushback.
• Complete the Engine Start Checklist
• Verify with pushback operator the area is clear and the nose gear towing light is indicating
green
Check:
• STEER OFF (EICAS)
• Emergency parking brake OFF

Communicate:
• Steering OFF
• Brakes released
• Ready for pushback
NOTE: When using hand signals, the "Release Brakes" hand signal indicates the
nosewheel steering has been confirmed OFF and the brakes are released.
Lektro
The pushback procedure using the Lektro is similar to a standard pushback, except:
• The brakes must be released, and the aircraft positioned into the Lektro bucket by the
operator prior to pushback
• The Lektro operator requests the brakes be set after pushback, prior to removing the aircraft
from the bucket
→
ENGINE START PROCEDURE
Engine 1 is normally started first and utilized for single engine taxi. When the crew elects to taxi
single engine on engine 2, electric hydraulic pump 1 selector knob must be turned ON. Once
engine 1 is running, turn electric hydraulic pump 1 to AUTO.
The APU is shut down after engine start for single engine taxi, unless:
• Performance requirements dictate otherwise
• Anticipated ground delays and engine shutdown are expected
NOTE: When the APU is shutdown during single engine taxi, the ENGINE CROSSBLEED
START procedure is used.
NOTE: During ground starts only:
• The transition of the flight deck START / STOP switch from STOP to START must
be less than 30 seconds or the FADEC prevents an engine start until the switch is
cycled through STOP.

ENGINE START - ACTIONS and CALLOUTS
CP FO
“STARTING ENGINE 1 (2)”
• Turn the START / STOP selector to START
momentarily (2 sec) and back to RUN, keeping
the hand on the START / STOP selector to
abort the engine start, when necessary
• Start Clock
• Monitor IGN A (B) at 7% N2, fuel flow at
approximately 20% N2
• Ensure N2 and N1 accelerate normally, and
positive oil pressure is indicated
• Verify the annunciation IGN A (B) goes out at
approximately 50% N2
ENGINE START
SEQUENCE
“START ENGINE 1 (2)”
• Verify the engine stabilizes at idle and at the
correct N1 target (1)
“NORMAL START”
NOTE - Callouts are shown in bold text.
(1) After second engine start, verify parameters are normal, and the EICAS N1 target value is
within +0.5% to -0.1% of the planned N1 value. For example, if the planned N1 value is
90.0, then the N1 target on the EICAS must be 89.9 to 90.5.
An EICAS N1 value outside the required range may be an indication of a disagreement between
OAT used to calculate the planned N1 and that sensed by FADEC. It may be corrected by
reducing the flex assumed temperature or updating the takeoff performance numbers with the
current OAT. When attempts to match the EICAS N1 in this manner are unsuccessful, it may
indicate a mechanical problem. The takeoff should not be attempted.
An N1 disagreement may also indicate improper set up (i.e., TO-x, ECS, A/I).
ENGINE WARM UP
In order to allow thermal stabilization of the engines, operate the engines at or near IDLE for
at least two minutes, before selecting higher thrust settings. Taxi time at or near IDLE may
be included in this warm-up period.
NO BREAK POWER TRANSFER (NBPT)
To avoid power interruptions, it is recommended to wait 30 seconds after the N2 stabilizes before
shutting down the APU or disconnecting a GPU; otherwise, follow the power transfer using the
Electrical Synoptic Page.

ENGINE START
Page: 04-03.11 of 14

TAILWIND
When starting engines with a tailwind crews should expect higher ITTs.
When it is necessary to perform an engine start with a tailwind, monitor N1. When there is no
positive increase of N1, before starter cutout (50% N2), the start must be manually aborted.
ENGINE CROSSBLEED START
Only the first officer performs an engine start while the aircraft is moving. The parking brake must
be set for the captain to conduct an engine start.
A crossbleed start may be conducted by following the flow procedure below, without reference to
a checklist procedure. It is accomplished anytime it is convenient, considering crew workload
and proximity to the takeoff runway.
A crossbleed start does not require ATC approval when performed on an active taxiway.
However, the crew must use caution due to jet blast.
Do not initiate the second engine start or perform a crossbleed start while crossing a runway.
On congested ramp areas or taxiways, the aircraft must be stopped, parking brake ON, and
start approval obtained from ATC.
The second engine start is completed in time to allow a two-minute engine warm-up before
applying takeoff thrust.
CROSSBLEED START FLOW PROCEDURE (FO)
REAR OF AIRCRAFT ..................................................................................................... CLEAR
THRUST LEVER (OPERATING ENGINE) ................................................................. ADVANCE
DUCT PRESSURE ..................................................................................................40 to 45 PSI
• Verify on MFD STATUS synoptic page prior to engine start
START / STOP SELECTOR ...........................................................................START, then RUN
• Start the engine using normal start procedures
After the engine is started and stabilized:
THRUST LEVERS .............................................................................................................. IDLE
N1 TARGET .................................................................................................................... VERIFY
• Callout “NORMAL START”

ENGINE START
Page: 04-03.12 of 14
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
COLD WEATHER OPERATIONS
Before starting engines, assess the surface conditions of the ramp. Special considerations are
given to contamination that could be encountered during the engine start and pushback.
Do not start the engine until the freedom of N1 fan rotation is verified and all ice deposits are
removed from the air inlet. Water may freeze in the area of the lower intake, jamming the
adjacent fan blades. Under no circumstance is it permissible to use deicing/anti-icing fluid for the
removal of the contaminant.
Perform a normal engine start during cold weather/cold soak conditions. When the engine does
not start, maintenance procedures may be required or ground heating may be necessary to warm
the nacelle, Air Turbine Starter (ATS), and Starting Control Valve (SCV).
OIL
During start with low oil temperatures, the oil pressure may drop below minimum oil pressure
levels temporarily after start. When the oil pressure remains below minimum levels for more than
two minutes, the engine must be shut down and the oil heated. In this case, the oil temperature
is raised to at least -20°C to improve the chances of a successful engine start. During the
subsequent start, after heating the oil, when the oil pressure does not recover, the engine must
be shut down.
During cold weather starts, initial oil pressure response may be slow. As the engine accelerates,
the oil pressure may rapidly increase then exceed the normal range. The high oil pressure
progressively reduces to normal as the engine achieves normal operating temperatures. Refer to
the limitations section for oil pressure limitations.
NOTE: When oil pressure is not indicated by the time idle rpm is achieved, conduct an
immediate shutdown of the engine.
IDG
Expect the generators to be slow in producing steady power due to cold oil in the IDGs. Five
minutes may be required for the IDGs to stabilize sufficiently.
CAUTION:
During cold weather operations, oil pressure peaks to full scale may occur due to
high oil viscosity. Oil pressure decreases as the oil temperature increases. When
the oil pressure remains above normal operating range, the engine is shutdown.

PUSHBACK PROCEDURES - IRREGULAR OPERATIONS
Lektro - APU Inoperative
The aircraft must be positioned into the Lektro bucket prior to engine start. Prior to pushback,
the captain communicates:
• Brakes ON
• Engine start complete
CAUTION: When the ramp is contaminated, traction may be compromised.
Engine start is delayed until the pushback procedure is complete. When an
engine start is required prior to pushback:
– Only one engine is started.
– During pushback, the operating engine must remain at idle.
– Repositioning of the aircraft to a location with adequate traction may be
required prior to crossbleed start.
– The PIC does not permit engine start or pushback without operable headsets.
APU INOPERATIVE ENGINE START
WARNING: DO NOT START BOTH ENGINES AT THE GATE.
GROUND CREW MUST DISCONNECT ALL LOW PRESSURE HOSES FROM THE
AIRCRAFT PRIOR TO START.
JETWAY OPERATIONS
NOTE: Single engine start on engine #2 requires HYD ELEC PUMP SYS 1 to be ON. The
HYD PUMP is turned back to AUTO, when applicable.
The #2 engine may be started at the gate with the jetway connected. The #1 engine is started
once clear of all personnel and equipment. Use of an external ground power cart, when available,
is recommended to allow the jetway to be removed prior to engine start.
When jetway ground power must be used for engine start, the captain will conduct a crew safety
briefing and flight attendants will not arm doors until the jetway is clear. Leave the flight deck
door open while starting the number #2 engine to enable communication with flight attendants.

ENGINE START - EXTERNAL AIR
For external airstarts:
• Complete the Engine Start Checklist. If the jetway is attached, report DOORS / WINDOWS
“to go.”
• Start Engine #2 first using the QRH Engine Ground Pneumatic Start procedure.
• After engine start, direct the ground crew to disconnect external air and ground power.
• Once the jetway is removed and clear, flight attendants will arm doors and report “Ready
to taxi.”
• When DOORS / WINDOWS are “to go,” repeat the Engine Start Checklist to ensure DOORS /
WINDOWS ... CLOSED & LOCKED and all items complete.
• Once ground power and external air are removed and clear, complete pushback with # 2
engine, not above idle thrust.
• When clear of all personnel, equipment, and when appropriate, crossbleed start Engine #1.
CAUTION: To avoid cabin air contamination, do not operate air conditioning packs
while using the ground pneumatic start cart.
ENGINE START - BATTERY
When a battery engine start is required, refer to QRH Engine Battery Start.
ABNORMAL ENGINE START INDICATIONS
Although FADEC provides automatic over-temperature protection and automatically aborts the
start in the event of a hot start, hung start, or no light off, the engine start must be manually
aborted when:
• No positive oil pressure indication within 10 seconds after N2 speed starts to increase.
• During engine start with a tailwind, when a positive increase of N1 is not indicated before
starter cutout (50% N2). In this case, the airplane is repositioned prior to engine start to
minimize tailwind effects.
• No ITT indication within 30 seconds after initiation of fuel flow (no light off).
• ITT exceeds start limit (815°C - Hot).
• Oil pressure stabilizes below the engine limits.
• N1 and/or N2 failing to accelerate to stable idle speed (hung start).
• An intermittent electrical pneumatic or starter malfunction occurs before the starter
disengagement.

AFTER START EXPANDED CHECKLIST
FLAPS.......................................................................... (__), (__) SET / (__) CONFIRMED FO/CP
NOTE: When deicing/anti-icing is required, leave the flaps up and accomplish the AFTER
START CHECKLISTS once deice/anti-ice is complete.
• The first officer reads the flap setting from the planned takeoff data and verifies the correct
flap setting is displayed on EICAS (e.g., “SOURCE DATA 2, 2 SET”).
• The captain verifies the correct flap position is displayed on EICAS and matches the flap
setting on the FMS TAKEOFF page (e.g., “2 CONFIRMED”).
FLIGHT CONTROLS ...............................................................................CHECKED L / R CP/FO
• Both pilots verify the flight controls are checked.
ELECTRONIC DEVICES ............................................................ AIRPLANE MODE L / R CP/FO
Verify:
• All electronic devices are in Airplane Mode.
NOTE: All EFBs must remain connected to receive operational control changes until the
After Start Checklist, as designated by the checklist to select Airplane Mode. This is
accomplished by selecting Airplane Mode in Settings. Airplane Mode is mandatory for all
flight operations, except as outlined in the Electronic Flight Bag (EFB) Program Manual
SP 3105, Use During Aircraft Operations (Block Out to Block In). Crewmembers may
reference the iPad Training, Connectivity section for further information.
While the towbar and tug are being disconnected, the marshaller should:
• Be visible to the flight deck crew and give the stop signal (wands crossed).
• When the ground equipment and personnel are clear of the aircraft, the marshaller should
display the hold position/standby signal (arms down at a 45-degree angle).
When ready to taxi, the PIC gives the “Ready to Taxi” signal (Station Operations Manual SP 5500)
to the marshaller (at night, the PIC flashes the nose landing light to indicate they are ready to
taxi). If it is safe to taxi, the marshaller then gives the salute/end marshalling signal and departs.
CAUTION: Do not taxi until the salute/end marshalling signal is observed by the PIC.
NOTE: Power Out Procedures: The flight deck crew may flash taxi lights to indicate they
are ready to taxi.
