CAPTAIN AND FIRST OFFICER AFTER START EXPANDED FLOW
After the engine(s) is started, the aircraft is completely stopped, and ground crew and equipment
are clear, the captain initiates the flow by stating:
“FLAPS ___, APU ___, FLIGHT CONTROL CHECK”
NOTE: When deicing/anti-icing is required, leave the flaps up and accomplish the AFTER
START CHECKLIST once deice/anti-ice is complete.
FLAPS
The first officer references the takeoff data and moves the flap lever to the required position.
This can be accomplished during pushback.
NOTE: When the EICAS SLAT / FLAPS indication differs from flap setting on the FMS
TAKEOFF INIT page, the aural message “NO TAKEOFF, FLAPS” sounds during the takeoff
configuration check.
APU
The first officer selects the APU to match the ECS takeoff data. For:
• ECS ON, the APU should be OFF.
• ECS OFF, the APU should be ON.
HYDRAULIC SYS 1 ELEC PUMP
The first officer selects the HYDRAULIC SYS 1 ELEC PUMP to AUTO.
NOTE: Single engine taxi on Engine #2 requires HYDRAULIC SYS 1 ELEC PUMP to be ON.
Turn the switch back to AUTO after Engine #1 is started.
ADS PROBES HEATER
The first officer pushes out the ADS PROBES HEATER button, when applicable.

CAPTAIN AND FIRST OFFICER AFTER START EXPANDED FLOW (continued)
FLIGHT CONTROLS
The captain displays the FLIGHT CTRL synoptic page on the MFD. The captain verifies STEER
OFF status message is displayed on EICAS. Press the STEER DISC button, if required.
The captain checks the flight controls; elevator, aileron, and rudder, are checked for free travel to
the full deflection stops. The Captain and first officer verify the dashed box outlines are displayed
on the FLIGHT CTRL synoptic page during the check. A solid green box indication is not a
requirement for a successful check.
The captain presses and holds the steering handle down until the STEER OFF status EICAS
message extinguishes.
NOTE: When only one engine is operating, check one surface at a time (i.e., aileron, elevator,
rudder) to avoid causing a spurious FLT CTRL NO DISPATCH EICAS message.
ELECTRONIC DEVICES
The captain and first officer ensure all electronic devices are in AIRPLANE MODE.
The captain calls “AFTER START CHECKLIST.”

TAXI
GENERAL
Before taxi out, the flight deck crew completes the After Start Checklist. This also applies
to single engine taxi.
PRIOR TO TAXI:
• Clear left and right
• Both flight deck crewmembers have the Airport Diagram chart available for reference
• Complex taxi instructions are written down
• Challenge and response are used by the flight deck crew when confirming taxi instructions
• The Before Takeoff Checklist is completed prior to initiating a back-taxi on the runway to
be used for takeoff
DURING TAXI:
Flight crews minimize “heads-down” activities, such as entering and verifying data in the FMS
while the airplane is moving.
The captain’s primary responsibility and attention is focused on taxiing the aircraft. The first
officer’s primary responsibility is monitoring and providing feedback of the aircraft’s progress
along the cleared taxi route and setting FMS and take off performance, when needed, as
directed by the captain.
Follow ATC taxi instructions and verify differences from the initial briefing when applicable.
Briefing the taxi route reduces the workload during the departure and arrival WAANT briefings.
ATC is contacted anytime there is a concern about a potential conflict.
Avoid taxiing closely behind other aircraft where FOD may be blown.
LOAD SHED EICAS message may be displayed on ground when an airplane is powered by only
one AC power source and indicated airspeed is at or above 50 kt.
MAXIMUM TAXI SPEEDS
Straight / DRY 30 kt
Straight / WET or CONTAMINATED 10 kt
Turns / DRY 10 kt
Turns / WET or CONTAMINATED 5 kt
CAUTION: At idle thrust, the airplane may accelerate to a higher taxi speed than
desired.

TAXI
Page: 04-05.3 of 5
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
BRAKES
General rules:
• During taxi, minimize the duration of each brake application, when practical, by using shorter
applications with higher pressure on the pedals
• Avoid using brakes asymmetrically
THRUST USE DURING TAXI
The initial taxi thrust setting should be no greater than 40% N1. Once stabilized, if more thrust is
required when operating in close proximity to equipment, terminals, and personnel, ensure a
slow increase in thrust to the minimum amount required. Accept that taxi speed may initially be
slow to mitigate jet blast damage.
CAUTION: The avoidance of jet blast damage must always be considered during
single or two engine taxi.
SINGLE ENGINE TAXI
Normally, engine #1 is used for single engine taxi. Single engine taxi is conducted for all taxi
operations, except the following conditions:
• Ramp/Taxiway surface contamination
• Maneuvering room in the ramp/gate area is marginal
• Taxi time to/from a runway is expected to be < 5 minutes
• Airport-specific regulations prohibit single-engine taxi
• Risk of injury to personnel or damage to equipment
• Captain’s discretion
Additionally, when making the decision to conduct a single engine taxi, consider:
• Airplane weight,
• Uphill slopes,
• Engine warm up,
• Taxi time to the active runway, and
• Equipment, terminal, and personnel behind the aircraft from the start of initial taxi throughout
the turn.
NOTE: Single engine taxi on engine #2 requires HYD ELEC PUMP SYS 1 to be ON. The HYD
PUMP is turned back to AUTO, when applicable.
NOTE: When performing a Single Engine Taxi Out in icing conditions, the Engine Run-Up
procedure must be accomplished before takeoff.

STEER FAIL
The steering system may be reset when a STEER FAIL message is displayed on EICAS.
• Press and hold Steer Disc Switch,
• Press and release Nosewheel Steering Handle, and
• Release Steer Disc Switch
This reset procedure may only be used under the following conditions:
• On the ground with the parking brake set
• Once per flight leg
This fault message is considered a nuisance message and no further action is required when
the fault clears.
When the reset does not clear the fault message, a report to Maintenance is required in
accordance with the maintenance reporting procedures outlined in Flight Operations Manual
SP 3100 Chapter 06 Airworthiness.
LIGHTS - EXTERNAL
TAXI LIGHTS - AS REQUIRED
The first officer normally operates taxi lights.
• This does not preclude the captain from operating lights, as necessary.
NOTE: Operate lights in accordance with the Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 05
Flight Operations (Taxi Operations).
COLD WEATHER OPERATIONS
ENGINE RUN-UP
During ground operations of more than 30 minutes in icing conditions, or when increased fan
vibration due to fan ice accumulation is present, the engine thrust may be increased at 30-minute
intervals to approximately 54% N1 and held at that thrust level for 30 seconds, or until the fan
vibration level returns to normal. When airport surface conditions and the concentration of other
aircraft do not permit the engine thrust to be increased to 54%, set an acceptable thrust level
and time interval that is safe and practical.

ENGINE FIRE ON THE GROUND
When a fire condition is detected, the associated EICAS messages and alarms are generated.
Even though the ENGINE 1 (2) FIRE procedure is designed to guide the pilots to solve this
emergency situation in all conditions, there are some differences when it happens on the ground.
ENGINE FIRE ON THE GROUND PROCEDURE
Whenever possible, stop the airplane so the fire is downwind of the fuselage. After the aircraft
has come to a complete stop, apply the parking brake, alert the FA’s, declare emergency with
ATC or local authority, and perform the appropriate QRC. As determined by the captain, perform
an evacuation when situation requires.
ENGINE FIRE ON THE GROUND
ACTIONS AND CALLOUTS
CP FO
• The pilot first noticing the malfunction identifies and announces:
With the airplane “ENGINE ___ FIRE”
static, taxiing in,
taxing out, or after
RTO
“ENGINE FIRE QRC”
• Stop the aircraft when necessary
and appropriately
• Declare emergency with ATC or
local authority
• Obtain QRC from the holder
• Assist with tasks determined by
the captain
With the airplane
static or when
the airplane has
stopped
• Set Parking Brake On
• Alert FAs (PA)
“REMAIN SEATED AND LISTEN
TO YOUR FLIGHT ATTENDANT”
• Communicate with ATC and
emergency personnel.
• Read and accomplish the QRC
tasks
• Report “ENGINE FIRE QRC
COMPLETE”
NOTE - Callouts are shown in bold text.

GENERAL
The Runway and Performance Change Checklist is accomplished prior to the Before Takeoff
Checklist.
When tasks are complete, the captain calls for the checklist. The captain and first officer
complete the checklist challenge and response, and the first officer calls the checklist complete.
When there are no changes to runway, routing, or performance after accomplishing the BEFORE
START CHECKLIST, the first officer states:
"RUNWAY PERFORMANCE CHANGE CHECKLIST COMPLETE, NO CHANGE."
When operationally prudent, consider stopping the aircraft to make changes.
NOTE: When there is a runway, routing, or performance change, accomplish the RUNWAY
/ PERFORMANCE CHANGE CHECKLIST. Then, accomplish the BEFORE TAKEOFF
CHECKLIST.

BEFORE TAKEOFF CHECKLIST
The captain calls for the Before Takeoff Checklist after both engines are running and ATC
assigns the departure runway. The captain must allow the first officer sufficient time to complete
the flow and checklist prior to entering the runway.
NOTE: When slat/flaps are left up during taxi (e.g., deice/anti-ice), the first officer will select
the takeoff flap position prior to beginning the flow.
The captain initiates the checklist by stating, “TWO ENGINES, BEFORE TAKEOFF
CHECKLIST.”
The first officer completes the flow and reads the planned takeoff data for the assigned runway.
The captain confirms performance is set correctly by responding with the indications on the
MCDU, EICAS, and PFD.
CAUTION: It is critical for the first officer to read the planned takeoff data for the
assigned runway.
CAUTION: If the performance, runway, or routing changes after completing the
Before Takeoff Checklist make the required updates and accomplish both the
Runway Performance Change Checklist and Before Takeoff Checklist again.
CAUTION: Anytime engine(s) have been shut down after the BEFORE TAKEOFF
CHECKLIST has been completed, (e.g., to conserve fuel for a delay), ensure both
engines are running and the BEFORE TAKEOFF CHECKLIST is accomplished again
before entering the runway.
When operationally prudent, consider stopping the aircraft to make changes.
(Flow Pattern - Challenge and Response - CP/FO)
CABIN ......................................................................................................................SECURED FO
TAKEOFF DATA .......................................RWY (__), LNAV or HEADING (__), (__) FO / SET CP
THRUST................................................................. (__) FO / TWO ENGINES (__) CHECKED CP
EICAS ......................................................................................................................CHECKED CP
BRAKE TEMP .........................................................................................................CHECKED FO
TAKEOFF CONFIG .................................................................................................CHECKED CP

Page: 04-07.4 of 8

BEFORE TAKEOFF EXPANDED CHECKLIST (continued)
THRUST.................................................................(__) FO / TWO ENGINES (__) CHECKED CP
The first officer reads the N1 thrust setting from the planned takeoff data:
e.g., “90.0”
WARNING: THE USE OF FLEX THRUST TAKEOFF IS PROHIBITED FOR THE
FOLLOWING:
• TAKEOFF ON CONTAMINATED RUNWAY.
• REPORTED OR FORECAST WINDSHEAR.
• WHEN A SPECIAL DEPARTURE PROCEDURE SPECIFIES FULL THRUST
FOR TAKEOFF.
The captain verifies two engines are running, parameters are normal, and the EICAS N1
target value is within +0.5% to -0.1% of the planned N1 value. For example, when the
planned N1 value is 90.0, the N1 target on the EICAS must be 89.9 to 90.5. The captain
states:
e.g., “TWO ENGINES, 90.1 CHECKED.”
An EICAS N1 value outside the required range may be an indication of a disagreement
between OAT used to calculate the planned N1 and that sensed by FADEC. It may be
corrected by reducing the flex assumed temperature or updating the takeoff performance
numbers with the current OAT. When attempts to match the EICAS N1 in this manner are
unsuccessful, it may indicate a mechanical problem. The takeoff should not be attempted.
An N1 disagreement may also indicate improper setup (i.e., TO-x, ECS, A/I).
EICAS ..................................................................................................................... CHECKED CP
Check:
• Only applicable EICAS messages are displayed.
• ATTCS is armed.
• At least MINTO fuel.
• Fuel balance is within the limit.
BRAKE TEMP ........................................................................................................ CHECKED FO
Verify all four brake temperatures indicate green. Takeoff is prohibited with brake
temperature indications not green.
Set the MFD to MAP.

ENTERING RUNWAY
RUNWAY VERIFICATION
BEFORE CROSSING HOLD SHORT LINE
The first officer verifies the correct runway by any means available (e.g., 10 - 9 chart, signs,
markings). The first officer’s readback to ATC or call over CTAF is the confirmation of the correct
runway. The captain confirms the correct runway by any means available (e.g., 10 - 9 chart,
signs, markings) and states: e.g., "RUNWAY 26R CONFIRMED."
NOTE: When the captain responds to ATC instructions or makes the CTAF call when the first
officer is off frequency, the FO responds: e.g., "RUNWAY 26R CONFIRMED."
NOTE: Operate lights in accordance with the Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 05
Flight Operations (Taxi Operations).
LIGHTS
When cleared into position or taxiing onto any runway, the first officer turns ON all exterior lights.
NOTE: Strobes may be delayed until cleared for takeoff when they create a hazard to other
aircraft. Logo lights may be required during daylight conditions with low visibility.
IN POSITION
When aligned with the runway centerline, use all available information such as heading and FMS
course indication (PFD), lateral profile (MFD), and departure runway (MCDU) to ensure the
airplane is on the assigned runway. The captain and first officer state:
CP: "HEADING CHECKED"
FO: "CHECKED"
AUTOTHROTTLES
After the runway heading verification, the captain arms the autothrottles and both pilots silently
verify ROLL & TO and autothrottles (TO white) are displayed in the FMA.
After arming autothrottles, visually confirm the correct selection was made on the FMA.
• A white TO is displayed.
– Confirm a dual cue flight director is found on the PFD.
• When any of these indicators are missing the ALT button may have been inadvertently
selected. Deselect the ALT button, press TOGA, and re-select the A/T button.
– Visually confirm the FMA and PFD are displaying the correct information.

SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
TAKEOFF
Page: 04-08.2 of 20
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
GENERAL
Takeoff performance is predicated upon one of the following methods:
NORMAL TAKEOFF (NRM)
UP TO 10 KT TAILWIND
• Line up on the runway and advance thrust to 40% N1, with or without applying the brakes.
• When the engines stabilize at 40% N1, advance thrust levers to TO/GA detent.
11-15 KT TAILWIND
• Line up on the runway and apply brakes.
• With the AT disarmed, advance thrust to 60% N1
• When engines stabilize at 60% N1:
– Release the brakes, arm the AT, and slowly advance both thrust levers to TO/GA
detent before 60 kt; or
– Make sure the AT has advanced both thrust levers to TO/GA detent before 60 kt.
STATIC TAKEOFF
Line up on the runway, apply brakes, and advance thrust to 40% N1. When the engines
stabilize at 40% N1, advance thrust levers to TO/GA detent. Release the brakes when
the takeoff thrust (N1 target) is achieved.
NOTE: Due to the possibility of compressor stall, a static takeoff is not recommended
with a crosswind greater than 25 kt.
TAKEOFF AND THRUST SETTINGS
Do not use the steering handle during the takeoff roll. Use the rudder pedals.
Advance the thrust levers to 40% N1. After the engines stabilize, increase thrust until the AT
engages and automatically moves the thrust levers to TO/GA detent. Do not ride or provide
positive pressure to the thrust levers once the ATs take over.
Verify target N1 is set, engine parameters are normal and the ATTCS indication is armed.
After the takeoff thrust is set, the captain’s hand remains on the thrust levers until V1. Any
abnormality during the takeoff roll must be called out in a loud and clear voice.
NOTE: After 60 kt, the ENG TLA NOT TOGA EICAS message is displayed whenever the
thrust levers are not in the TO/GA detent. The PIC may elect to continue by manually moving
the thrust levers to the TO/GA detent and verifying target thrust is set. This message may
remain even if the N1 target is achieved.

SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
TAKEOFF
Page: 04-08.3 of 20
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
The 80 kt callout has two main purposes: pilot incapacitation check and airspeed crosscheck.
NOTE: When flying with high pitch and/or bank angles, a spurious FUEL 1 (2) LO LEVEL
WARNING EICAS message may appear even though the fuel quantity indication is green.
ROTATION AND LIFTOFF
At VR, rotate smoothly toward the target pitch attitude in one continuous motion. Use a rotation
rate of approximately 3° per second. Correct rotation technique is important to ensure adequate
performance is obtained.
Rotate the airplane following the flight director guidance. With all engines operating, the flight
director guidance commands a pitch attitude to maintain V2 + 10 kt while climbing to the
acceleration altitude.
After liftoff and once a positive rate of climb has been established, select landing gear up.
INITIAL CLIMB SPEEDS
Reaching 1,000 ft AFE or minimum flap retraction altitude, with flaps retracted, maintain airspeed
at or less than 200 kt up to 2,500 ft (unless operating in class B airspace), then normal climb
profile or ATC assigned speed.
Green dot may be used when an immediate return to field scenario does not allow time to
calculate landing speeds.
SLAT / FLAPS RETRACTION
The flaps are retracted using the “F -BUG,” and pitch angle adjusted to maintain an appropriate
rate of climb.
After slat/flaps retraction is complete, accelerate to a normal enroute climb speed. When the
“F-BUG” disappears before total slat/flaps retraction, retract slat/flaps following Green Dot + 10 kt.
During flaps 4 takeoffs, until the flaps are retracted to flaps 2, the flap limit speed may be less
than the desired VFS.
After each flap retraction, the PM checks the selected airspeed bug to confirm it matches the
VFS bug. When the selected airspeed bug is less, it is adjusted up to the VFS. bug or flap limit
speed, whichever is less.

NORMAL TAKEOFF (cont'd.)
ACTIONS and CALLOUTS
PF PM
Silently verify magenta FLCH active
At acceleration
altitude
“CLIMB SEQUENCE, AFTER TAKEOFF
CHECKLIST” • Retract flaps on schedule

accomplish the After Takeoff
Checklist
“AFTER TAKEOFF
CHECKLIST COMPLETE”
(1) If issued a heading clearance on departure, disarm LNAV before takeoff.
CROSSWIND TAKEOFF
During rotation, keep the control wheel in the displaced position to maintain the wings level
during liftoff. Smoothly recover from the sideslip by slowly neutralizing the control wheel and
rudder pedals after liftoff.
NOTE: When operating on contaminated runways, ensure runway alignment before applying
takeoff thrust.
COLD WEATHER OPERATIONS
Takeoff is prohibited with frost, ice, snow, or slush adhering to any aircraft critical surface.
When performing a Single Engine Taxi Out in icing conditions, the Engine Run-Up procedure
must be accomplished before takeoff.
ENGINE RUN-UP PROCEDURE
Apply brakes and advance the thrust levers to 40%. Let engines stabilize and continue thrust
lever advancement to approximately 54% N1. Hold the selected thrust setting for 30 seconds or
until fan vibration level returns to normal. The engine run-up procedure may be performed in
conjunction with final takeoff power settings.
NOTE: The Engine Run-Up procedures is recommended to be accomplished before takeoff in
the following circumstances:
• When performing a Two Engine Taxi Out in icing conditions.
• In icing conditions, for ground operations of more than 30 minutes.
• In case of increased fan vibration due to fan ice accumulation.

TAKEOFF
Page: 04-08.7 of 20

REJECTED TAKEOFF (RTO)
An RTO procedure is initiated prior to V1 when any emergency event occurs affecting the safety
of the flight. Many faults may cause a decision to reject, such as an engine fire, engine failure,
or some other system faults or events. The sooner an RTO decision is made, the greater the
chance to safely stop within runway limits. RTO above V1 may lead to a runway overrun and is
the main cause of overrun accidents.
Once the decision to reject a takeoff is made, an RTO must not be delayed. Delaying the RTO
may lead to a runway overrun.
A successful RTO depends upon:
• Adequate briefing, procedures, and coordination
• Accurate alert and monitoring during the takeoff phase
• Callouts made in loud, clear, and precise communication
• Captain’s timely decision
RESPONSIBILITY
Whoever first identifies the undesirable event or malfunction must announce it. The decision to
perform an RTO is the captain’s responsibility. The RTO must be immediately initiated.
ACTIONS
• Actions are taken considering that, during all takeoff procedures, the captain keeps their
hand on the thrust levers until V1.
• Once the RTO decision is made, the first action is to set thrust levers to idle, disconnect
the autothrottle, verify autobrake actuation, or manually apply maximum brakes, and apply
reverse thrust, as required
• The use of maximum manual braking on wet or dry runways generally improves the
allowable takeoff weight but requires immediate braking action by the captain during an RTO.
Operationally speaking, however, the autobrake set at RTO helps by reducing the workload on
the initial maneuver. When the takeoff runway available is limited by performance, the pilot
manually overrides the autobrake as soon as practical and apply maximum braking.
NOTE: When an RTO is performed below 60 kt (before HOLD is displayed) and AT is not
disconnected, the autothrottle moves the thrust levers back to TO/GA detent, when both
throttles are moved to a position above 40° TLA. When throttles are moved below 40° TLA,
or reverse thrust is applied during an RTO, the AT disconnects.
• Directional control must be maintained by applying rudder pedal steering
• Monitor airplane deceleration, cancel any aural warnings upon malfunction identification,
verify reverse thrust status, and advise ATC
• At 60 kt or below, thrust lever(s) may be smoothly returned to minimum reverse, then to idle at
30 kt, or kept at reverse until the airplane comes to a complete stop. The flight deck crew
must consider airplane speed, runway length available, and the runway condition.

Date: 11 DEC 23
• The airplane remains on the runway until it is absolutely clear an emergency evacuation is
not necessary, and the entire scenario analyzed. When necessary, external information is
requested, or the runway vacated to a safe position according to ATC instructions.
• When the RTO decision was made due to a fire, consider positioning the airplane so the
fire is on the downwind side of the fuselage
• When a decision to proceed to a new takeoff is made just after an RTO, all FMS initialization
data is verified and confirmed, when 80 kt was exceeded
When the decision is made to reject the takeoff on a contaminated runway, maximum
deceleration is achieved by using maximum reverse thrust and maximum braking. Anticipate the
possibility of skidding. Keep the nosewheel centered and maintain directional control with the
rudder and small inputs of the nosewheel steering.
When a skid develops, reduce reverse thrust to idle reverse and, when necessary, return the
engines to idle forward thrust to return to the centerline. Regain the centerline with nosewheel
steering, rudder, and/or differential braking.
Directional control problems on slick runways may be encountered due to excessive cycling of
the anti-skid system. To correct the condition, reduce brake pressure.
REJECTED TAKEOFF
ACTIONS and CALLOUTS
CP FO
• The pilot first noticing the condition or malfunction identifies and
announces it.
Decision to reject the
takeoff
• “REJECT” or “REJECT MY
CONTROLS”
• Reduce thrust levers to idle,
disconnect AT, and apply reverse
thrust as necessary
• Verify or apply maximum braking
• Use reverse thrust as necessary
• Smoothly return thrust levers to idle
• Monitor airplane
deceleration status
• Verify reverse thrust
• Cancel any aural warnings
• Call out:
“80 KNOTS”
• Advise ATC
REJECTED TAKEOFF (cont'd.)

NOTE - Callouts are shown in bold text.
TAIL STRIKE CONSIDERATIONS
Although tail strikes are more common during the landing phase of flight, they may also occur
during takeoff and may cause extensive structural damage. The major identified causes of a tail
strike during takeoffs are:
• Improper Stabilizer Trim
• Improper Rotation Speed
• Excessive Rotation Rate
CLOSE - IN TURNS TAKEOFF
In case of an immediate turn after takeoff due to obstacle or special departure procedures,
initiate the turn as required at V2 + 10 kt. When the turn is completed, accelerate, and retract
flaps using the “F-BUG.”

TAKEOFF
Page: 04-08.11 of 20
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
WINDSHEAR
Whenever windshear is suspected during takeoff, the following precautions are taken:
• Use maximum takeoff thrust (T/O 1 with no reduction).
• Use optimized flaps.
NOTE: Selection of LLVL WIND SHR in ACARS will return T/O 1, Optimized Flaps settings,
i.e., Flaps 1 when obstacle and runway length permit, and may return optimized V2. When
ACARS is not available, contact the Dispatcher.
NOTE: Optimized V2 simply means that when runway length, aircraft weight, and weather
conditions require, V1 and V2 will be automatically increased with the intent to pick up a
slightly higher speed lifting off the runway.
• Fly the flight director guidance.
• Use the longest runway available with the lowest possibility of a windshear encounter.
• Turn ON the radar using the FSBY OVRD on the Weather Radar Virtual Controller Panel to
enable it before initiating the takeoff when convective activity is in the vicinity of the airport
and departure path.
• Monitor airspeed trend during takeoff. In case of any evidence of windshear before V1,
consider an RTO.
• Develop an awareness of normal airspeed, attitude, and vertical speed. Closely monitor the
vertical flight path instruments (e.g., vertical speed and altimeters) and call out any deviations.
WINDSHEAR ESCAPE MANEUVER
When a windshear warning is encountered after V1 and during initial climb:
• Advance thrust levers to MAX and press the TOGA button;
• Follow flight director guidance;
• Do not change flaps or landing gear configuration until out of the windshear condition and
terrain clearance assured;
• Reduce to climb thrust, retract landing gear and slats/flaps when the airplane is out of the
windshear condition.
NOTE: PM states “CLIMBING” or “DESCENDING” based upon VSI indication until the
aircraft has returned to a safe airspeed and altitude.
When the Caution/Warning WSHEAR indicator on the PFD disappears, the PM states
“WINDSHEAR CAUTION GONE” or “WINDSHEAR WARNING GONE.”
When the radar altimeter indicates less than 1,000 ft AGL and the airplane is descending,
the PM calls “DESCENDING (___)” based upon the RA value observed. For example:
“DESCENDING 700,” “DESCENDING 600.”
NOTE: When the windshear caution is active, the pilot has the option of continuing the flight
using the current flight director mode or selecting windshear escape guidance mode by
pressing the TOGA button. The windshear escape guidance mode does not automatically
revert to any other flight guidance mode. The pilot must manually select another mode in
order to exit windshear escape guidance.

ENGINE FAILURE AT OR AFTER V1
Single engine operations require strict adherence to the Engine Failure At Or After V1 profile.
For one engine inoperative, limit the bank angle to:
• 15° at V2
• 20° at V2 + 5 kt
• 25° at V2 + 10 kt

ENGINE FAILURE, FIRE, SEVERE DAMAGE, OR SEPARATION
(AT OR AFTER V1) – ACTIONS AND CALLS
ENGINE FAILURE, FIRE, SEVERE DAMAGE, OR SEPARATION
AT OR AFTER V1
ACTIONS AND CALLOUTS
PF PM
• The pilot first noticing the condition or malfunction announces the malfunction
• When an engine fails, EO AUTO (white) arms on the upper left side of FMA
Before VR
• Control the airplane using rudder
“CHECK THRUST”
• Cancel aural warning
• Verify TO-X RSV thrust active on
operating engine. If not, push thrust
levers to MAX.
“THRUST CHECKED”
At VR • Rotate to appropriate pitch attitude
“ROTATE”
Positive rate of
climb
• Confirm positive rate of climb
“GEAR UP”
Climb at V2 to V2+10
• Verify positive rate of climb
“POSITIVE RATE”
“GEAR UP”
• Position landing gear level up
• Monitor speed and altitude
400 ft AGL
“HDG, AUTOPILOT ON”
• Silently verify BANK ON
• Select HDG and AUTOPILOT and
• Silently verify BANK ON
EO AUTO (magenta) becomes active on the upper right side of FMA
Silently verify FLCH active and airplane is pitching for VFS
At Acceleration
Altitude
• Retract flaps on schedule
“FLAPS ___”
• Retract flaps according to PF’s
command until flaps 0
“FLAPS UP”
Reaching VFS
“CONTINUOUS, BANK OFF”
Set CONT thrust and BANK OFF
ENGINE FAILURE, FIRE, SEVERE DAMAGE, OR SEPARATIONAT OR AFTER V1 (cont'd.)

ENGINE FIRE, SEVERE DAMAGE, OR SEPARATION QRC
ACTIONS AND CALLOUTS
QRC ACTIONS and CALLOUTS
PF PM
Reading the QRC
• Disengage Autothrottle
“DISENGAGED”
• Place hand on failed engine thrust
lever and say:
“THRUST LEVER 1(2) IDLE
CONFIRM?”
• Reduce affected engine thrust lever
to idle and say:
“1(2) IDLE”
• Confirm the failed engine’s START /
STOP selector:
“CONFIRMED”
• Confirm failed engine’s fire EXT
Handle:
“CONFIRMED”
• Read checklist aloud:
“AUTOTHROTTLE DISENGAGE”
“THRUST LEVER 1(2) IDLE”
• Confirm the failed engine’s thrust
lever is being reduced:
“CONFIRMED”
• Place hand over the failed engine
START / STOP selector
“START / STOP SELECTOR 1(2)
CONFIRM?”
• Select the START / STOP selector
to STOP
• Place hand on failed engine fire
extinguisher handle:
“FIRE EXTINGUISHER HANDLE 1
(2) CONFIRM?”
• Pull FIRE EXTINGUISHER Handle
• Complete the Engine Failure Fire,
Severe Damage, or Separation
Checklist:
“_________ CHECKLIST
COMPLETE”
When the QRH is
complete
“AFTER TAKEOFF CHECKLIST”
Accomplish the procedures and
checklist:
“AFTER TAKEOFF CHECKLIST
COMPLETE”

TCAS
GENERAL
During operations in the terminal area:
• Set the MFD range to 5 or 12.5 nm as appropriate
TRAFFIC ADVISORY (TA)
A TCAS intruder generating a TA is depicted by a solid yellow circle on the TCAS display and is
accompanied by a “TRAFFIC, TRAFFIC” aural warning.
When a TA is generated, the:
• PF places hands on the control column and thrust levers to be prepared to react to TCAS
resolution
• PF visually searches for the intruding aircraft and maneuver to maintain separation only
when the traffic is acquired visually
• PM turns all external lights and the seat belt sign ON
• PM refers to the TCAS display, states the relative position, and assists the PF in a visual
search for the intruder. When a deviation is required after visual acquisition of the intruder,
notify ATC as soon as practical.
RESOLUTION ADVISORY (RA)
A TCAS intruder generating an RA is depicted by a solid red square on the TCAS display,
accompanied by threat escape voice message, and a vertical speed indication to be flown
as an RA maneuver.
The RA-generated vertical speed is a climb or descent rate indicated by a green arc on the VSI.
Unless it is unequivocally clear the target acquired visually is the one generating the RA and
there are no complicating circumstances, the TCAS RA compliance procedure must be followed.
TCAS (RA) compliance procedure:
PF:
AUTOPILOT .................................................................................................... DISCONNECT
AUTOTHROTTLE ........................................................................................... DISCONNECT
PITCH .................................................................................(green rectangle) FLY-TO / ZONE
THRUST ......................................................................................................... AS REQUIRED
As much as possible, clear the airspace in which you maneuver. Adjust vertical rate promptly
and smoothly. Keep the VSI needle outside the red band pitching for the FLY-TO / ZONE.
Receiving the “CLEAR OF CONFLICT” aural, return to your assigned altitude quickly and
smoothly, unless directed otherwise by ATC.

PM:
Monitors the airspeed and altitude and calls PF attention for altitude or airspeed too low or
airspeed close to maximum.
Keeps scanning for visual contact with the intruder.
Advises ATC as soon as possible that you are climbing or descending in response to a TCAS RA.
WARNING: WHEN AN EGPWS WARNING OCCURS DURING AN RA MANEUVER,
IMMEDIATELY ABANDON THE RA MANEUVER AND EXECUTE THE APPROPRIATE
EGPWS RECOVERY PROCEDURE.
WARNING: IT IS POSSIBLE IN SOME CASES TO HAVE INSUFFICIENT AIRPLANE
PERFORMANCE TO FOLLOW THE TCAS COMMAND WITHOUT FLYING INTO STALL
WARNING OR BUFFET. CONDITIONS WHERE THIS MAY OCCUR INCLUDE:
• BANK ANGLE IN EXCESS OF 15°
• OPERATIONS AT AIRPORTS ABOVE 5,300 FT MSL OR TEMPERATURES
GREATER THAN ISA + 28°C
• ENGINE INOPERATIVE
• FAILURE TO CONFIGURE THE AIRPLANE TO GO-AROUND FOLLOWING A
CLIMB RA IN LANDING CONFIGURATION
• FAILURE TO ADVANCE THRUST TO MAXIMUM CONTINUOUS THRUST
FOLLOWING A CLIMB RA AT REDUCED THRUST
• SPEEDS LESS THAN NORMAL OPERATION SPEEDS
• ABNORMAL CONFIGURATIONS, WHICH REDUCE PERFORMANCE (E.G., GEAR
DOWN)
• TCAS COMMAND REVERSAL TO A “CLIMB-CLIMB NOW”
• BUFFET MARGIN LESS THAN 0.3 G
UPSETS
Refer to Chapter 05 EMERGENCY / ABNORMAL PROCEDURES, UPSETS.

CLIMB
Page: 04-09.2 of 4
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
GENERAL
Continuous monitoring of the flight path during climb and level off, by at least one pilot, is required.
The climb phase has a significant impact in fuel consumption when considering short and
medium range flights. It represents 20% to 40% of the trip time, registering fuel flows 40%
greater than during cruise.
CLIMB
Crews should not manually select CLB 1.
Exceptions are:
• Operationally needed for safety of flight
or
• Directed by departure procedure.
CLIMB DEVIATION AND CALLOUTS
The PM is responsible for monitoring all aspects of the climb phase of flight and must call out any
observed discrepancies or deviations. When a deviation is announced:
The PF immediately corrects the deviation and responds with “CORRECTING.”
The PM verifies the correction is appropriate and calls any further deviations.
DEVIATION CALLOUT
Target speed +/-10 KIAS or .02M “SPEED”
Bank angle in excess of 30° “BANK”
Heading from bugged +/- 10° “HEADING”
Course from established 1/4 deflection “COURSE”
Pitch angle above or below typical that may
result in a profile deviation
“PITCH”
Vertical speed too high or low that may result
in a profile deviation
“VERTICAL SPEED”
Any autopilot or FD malfunction Call the failure
EGPWS callouts not performed automatically
by the airplane
Perform callouts

ENROUTE CLIMB
CLIMB
ACTIONS AND CALLOUTS
PF PM
At 10,000 ft MSL “TEN THOUSAND” “TEN THOUSAND”
• STERILE sign switched OFF.
At 18,000 ft or
transition altitude
“STANDARD”
• Push the BARO SET (PUSH
STD) and cross-check the
altimeters.
“STANDARD”
• Push the BARO SET (PUSH
STD) and cross-check the
altimeters.
• External lights OFF, except NAV,
STROBE, and RED BCN.
• FSTN BELTS signs may be
switched OFF upon captain’s
discretion.
NOTE - The captain sets the IESS to STD climbing through 18,000 ft or the transition altitude.
COLD WEATHER OPERATIONS
Performance changes, such as decreasing airspeed, reduced climb rate, and/or higher than
normal pitch angle, may be an indication ice has formed on the airframe. When ice-related
performance decreases are noted, ensure all icing systems are selected ON. Make sure the
required thrust and attitude are being properly maintained.
NOTE: Engine and Wing Ice Protection Systems operation is automatic and based upon the
primary ice detection system. However, the flight deck crew remains responsible for monitoring
icing conditions and for manual activation of the ice protection system when icing conditions
are present and the ice detection system is not activating the ice protection system.

SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
CRUISE
Page: 04-10.2 of 12
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
GENERAL
The cruise phase of flight begins upon reaching the top of climb (TOC) and terminates, at the
top of descent (TOD).
Top of climb (TOC) is considered an area of vulnerability (AOV). All non-critical duties must
be deferred until after cruise speed and altitude are stable, as indicated by a fixed and static
speed within the speed bug.
Monitoring of thrust management and cruise speed must be maintained by both crewmembers,
beginning at TOC, and throughout the entire cruise phase of flight.
CRUISE DEVIATIONS AND CALLOUTS
The PM is responsible for monitoring all aspects of the cruise phase of flight and must call out
any observed discrepancies or deviations. When a deviation is announced:
The PF immediately corrects the deviation and responds with “CORRECTING.”
The PM verifies the correction is appropriate and calls any further deviations.
DEVIATION CALLOUT
Target speed +/-10 KIAS or .02M “SPEED”
Bank angle in excess of 30° “BANK”
Heading from bugged +/- 10° “HEADING”
Course from established 1/4 deflection “COURSE”
Altitude from selected +/- 100 ft “ALTITUDE”
Pitch angle above or below typical that may
result in a profile deviation
“PITCH”
Any autopilot or FD malfunction Call the failure
EGPWS callouts not performed automatically
by the airplane
Perform callouts
CRUISE SPEED
CRUISE SPEEDS
NORMAL APG
Give consideration to schedule integrity and efficiency. Deviations from the planned true air
speed may significantly increase trip fuel burn and must be critically evaluated to ensure safety
margins are maintained.
NOTE: An ATC assigned speed overrides APG unless safety will be compromised (i.e., fuel).

MAXIMUM RANGE CRUISE (MRC)
MRC is the speed that provides the furthest distance traveled for a given amount of fuel burned
and provides the best possible fuel mileage.
LONG RANGE CRUISE SPEED (LRC)
LRC offers a somewhat faster cruise speed, but with only a small sacrifice (1%) in fuel mileage.
It is an accepted compromise between fuel mileage and shorter cruise times.
PITCH LIMIT INDICATOR (PLI)
Operations with the PLIs continuously displayed is prohibited. Occasional flickering of the PLIs
due to meteorological conditions is acceptable. Always maintain a safe margin above green dot.
ALTITUDE
Altitude selection is based upon the altitude capability presented on the dispatch release or
ACARS.
When choosing a cruise altitude consider:
• Planned altitude
• FMS Perf Data Ceiling Altitude (provides minimum of 300 fpm rate of climb based on selected
cruise speed)
• Tropopause altitude
• Weather (Mountain wave, turbulence, winds aloft, convective activity, ISA deviation)
• Green dot
FLIGHT CONTROL TRIMMING
AIRPLANE TRIMMING
The autopilot trims the airplane for pitch using the stabilizer trim function. Roll and yaw trimming
is achieved manually through dedicated switches.
When the airplane is not properly trimmed for roll and yaw, the autopilot applies primary control
displacement to compensate for tendencies, which under some conditions, may significantly
increase drag and consequently affect fuel performance

TRIMMING TECHNIQUE
YAW
With the autopilot engaged, ensure the fuel is properly balanced, engine thrust is symmetric,
the HDG mode is selected before trimming.
The yaw trim switch is actuated in the direction that corresponds to the slip/skid indicator
position. In most cases, only small and brief actuation are sufficient.
To avoid over trimming, allow approximately three to five seconds between actuations.
ROLL
Roll trimming is performed after the airplane has been trimmed for yaw. An exception to this rule
is a wing heavy condition, in manual flight, when the skip/skid indicator is centered.
In this case, roll trim is actuated in the same direction inputs are needed or towards the downside
of the control wheel, when a deflection is noticeable. Both parts of the roll trim switch are pressed
simultaneously. In most cases, only sharp, brisk movements are required.
FUEL TREND MONITORING
Flight deck crews must monitor flight time and fuel burn while in the cruise phase of flight with
reference to the flight plan. Fuel trend monitoring takes place for all flights beginning at the top of
climb (TOC) and at least once each hour thereafter.
The resources available to assist in this process are:
• Enroute Plan
• Expanded flight release
• PERF DATA page
When during flight the PIC determines unanticipated circumstances may result in landing with
less than Minimum Fuel, request air traffic delay information from ATC in order to make a
determination when a fuel status declaration is necessary.
Refer to Chapter 5 EMERGENCY / ABNORMAL PROCEDURES (Emergency/Minimum Fuel).
FUEL IMBALANCE
Fuel efficiency may be significantly affected as the primary control surfaces are deflected by the
autopilot in an effort to compensate for an imbalance condition.
A fuel imbalance, for which no EICAS message is displayed and has not yet exceeded 800 lb,
may be regarded as a normal procedure and may be corrected without the use of the QRH.
Whenever a fuel imbalance exists, ensure it is not caused by a fuel leak.

With both engines operative, this is done by comparing fuel quantity indications on EICAS with
the FMS Fuel, before attempting a fuel crossfeed.
NOTE: When the FUEL IMBALANCE caution message is not displayed on the EICAS and
a crossfeed is performed, the advisory message FUEL EQUAL - CROSSFEED OPEN is
displayed, regardless of the imbalance value. This may lead to an additional imbalance
condition.
THROTTLE TECHNIQUE
Rapid and large throttle excursions must be avoided as they may be detrimental to the various
engine components and may accelerate engine deterioration.
When feasible, large speed changes with autothrottle engaged are planned in advance.
Excessive speed adjustments may cause thrust levers to completely retard and advance.
Override the autothrottles to prevent excessive movement and speed changes.
TURBULENCE
Flight through severe turbulence must be avoided.
For general turbulence procedures refer to Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 07
Adverse Weather (Turbulence paragraph).
TURBULENT AIR PENETRATION SPEED (VB)
Turbulent air penetration speed provides high/low speed margins in severe turbulence. Normally,
it is more comfortable to slow when encountering turbulence. As turbulence intensifies, airspeed
may need to be increased to avoid stalling. Increase speed as necessary up to turbulent air
penetration speed. Pilots must not exceed turbulent air penetration speed to avoid structural
damage. Consider descending to increase buffet margin.
AIRSPEED
Severe turbulence causes large and often rapid variations in indicated airspeed. Do not chase
the airspeed.
ATTITUDE
Maintain wings level and pitch attitude. Use the attitude indicator as the primary instrument. Do
not use sudden large control inputs.

PITCH TRIM
Maintain control of the airplane with the elevators. After establishing the trim setting for
penetration speed, do not change pitch trim.
ALTITUDE
Large altitude variations are possible in severe turbulence; do not chase altitude. Allow altitude
and airspeed to vary and maintain attitude.
AUTOTHROTTLE AND THRUST SETTING
When severe turbulence cannot be avoided, disconnect the autothrottle and make an initial thrust
setting for the target airspeed. Change thrust setting only in case of unacceptable airspeed
variation.
When the autothrottle is used, use the autothrottles override (OVRD) function to avoid changes
of engine thrust setting.
NOTE: Do not extend flaps, except for approach and landing.
SPURIOUS EICAS MESSAGES
Occasionally, turbulence may activate spurious EICAS messages, for example:
• AOA LIMIT FAIL
• STALL PROT FAIL
• REACTIVE WSHEAR FAIL
• AT FAIL
In some cases, loss of the following may occur:
• Low Speed Awareness (LSA)
• Pitch Limit Indicator (PLI)
• Stick Shaker; and
• ADS Probe 1(2)(3)(4) FAIL messages displayed after landing.
When the turbulent air condition causes the AT disengagement (AT FAIL), it occurs almost
simultaneously with the other EICAS message activation. Since the Master Caution tone has
priority over the aural alert associated with the AT disengagement (“THROTTLE”), the pilots may
not immediately recognize the AT status. The AT disengagement aural alert sounds after the
Master Caution tone are canceled.
When the above messages post on EICAS during turbulent or gusty conditions, pilots should
monitor AT status and airspeed and make the necessary adjustments.

MANUAL FLIGHT IN SEVERE TURBULENCE
The autopilot is engaged when flying in severe turbulence. At the pilot’s discretion, when the
crew reverts to manual flight in severe turbulence, trim the airplane for penetration speed and do
not change the stabilizer position. Control the airplane pitch attitude with the elevators using the
attitude indicator as the primary instrument. Do not make sudden large elevator control inputs.
Corrective actions to regain the desired attitude are smooth. Control the airplane attitude first,
then make corrections for airspeed, altitude, and heading.
RVSM
FLIGHT CREW AND ATC COMMUNICATIONS
RVSM operations require a more stringent altitude and tracking awareness on the part of flight
deck crews.
Communication between the flight crews and ATC requires early identification of problems and
subsequent notification to ATC of conditions that impact the ability of the aircraft to continue to
operate safely within RVSM designated airspace. Some of these areas of concern are:
• The inability of flight deck crews to maintain assigned and selected altitudes within a maximum
of +200 ft tolerance due to turbulence and/or mountain wave influence
NOTE: Severe turbulence and/or mountain wave activity can induce altitude deviations
of 200 ft or greater.
• Failure of any of the following required aircraft systems:
– Transponder(s)
– Either primary altimeter system (captain's or first officer's)
– Altitude alerting and capture capability
– Autopilot
When the flight deck crew encounters any of the above abnormalities or problems, they advise
ATC immediately that they are unable to continue as RVSM compliant, and coordinate a
contingency plan with ATC.
NOTE: TCAS not required.
PREFLIGHT PLANNING
Prior to conducting flight operations in RVSM airspace, flight deck crews must be aware of any
conditions affecting operation in RVSM airspace. This may include, but is not limited to:
• Reviewing reported and forecasted weather for the route of flight to identify possible areas of
severe turbulence and/or mountain wave activity considerations
• Verifying the aircraft is currently approved for RVSM operations. This can be accomplished by
ensuring the placard “RVSM CERTIFIED” is installed and contains no deferral sticker.
• Reviewing any maintenance log actions or MEL issues that invalidate RVSM certification or
capability

• During the external preflight inspection, paying particular attention to the aircraft fuselage
skin and areas around or influencing air flow around the static port areas for any potential
air flow disruptions
• During the internal checks, ensuring both primary altimeters are set to the local QNH (field
elevation) and they indicate within 75 ft of the current field location elevation. Additionally,
both primary altimeters must indicate within 75 ft of each other (only for aircraft that operate in
the RVSM environment).
PRIOR TO ENTRY INTO RVSM AIRSPACE
When any required equipment fails prior to entering RVSM airspace, a new ATC clearance is
obtained.
Prior to entering into RVSM airspace, flight deck crews ensure that:
• Both primary altimeter systems are operational (captain's and first officer's altimeters)
• The autopilot Automatic Flight Control System (AFCS) is operational
• The altitude alerting system is operational
NOTE: Swapping the transponder is not necessary because either transponder receives
its air data from the sourced side.
FLIGHT IN RVSM AIRSPACE
While flying in RVSM airspace:
• The flight deck crew increases vigilance to prevent deviations from the assigned flight level
• When changing flight levels, the airplane does not overshoot or undershoot a flight level
by more than 150 ft
• The flight deck crew continually monitors for altitude deviations from assigned exceeding
200 ft and notify ATC immediately
• To avoid TCAS Resolution Advisories during climb and level offs when aware of traffic in close
vicinity, monitor the climb and consider reducing vertical speed to 1,500 ft/min or less at least
1,000 ft before reaching the cleared flight level
• The autopilot is operative and engaged during level cruise, except for circumstances such as
the need to re-trim the airplane or when it must be disengaged due to turbulence
• The flight deck crew notifies ATC of contingencies (equipment failures, weather conditions)
affecting the ability to maintain the current flight level
– When altitude difference between PFD 1 and PFD 2 exceeds 180 ft, select ADS 3 on
the PFD that does not agree with IESS.
– To verify if altimeter difference is within tolerance, the flight crew should proceed as follows:
• Altitude ............................................................................................................ STABILIZE
Maintain variation within ± 50 ft.
• Air Speed ........................................................................................................ STABILIZE
Maintain variation within ± 5 kt.

– Wait a minimum of 15 seconds in straight and leveled flight, preferably with Autopilot

• When unable to notify ATC and obtain a clearance prior to deviating from the cleared flight

When required, declare an emergency with ATC when unable to comply with the assigned

HOLDING
In order to achieve maximum fuel economy, holding is performed in a clean configuration, gear
up.
FMS HOLDING CALCULATIONS
Leg times are defaulted to 1 minute at or below 14,000 ft and 1.5 minutes above 14,000 ft.
These values may be modified.
SPEED:
SKYWEST MINIMUM HOLDING SPEED
6,000 ft and below 200 kt
6,001 ft and above 210 kt
CAUTION: Some meteorological conditions may require holding at speeds greater
than the outlined minimum speed in order to provide an adequate margin above
stick-shaker speed (e.g., mountain wave, turbulence, high winds aloft, icing
conditions). When holding at speeds in excess of FAA Maximum Holding Speed,
notify ATC.
FAA MAXIMUM HOLDING SPEED
S / L – 6,000 ft 200 kt
6,001 ft – 14,000 ft 230 kt
14,001 ft and above 265 kt
NOTE: All entries are inhibited one minute prior to holding.

COLD WEATHER OPERATIONS
CAUTION: Intentional flight in severe icing conditions is prohibited. Holding in icing
conditions with flaps/slats extended should be avoided.
Operation in moderate to severe icing conditions may allow ice to buildup on the fan spinner
and/or blades. When allowed to accumulate, asymmetrical ice shedding may result in high fan
vibration.
For operations in moderate to severe icing conditions, reduce one thrust lever at a time towards
idle, advance to a minimum of 70% N1 for 10-30 seconds, then return thrust lever to the position
required for flight conditions.
Accomplish the periodic engine run up every 15 minutes when fan ice buildup is suspected (high
indicated or perceived vibration).
NOTE: Engine vibration indication may peak to the maximum value prior to ice shedding;
however, this does not affect the engine.
Operation of the ignition system is not required for this procedure provided the FADEC automatic
relight system is operating normally.
ICE CRYSTALS
Encountering ice crystals may result in an accumulation of ice on some interior engine
components causing a loss of thrust.
If engine performance degrades due to ice crystals, change altitude, if appropriate.
FREEZING RAIN AND FREEZING DRIZZLE
Atmospheric conditions involving freezing rain or freezing drizzle associated with supercooled
large droplets (SLD) may present a condition beyond those for which the airplane is certified.
Both freezing rain and freezing drizzle may exist down to ground level and cause ice to form
quite rapidly on all surfaces even during short exposures and on areas not normally known to
be subjected to ice accretion. This means the airplane is not designed to fly under freezing
rain/drizzle (SLD) conditions.
Abnormal ice formation on areas not usually affected by this phenomenon, or ice formation on a
previously treated upper surface of the wings, must be considered as severe icing conditions. In
this case, the anti-ice system is failing to reduce or control ice formation. Exit freezing rain/drizzle
conditions as soon as possible since continuous flight under such conditions is hazardous.

Operation in moderate to severe icing conditions may allow ice to build up on the fan spinner
and/or blades. When allowed to accumulate, asymmetrical ice shedding may result in high fan
vibration.
For operations in moderate to severe icing conditions, reduce one thrust lever at a time:
• Towards idle,
• Then advance to a minimum of 70% N1 for 10-30 seconds,
• Then return thrust lever to position required for flight conditions.
Accomplish this procedure every 15 minutes when fan ice buildup is suspected (high vibration).
NOTE: Engine vibration indication may peak to the maximum value prior to ice shedding. This
does not affect the engine.
Operation of the ignition system is not required for this procedure provided the FADEC automatic
relight system is operating normally.
DRIFTDOWN
After an in-flight engine failure or shutdown, maintaining altitude may not be possible. The
following procedure preserves altitude. The driftdown speed is green dot.
DRIFTDOWN PROCEDURE
After an engine failure or in-flight engine shutdown, the EO RANGE page is automatically
displayed on the MCDU. To Initiate drift down:
1. VNAV and FMS SPEEDS must be active.
2. CONFIRM EO: On the EO RANGE PAGE, select LSK 5R (CONFIRM EO).
3. On the EO RANGE PAGE, MAX ALT will display at LSK 1L. Set MAX ALT in the altitude
selector.
NOTE: The EO RANGE page can be found on PERF 2/2.
4. Perform applicable checklists, notify ATC, and monitor descent.
These actions enable EO DRIFTDOWN. The aircraft automatically performs the following
functions:
• DRIFT DOWN appears above the FMA.
• DD becomes the active autothrottle mode.
• Thrust on the operating engine advances to CON.
NOTE: Some QRH procedures require autothrottles to be turned off. When this action is
directed, ensure the operating engine’s thrust lever remains at CON. Turning autothrottles
back on, when appropriate, re-enables DD speed mode.

• Speed decays to green dot while flight director commands altitude hold.
• Once speed reaches green dot, gradual descent begins.
• Reaching selected altitude, ASEL and ALT occur. EO DRIFTDOWN is automatically canceled.
When EO DRIFTDOWN is no longer desired, it may be cancelled by selecting manual speeds,
turning VNAV off, or selecting EXIT EO AUTO on the EO RANGE page. To ensure the aircraft
does not resume EO DRIFTDOWN with further changes in automation, select EXIT EO AUTO
on the EO RANGE PAGE.
Without EO Automation:
If EO fails or is not available, initiate driftdown as follows:
1. Verify continuous thrust is selected and thrust levers are in TOGA detent.
2. Autothrottles OFF.
3. Manually bug green dot.
4. Set altitude selector to MAX ALT as indicated on the EO RANGE page.
5. Select green FLCH to ensure aircraft slows to bugged green dot, begins descent, but does
not attempt to level off at an intermediate altitude.
6. Perform applicable checklists, notify ATC, and monitor descent.
UPSETS
Refer to Chapter 05 EMERGENCY / ABNORMAL PROCEDURES, UPSETS.
ENGINE VIBRATION DURING CRUISE
When an amber engine vibration indication is displayed on the EICAS, with the airplane at top of
climb or in cruise flight, at approximately Mach 0.78, with associated engine N2 indication within
84 - 89%, a slight reduction in airplane speed may decrease engine vibration levels.
Refer to Chapter 05 Emergency/Abnormal (Engine Vibration (VIB) During Climb paragraph).
When the speed reduction does not decrease the vibration levels, the applicable QRH procedure
must be promptly accomplished.
At any time an engine vibration occurs in any other condition, the applicable QRH procedures
must be accomplished.

DESCENT DELEGATION OF DUTIES TABLE
PRIOR TO DESCENT TASKS
PF PM
Prior to descent
TASKS (1)
• MCDU programing:
Load the STAR (when applicable)
and approach for planned runway
• Set and cross-check the following
items as required for the approach
type:
– Minimums
– Frequency
– Course
– Accomplish WAANT briefing.
(4)
• Set AUTOBRAKE, as required
• Verify approach and landing
speeds
TASKS (1)
• Obtain WX
• Perform PAX briefing
→
• Landing distance assessment
• MCDU programing (2)
– Determine landing weight
– Determine landing distance
– Set landing speed data
• Set and cross-check the following
items, as required for the approach
type:
– Minimums
– Frequency
– Course
– Call Company when in range,
as necessary.
Transition
altitude (3)
“DESCENT CHECKLIST”
(1) PM tasks are completed prior to the flow and reading the checklist.
(2) Refer to Landing Performance and Landing Speeds in this section.
(3) When transition altitude is not FL180, initiate the Callout, Flow, and Descent Checklist at FL180,
and ensure altimeters are adjusted to the local altimeter when below the actual transition altitude.
(4) Prior to conducting the Arrival WAANT briefing, the PM reads the arrival, approach, and missed
approach procedure fixes, speeds, and altitudes from the charts.
Simultaneously, PF verifies on the FLIGHT PLAN page:
• Waypoint names and sequence,
• Speed restrictions,
• Crossing altitudes,
• Appropriate discontinuities appear.
Both pilots visually cross-check the flight plan by sequencing through each waypoint on the MFD
PLAN page.
This should be accomplished prior to descent.

DESCENT EXPANDED CHECKLIST
(Flow Pattern - Challenge and Response - CP / FO)
LIGHTS AND SEAT BELT SIGNS .................................................................................... SET CP
Landing and wing inspection lights are on during all flight operations below FL180.
Ensure sterile and seat belt lights are on.
ALTIMETER ..................................................................(___) SET L&C / R&X-CHECKED CP/FO
Descending through 18,000 ft, the left, right, and standby altimeters are set to the current
QNH. The transition altitude is not 18,000 ft, ensure altimeters are set when below the
actual transition altitude.
The PM states all four digits of the altimeter setting. The PF restates the altimeter setting
and sets it on the appropriate altimeter.
Checklist dialog is conducted as follows:
Captain as PM:
CP: “Altimeter (___), set left and center.”
FO: “(___), set right and cross-checked.”
First officer as PM:
FO: “Altimeter (___).”
CP: “(___), set left and center.”
FO: “Set right and cross-checked.”
NOTE: The standby altimeter may be set to destination altimeter setting once the aircraft
is cleared below 18,000 ft.
LANDING DATA ................................................................................................. SET L / R CP/FO
Ensure landing data is set based upon ACARS, TLR, or the QRH.
AUTOBRAKE...................................................................................................................... (__) PF
Ensure Autobrake is set according to the landing conditions expected.

DESCENT
Page: 04-11.8 of 14
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
GENERAL
Good descent planning is essential to proper flight path management and stabilized approaches.
Continuous monitoring of the flight path during descent and level off, by at least one pilot, is
required.
A good rule of thumb is to be at 10,000 ft AFE, 30 miles from the airport at 250 kt. During the
descent, adjustments to descent rates may be necessary.
To calculate descent rate:
• Determine the amount of altitude to lose.
• Multiply the value by 3 (equals the distance required for descent, no wind).
• Multiply the current ground speed by 5 (equals the approximate rate of descent, no wind).
CAUTION: When operating in FLCH mode, excessive descent rates can occur.
Crews must monitor descent rates when descending in close proximity to terrain in
order to avoid a low altitude unstabilized approach.
NOTE: To preclude CFIT at low altitudes where terrain closure rates can significantly reduce
recognition and response time, vertical speed must not exceed 1,500 FPM when below 2,500
ft AGL. This provision does not apply when established on an approach or when conducting
alternate procedures outlined in an airport briefing guide.
DESCENT SPEEDS
Recommended descent speeds are dependent on operational requirements.
DESCENT SPEED SCHEDULE
Above 10,000 ft MSL 280 kt to M 0.74
Below 10,000 ft MSL 250 kt

DESCENT DEVIATIONS AND CALLOUTS
The PM is responsible for monitoring all aspects of the descent phase of flight and must call
out any observed discrepancies or deviations.
When a deviation is announced: The PF immediately corrects the deviation and responds
with “CORRECTING.”
The PM verifies the correction is appropriate and calls any further deviations.
DEVIATION CALLOUT
Target speed +/-10 KIAS or .02M “SPEED”
Bank angle in excess of 30° “BANK”
Heading from bugged +/- 10° “HEADING”
Course from established 1/4 deflection “COURSE”
Pitch angle above or below typical that may
result in a profile deviation
“PITCH”
Any autopilot or FD malfunction Call the failure
EGPWS callouts not performed automatically
by the airplane
Perform callouts
VNAV OPERATION
The FMS calculates the TOD based upon the speeds and angles entered on the PERF INIT
page. The default angle is 3°. Any change may be made on the PERF INIT page or PERF
DESCENT page, either during the FMS initialization or in flight.
Approaching the TOD, set the Altitude Selector to the cleared altitude; the FMS commands the
descent upon reaching the TOD. The FMS commands a VPATH descent.

SPEED BRAKE USAGE
The table below shows the deceleration distances required for IDLE thrust, for speed brakes up
or down, in a clean configuration.
CAUTION: This table is only to be used as a guide for descent planning and is no
guarantee the aircraft meets waypoint speed restrictions.
CONFIGURATION LEVEL FLIGHT
1,000 ft/min
DESCENT FLIGHT
SPEEDBRAKES DOWN
(STOWED)
1 NM for each 10 kt speed
reduction
2 NM for each 10 kt speed
reduction
SPEEDBRAKES UP
(DEPLOYED)
0.5 NM for each 10 kt speed
reduction
1 NM for each 10 kt speed
reduction
LANDING PERFORMANCE
Flight deck crews are required to make a landing performance assessment and determine a
Minimum Landing Distance using ACARS or the TLR based upon a destination airport’s current
conditions when:
• Conditions at the destination are different from those planned (e.g., runway surface
condition, runway braking capability, runway to be used, winds, airplane landing
weight/configuration/speed/deceleration devices); or
• The flight deck crew needs to know the absolute performance capability of the airplane in
emergencies or abnormal and irregular configurations of the airplane, such as an engine
failure or flight control malfunctions. In these circumstances, the flight deck crew must know
the actual landing distance (without an added safety margin) when making such an evaluation.
The landing performance assessment is made as close to landing, as practical, when the most
current airport conditions are available, respecting workload management.
Unless there are no other options, under emergency conditions, do not attempt to land on
runways that do not meet the assessment criteria, as specified by landing data information.
During the approach briefing, discuss:
• The outcome on the landing distance based upon runway conditions, etc. This detail, together
with wind gusts on the surface, affects the landing distance.
• The airplane status and operational restrictions
• Missed approach options when a failure occurs in the final approach affecting the required
landing distance. Failures affecting the landing distance are commonly associated to brakes,
ground spoilers, and/or thrust reversers.
• Autobrake selection according to runway conditions and type of operation
• The use of thrust reversers

Page: 04-11.11 of 14
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
On the FPL page, the altitude constrains and types of waypoints during the descent must be
assigned as DES. The constraint type is displayed directly above the altitude constraint. The
FMS automatically assigns constraints in the first half of the flight plans as climb (CLB) and
those in the last half as descent (DES). When needed, change the constraint type. For climb
constraints, C and CLB are accepted pilot entries; for D or DES are accepted pilot entries.
FLAPS CHOICE
Flaps 5 provides lower fuel consumption and reduced approach noise levels.
FULL flaps are required when the field length or conditions require its use.
Flaps FULL provides slower approach speed and reduced landing distance. Consider using
flaps FULL on short or contaminated runways, or to reduce required runway length due to
STALL PROT ICE SPEED.
LANDING SPEEDS
Obtain landing speeds and distances from ACARS, the TLR or QRH when ACARS is not
available.
NOTE: Ice speeds and landing performance must be used when the STALL PROT ICE
SPEED message is displayed on EICAS. When FMS approach speeds are below green dot,
maintain speed above green dot.
LAND AND HOLD SHORT OPERATIONS (LAHSO)
Land and Hold Short Operations (LAHSO), are approved at SkyWest Airlines under the following
conditions:
PILOT AUTHORITY
The PIC has the final authority to accept or decline any LAHSO clearance.
The PIC is expected to decline a LAHSO clearance when they determine it compromises safety.
When the PIC elects to decline the LAHSO clearance after receiving the ATIS, ATC must be
notified upon initial contact.

WEATHER
Weather minimums may be no lower than 1,000 ft and 3 miles (PAPI or VASI available and
working), otherwise 1,500 ft and 5 miles are required.
No windshear reported in the last 20 minutes prior to LAHSO clearance being issued.
The tail wind on the LAHSO runway must be less than 3 kt.
APPROACH
Approach must be stabilized by:
• 1,000 ft HAT for straight-in visual approaches
• MDA or 500 ft HAT, whichever is lower, when a circling maneuver is to be done after
completing an instrument approach
RUNWAY
ATC is responsible for providing available landing distance via ATIS, published U.S. terminal
procedures, or the special notices section of the Airport/Facility Directory.
• Calculated minimum landing distance must be less than LAHSO distance.
Dry runway required as per AeroData definition of dry runway. LAHSO operations are prohibited
on wet runways.
Touchdown must be in the first one-third of the available landing distance or in the first 3,000
ft, whichever is less.
Night LAHSO is conducted only where the FAA-Approved LAHSO night lighting system is
installed and operating.
No MEL item may exist affecting the stopping capability of the aircraft.
CLEARANCE
Pilots give a full read back of all LAHSO clearances.
A LAHSO clearance is not accepted below 1,000 ft AGL on final approach.
A LAHSO clearance, once accepted, must be adhered to just as any other ATC clearance,
unless an amended clearance is obtained, or an emergency occurs. A LAHSO clearance does
not preclude a rejected landing (go-around).
When the computation of LAHSO data interferes with other flight deck safety duties, do not
accept a LAHSO.

REJECTED LANDING
A rejected landing (go-around) is performed when:
• Unable to land in the first one-third of the available landing distance or first 3,000 ft, whichever
is less
• Any condition exists in which the PIC determines a safe LAHSO cannot be completed within
the confines of the hold short point (e.g., emergency situation, unsafe condition on the
runway, not properly configured)
On a rejected landing, fly the published missed approach procedure or instructions given by
ATC and:
• Promptly notify ATC
• Fly heading and/or altitude assignments as published until otherwise directed by ATC
AIRPORTS
Airports where LAHSO may be expected are ORD, DFW, YYC, and STL. These airports have an
FAA-approved LAHSO night lighting system. When the airport is not equipped with the lighting
system, like LAS, then you may still receive a LAHSO clearance during daytime operations.
(Refer to the LAHSO Lights graphic below.)

GENERAL
This section outlines recommended operating practices and techniques for approaches and
maneuvers.
APPROACH CONDUCT
No approach is initiated, unless the prevailing conditions are understood, briefed, and a landing
is anticipated without undue risk.
Approaches with Vertical Guidance are recommended, when available.
Visual approaches / VFR Arrivals may be flown in VMC, when FOM and Ops Specs requirements
are met, and the PIC determines it is a safe course of action.
Observe strict adherence to standard callout procedures. Any doubt as to the approach’s
success must lead to a go-around.
Guard the controls and throttles throughout all phases of the approach, landing, and rollout
when the autoflight system is engaged. Be prepared to manually land or initiate a go-around
any time it is considered necessary.
APPROACH DEVIATION CALLOUTS
The PM is responsible for monitoring all phases of the approach and must call out any observed
discrepancies or deviations.
When a deviation is announced:
The PF immediately corrects the deviation and responds with “CORRECTING.”
The PM verifies the correction is appropriate and calls any further deviations.
DEVIATION CALLOUT
Rate of descent more than 1,000 ft/min “SINK RATE”
Course and/or Glide Path deviation greater than 1 dot “COURSE” / “GLIDE PATH”
Above: Target speed +10 KIAS
Below: Target speed -5 KIAS; or any speed less than
VREF, whichever is higher
“SPEED”
Bank angle in excess of 25° “BANK”
Pitch angle below -5° or above 5° in relation to the
established pitch
“PITCH”
Any autopilot or FD malfunction Calls the failure
EGPWS callouts not performed automatically by the
airplane
Perform the expected callouts
Unstable approach “UNSTABLE, GO-AROUND”

APPROACH CONDUCT WITHOUT FMS
When it is not possible to use the FMS to conduct the approach, or the procedure is not loaded in
the navigation database, the following procedure is authorized:
• When possible, reset all radios and approach course prior to descent
• Starting the approach, V / L is selected as the primary navigation source
• HSI - Full compass or Arc mode
• Adjust the bearing pointers, as necessary (VOR or ADF)
• Hard tune radios and change course whenever necessary during the approach
• HDG lateral mode is used during all VOR approaches. When ILS or LOC only, is intended,
HDG lateral mode is used, until final course is intercepted on LOC lateral mode.
• FPA or V / S vertical modes are used during all VOR or LOC only approaches. When an ILS is
intended, GS vertical mode is used on glide path interception during final approach phase.
When a go-around is performed the PF asks for HDG lateral mode only and the PM is responsible
for hard tuning radios and setting courses according to the missed approach profile.
APPROACH MINIMA
Set approach minima as follows:
DA Set published DA rounded up to 10 ft increment.
RA (CAT II) Set published RA rounded up to 5 ft increment.
MDA Set published MDA plus 50 ft [i.e., derived decision altitude (DDA)]. If there is a
ball note see USING DA IN LIEU OF MDA below.
MDA (LOC) Set published MDA rounded up to 100 ft increment.
LP minima are not authorized.
For circle-to-land maneuvers set the published circle-to-land MDA for Category D, but never less
than 1,000 ft HAA. Round up to 100 ft increment. The visibility must be at least 3 SM.
CAUTION: Published circle-to-land minima provide as little as 300 ft of obstacle
clearance within the maneuvering area.

USING DA IN LIEU OF MDA
Operations Specification (Ops Spec) C073 authorizes the use of an MDA as a DA if the following
ball note is on the approach chart.
NOTE: Ops Spe C073 is not authorized for LOC approaches.
LANDING SPEEDS
Visual, Non-Precision, and Cat I Approaches:
The following method is used for wind additive corrections.
VAP = VREF+ 1/2 steady headwind component + gust factor
Minimum VAP = VREF + 5
Maximum VAP = VREF + 20
Cat II Approaches and/or Ice Accumulation:
The following method is used for wind additive corrections.
VAP = VREF + 1/2 steady headwind component + gust factor
Minimum VAP = VREF
Maximum VAP = VREF + 20
NOTE: ACARS calculates and adds only one half the steady headwind component to VREF,
not the gust factor. Perform the above calculation manually when gusty winds are present.
Consider using the FMS LANDING INIT page to determine one half the headwind component,
then add the gust factor.
NOTE: When the STALL PROT ICE SPEED advisory EICAS message is displayed landing
speeds must be adjusted according to the ice accretion table in the QRH or via the ACARS
SPIS prompt regardless of the icing condition on landing.

QRH Abnormal Procedures
Often the QRH will direct the pilot to set landing speeds based on a “new” VREF, e.g., VREF new
= VREF FULL+ abnormal speed correction. In this case, use the appropriate QRH and Landing
Speed table (without/with ice accumulation.) The minimum VAP = VREF NEW + 0 kt and the
maximum VAP = VREF NEW + 20 kt.
VAC must be the higher of:
• VREF NEW
• VAC from the Approach and Landing Speeds table (QRH)
• The go-around speed if instructed differently by the QRH
NOTE: Performance values take into account the threshold is crossed at 50 ft at VREF .
STABILIZED APPROACH CRITERIA
A stabilized approach is characterized by a constant angle, constant rate of descent approach
profile ending near the touchdown point. An approach that meets the parameters described
below provides the best opportunity for a stable approach. Pilots must meet the stabilized
approach criteria with the conditions listed by 1,000 ft HAT or a go-around is mandatory.
NOTE: For a circle-to-land maneuver, the aircraft must be stabilized and aligned with the
runway by 500 ft HAT.
Prior to accepting ATC requests for fast or short-approaches, consider the ability to meet these
criteria.
RNP AR approaches often have short final approach segments. In these cases, plan, and brief
to be configured prior to the FAF (e.g., BOI runways 28L and R).
NOTE: Some airports have an elevated risk due to unstable approaches. The 10-7 chart for
these airports has a red strip labeled “Safety Alert Airport.”
INSTRUMENT APPROACHES
By 1,500 ft HAT or the FAF, whichever occurs first:
• Landing gear down
• FLAPS 3
• Airspeed no greater than 180 kt
The PM will announce any deviations from the above criteria (e.g., “SPEED” or “FLAPS”).
The PF must take immediate correction action and state, “CORRECTING.”

Page: 04-12.7 of 52
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
By 1,000 ft HAT:
• On lateral and vertical profile.
• Airspeed within +15 and -5 kt of VAP
• Sink rate is no greater than 1,000 ft/min; if an approach requires a higher sink rate, a briefing
is required.
• The aircraft must be fully configured for landing and the BEFORE LANDING CHECKLIST
complete.
When a transient unstable condition or an EGPWS notification occurs at or below 1,000 ft
HAT and above 500 ft HAT, the PM states the deviation (e.g., “SPEED” or “SINKRATE”).
The PF states “CORRECTING” and makes the proper adjustment. When the deviation
from the above criteria is not immediately corrected, the aircraft is considered unstable,
and a missed approach is mandatory. The PM will command a go-around by announcing
“UNSTABLE, GO-AROUND.”
By 500 ft HAT:
• Airspeed established within the bug with adjustments for gust factor applied, if required.
• Thrust levers above idle.
Except for momentary airspeed and descent deviations, any unstable condition or any
EGPWS notification that occurs at or below 500 ft HAT requires a missed approach. The PM
will command a go-around by announcing “UNSTABLE, GO-AROUND.”
VISUAL APPROACHES
By 1,500 ft HAT:
• Landing gear down.
• FLAPS 3.
• Airspeed no greater than 180 kt.
The PM will announce any deviations from the above criteria (e.g., “SPEED” or “FLAPS”).
The PF must take immediate corrective action and state “CORRECTING.”
By 1,000 ft HAT:
• Lateral and vertical profile established within the standard service volumes for Visual
Glideslope Indicators (± 10° of the extended centerline and 4 NM (VASI) / 3.4 NM (PAPI)
from the runway threshold).
• Airspeed within +15 and -5 kt of VAP.
• On glide path.
• Sink rate is no greater than 1,000 ft/min; if an approach requires a high sink rate, a briefing is
required.
• The airplane must be fully configured for landing and the BEFORE LANDING CHECKLIST
complete.
