By 500 ft HAT:
• Airspeed established within the bug with adjustments for gust factor applied, if required.
• Thrust levers above idle.
When corrections are not immediately apparent or effective and/or GPWS notifications continue
at or below 500 ft HAT, a go-around is mandatory. The PM will command a go-around by
announcing “UNSTABLE, GO-AROUND.”
NOTE: For a VMC circle-to-land maneuver, the aircraft must be stabilized and aligned with
the runway by 500 ft HAT.
NOTE: EGPWS callouts are based on RA. The above criteria are based on HAT. Use caution
at airports where RA does not accurately indicate HAT altitude (e.g., ABQ and BIL).
CAUTION: Airspeed should be established at VREF with appropriate gust factors
applied by 50 ft HAT for all approaches. Do not fly below the glide path in an
attempt to decrease landing distance.
TEMPERATURE COMPENSATION
Barometric altimeters are calibrated to indicate true altitude only under ISA conditions of
temperature and sea level pressure. RNAV approaches (except LPV) use Baro-VNAV; therefore,
the vertical path is affected by temperature. When the temperature is colder than ISA, the
generated vertical path is shallower than published, placing the aircraft closer to the terrain.
When the temperature is warmer, the generated glidepath is steeper than published.
Temperature Compensation (TEMP COMP) corrects the aircraft vertical path back to the correct
true altitude and approach angle for non-standard temperatures.
Pilots must apply temperature compensation when:
1. The Airport Information page (10-7) has a “Cold Temperature Airport” blue bar, and the OAT
is colder than the temperature listed in the Temp Restriction column. In this case, apply
temperature compensation for all approaches on all segments for that airport.
2. The airport temperature is outside the operational temperature limitations in the Remarks
section on the instrument approach chart.
3. At foreign airports and the OAT is colder than -30°C. In this case, apply temperature
compensation for all approaches to that airport.
CAUTION: It is vitally important that the vertical path and minimum IFR altitudes are
compensated for below ISA temperatures correctly. For example, an incorrectly
entered sign for temperature in below ISA conditions would lower the vertical path
and indicated waypoint altitudes when, in fact, they should be raised.

Pilots are required to advise ATC when intending to apply temperature compensation. Provide
the amount of correction required on initial contact, or as soon as practical, for the intermediate
segment (initial intercept altitude) and the published missed approach holding point.
Example:
“SKYWEST 5435 REQUIRES 9,400 FT AT ADANE, 9,400 FT AT DRAKE FOR COLD
WEATHER OPERATIONS.”
When being vectored onto the approach, provide ATC with the corresponding compensated
segment altitude, if possible. This eliminates having to climb once on the approach.
NOTE: Radar vectors in Canada are temperature compensated. For more information on
temperature compensation, refer to the 27.2 NG FMS Pilot’s Guide. This can be found on
the EFB.
Enabling Temperature Compensation Option
Temperature compensation is an FMS option. If the pilot enters a temperature and a temperature
compensation option does not appear, follow the procedure to enable and configure in the NG
FMS Pilot Guide (ERJ 175 library) on the EFB.
APPROACH PROCEDURES
The initial approach procedure may be flown using LNAV (preferred) or HDG mode for lateral
tracking, VNAV, VGP, or FPA mode for altitude changes.
VNAV is the preferred mode for the initial approach when the FMS flight plan is programmed for
the intended arrival.
For non-precision approaches, using FMS as the navigation source, the APPR annunciator must
be displayed 2 NM before the FAF. It must be displayed for the remainder of the approach. This
is a positive cue the sensor configuration is correct and sensor integrity is within limits for the
approach. The approach annunciator is not displayed during localizer-based approaches since
the FMS, as the navigation source, is not authorized to be coupled during localizer approaches.
The DGRAD annunciator must be off throughout the approach. When DGRAD is displayed, the
FMS as the navigation source must not be used for the remainder of the approach. Continue the
approach using raw data or perform a missed approach procedure.
NOTE: The FMA must be verified after changing any flight mode to ensure the correct mode
has been selected, armed, and/or engaged as reflected by the behavior of the airplane.

VISUAL GLIDESLOPE INDICATORS (VGI)
The standard service volumes for VGI provide safe obstruction clearance within plus or minus
10 degrees of the extended centerline and 4 NM (VASI) / 3.4 NM (PAPI) from the runway
threshold. Upon arrival at a non-precision minimum (e.g., MDA) transition to the VASI / PAPI to
continue the landing.
CAUTION: For some runways, the VGI service volume is not standard. check the
Jeppesen Airport Information Chart (i.e., 10–9) notes as the VGI service volume may
be narrowed, shortened, or offset (e.g., PAPI unusable beyond 2.9 NM; Does not
provide obstacle clearance beyond 2.9 NM from threshold).
ACTIVATING APPROACH SPEEDS
The approach speeds schedule is activated either by FMS prompt or by extending flaps.
When the aircraft is within 30 miles of the destination airport, the ACT APP SPD prompt is
displayed on the active flight plan page at LSK 6R. At the crew’s discretion, selecting ACT APP
SPD commands FMS speeds according to the APPROACH SPEEDS page schedule.
VISUAL APPROACH
General
The flightcrew conducts visual approaches in accordance with: Operations Specifications C077,
Terminal Visual Flight Rules, Limitations, and Provisions and Flight Operations Manual SP 3100.
Maintain altitude awareness when performing visual approaches. Pilots are responsible for
terrain and obstacle clearance. Increase vigilance at night and in mountainous terrain.
Visual Approach
• Appropriate levels of automation should always be used.
• When conducting a visual approach in mountainous terrain or obstacles, terrain mode must
be displayed and monitored by at least one pilot.
• Arrival segments should be used when descending from the enroute environment to the
final approach segment.
• Must back up visual approach with an appropriate instrument approach procedure, when
available.
• Use all available resources (i.e., charts, terrain map) to ensure terrain clearance.
• The PF should brief their plan for descending from the arrival segment onto the final approach
segment to ensure a shared mental model is established. This briefing should include
minimum target altitudes at various phases of the arrival and approach in reference to the
terrain.

• Review airport 10-7 charts for Safety Alerts and additional information or restrictions. A visual
approach with one engine inoperative is conducted in the same manner as a visual approach
with all engines operative.
• To avoid the possibility of an unstabilized approach, crews conducting a visual approach to a
runway without vertical guidance should consider the following:
– Use FMS visual approach and vertical guidance.
– Use a 3:1 descent profile and VGSI.
Visual Approach at Night
When conducting a visual approach at night:
• The approach is conducted in accordance with Flight Operation Manual SP 3100 and requires
a full approach briefing.
• Consideration should be given to selecting approaches with vertical guidance.
• Terrain mode must be displayed and monitored by at least one pilot.
• An increased emphasis on briefing terrain and obstacles is necessary.
WARNING: CREWS SHOULD OPERATE IN ACCORDANCE WITH FLIGHT
OPERATIONS MANUAL SP 3100. USE CAUTION WHEN ACCEPTING A VISUAL
APPROACH IN AREAS OF RISING TERRAIN OR MARGINAL WEATHER. CAREFUL
CONSIDERATION MUST BE GIVEN TO THE AIRCRAFT’S ABILITY TO MANEUVER
SAFELY WITHIN THE TERMINAL AREA BASED ON THE ABOVE CONDITIONS. IF
UNABLE, CREWS MUST NOT ACCEPT A VISUAL APPROACH CLEARANCE AND
WILL COMPLY WITH THE PUBLISHED INSTRUMENT APPROACH PROCEDURES.
CALLOUTS
Instrument approach callouts apply until ATC issues a visual approach clearance and the PF
calls “RUNWAY IN SIGHT.” At this point, transition to VISUAL APPROACH ACTIONS AND
CALLOUTS.
GO-AROUND FROM A VISUAL APPROACH
Remain in VMC. Instrument missed approach procedures do not apply.
The crew should brief a go-around plan. Include go-around altitude and direction of flight (e.g.,
runway heading or left/right traffic).
Follow ATC instructions or fly the published visual go-around procedure when available. Absent
instructions, enter the traffic pattern at an altitude of not less than 1,500 ft AGL.
At uncontrolled airports, aircraft are expected to remain VMC and complete a landing as soon as
possible. If unable to land, contact ATC for further clearance.

CHARTED VISUAL FLIGHT PROCEDURES (CVFP)
CVFPs are established for environmental/noise considerations, and/or when necessary for the
safety and efficiency of air traffic operations. The approach charts depict prominent landmarks,
courses, and recommended altitudes to specific runways.
CVFPs are named by the use of a primary landmark, such as, “River Visual Rwy 13,” “Stadium
Visual Rwy 25,” etc.
CVFPs will be used only at airports with an operating control tower.
Most approach charts will depict some NAVAID information which is for supplemental
navigational guidance only.
Unless indicating a Class B airspace floor, all depicted altitudes are for noise abatement purposes
and are recommended only. Pilots are not prohibited from flying other than recommended
altitudes if operational requirements dictate.
CVFPs do not have missed approach procedures. The go-around procedure should be briefed.
If a go-around is necessary for any reason, aircraft will be issued instructions by the tower.
RNAV VISUAL FLIGHT PROCEDURE (RVFP)
The flightcrew conducts RVFPs in accordance with:
• Operations Specifications, OpSpec C081, Special Instrument and RNAV Visual Flight
Procedures.
RVFPs are charted visual approaches designed to promote flight path repeatability and stabilized
visual approaches.
RVFP naming convention is based on the type of procedure and runway served e.g., “RNAV
Visual Rwy 31”. The RVFP must be coded in the aircraft RNAV system database and retrievable
by name, i.e., “RNAV-V 31”. Pilots are not authorized to build these procedures manually
Crews must review the Attention All Users Page (AAUP) and brief the procedure similar to
any instrument approach procedure.
Pilots must request the RVFP on initial contact with the controlling agency, unless previously
coordinated, e.g., “SKYW 5675 REQUESTS THE RNAV VISUAL RWY THREE-ONE.”
Pilots must fly the published RVFP route and comply with charted mandatory altitudes and
speeds unless otherwise cleared by ATC.
By accepting an RVFP clearance, pilots also accept the requirements and responsibilities
associated with a visual approach clearance, e.g., visibility minimums and cloud clearances.

RVFP APPROACH SET UP AND ACTIONS
RVFP approaches are FMS-stored approaches and operate similar to RNAV approaches.
• Load the appropriate approach from the FMS.
• When cleared for the approach, set the altitude specified on the approach chart or traffic
pattern altitude (TPA).
• Arm APP.
• Verify GP is active.
• RVFPs do not have missed approach procedures. The go-around procedure should be
briefed. If a go-around is necessary aircraft are issued instructions by the tower.

PROCEDURE TURN
During a procedure turn a maximum speed of 200 kt, or published speed, should be observed
from first crossing the course reversal IAF through the procedure turn maneuver to ensure
containment within the obstruction clearance area.
Some procedures turns are specified by a procedure track. The turns must be flown as depicted
and monitored on the PFD. The ground speed and the airplane position relative to the procedure
turn must be monitored.

LOCALIZER PERFORMANCE WITH VERTICAL GUIDANCE
(LPV)
An “LPV approach” is, more precisely, an RNAV (GPS) approach to an LPV minimum. LPV
takes advantage of the improved accuracy of WAAS lateral and vertical guidance to provide an
approach; this is very similar to an ILS and is flown to a DA.
RNAV approaches with LPV minimums have an additional construct stored in the navigation
database called a final approach segment (FAS) data block. The FAS data block models the
geometry of an ILS final approach segment. The lateral and vertical guidance get more sensitive
as the aircraft gets closer to the runway.
When loading an RNAV approach into the flight plan and an LPV minimum is available, it is
automatically selected by default. The crew must verify the correct WAAS channel (e.g., 82211)
and approach ID (e.g., W25B) are loaded (LSK 4R) and match the approach plate.
NOTE: LP minima are not supported and cannot be performed. On such procedures, if an
LNAV minimum exists, VGP may be used down to LNAV minima only.
NOTE: LPV approach operations are prohibited when the flight director is off.
NOTE: An LPV CERTIFIED placard is not required to be installed in the aircraft for LPV
operations.

Within 2 NM of the final approach fix, LPV becomes active.
The flight crew must visually match this data with the information on the approach chart to verify
the proper FAS data block has loaded into the GPS.
When LPV is armed and the system is unable to perform an approach to LPV minimums, the
LPV NOT AVAIL caution EICAS message is displayed and the LPV annunciation is removed
from the display.
When LPV is active and a failure is detected, the NO LPV message is annunciated, and an
amber X is displayed on the vertical deviation scale. The flight crew must either be able to
proceed visually or execute the missed approach procedure.
LPV SCRATCH PAD MESSAGES
The system provides the following LPV alerts in the FMS MCDU scratchpad when the MSG
annunciator is illuminated on the PFD:
• PREDICT LPV UNAVAIL is displayed when the GPS 5-minute look-ahead horizontal integrity
limit (HIL) value is greater than the FAS data block horizontal alert limit (HAL) value when
5 minutes from the FAF.
• FMS / LPV MISCOMPARE is displayed when the FMS channel ID for the approach is different
from the GPS channel ID loaded for the approach. The channel ID for a WAAS approach is
displayed on the approach plate.
• LPF APPR LOAD FAIL is displayed if the FAS data block fails to load successfully.
NOTE: If any of these messages are present, the crew will discontinue the approach.

REQUIRED NAVIGATION PERFORMANCE AUTHORIZATION
REQUIRED (RNP AR)
RNP approaches include unique capabilities that require special aircraft and aircrew authorization
(refer to Operations Specifications C384). All RNP approaches have reduced lateral obstacle
evaluation areas and vertical obstacle clearance requirements. Some procedures may require
the capability to fly radius to fix (RF) legs. An RF leg is a constant radius segment beginning
and ending at a fix.
NOTE: After beginning an RNP approach, follow the charted procedure either to a landing or
execute a missed approach. Do not initiate or accept a visual approach.
SkyWest Airlines is authorized to conduct RNP AR IAP operations for foreign approaches
according to Ops Spec C384 Table 2.
Some countries have adopted the ICAO standard for naming RNAV approaches. In these
countries, “RNAV” approaches will be named RNP (e.g., MMMY RNP Rwy 11). Notice the
AR designation is not included in the title. When the AR designation is missing, treat these
approaches as an RNAV approach.
SPEED LIMITS FOR RF LEGS
IAF to FAF 250 kt
FAF to DA 165 kt (Cat D)
DA to MAHP* 265 kt
*MAHP = missed approach hold point

APPROACHES WITH AN RF LEG IN THE FINAL APPROACH SEGMENT
(FAS)
An RF leg in the FAS imposes a 165 kt speed limitation to DA.
• Use category D minima
• Configure FLAPS 5 for landing
• If it is necessary to discontinue the approach prior to DA use the Discontinue Procedure.
APPROACHES WITH NO RF LEG IN THE FAS
When there is no RF leg in the FAS there is no speed requirement to DA during a missed
approach. The Discontinue Procedure is not required, although it may be useful. Initiate a
Missed Approach Procedure, as necessary. Category C minima and FLAPS FULL landings
are authorized.
REQUIRED EQUIPMENT
The “Dark and Quiet Cockpit” concept aids in identifying an abnormal aircraft system. With
inoperative equipment prior to flight, refer to the MEL to determine whether the failed equipment
affects RNP operations. If an equipment failure occurs in flight, refer to the QRH Required
Equipment section.
APPROACH MINIMA
SkyWest’s lowest authorized RNP approach minimum is RNP 0.30 with missed approach 1.0. If
multiple RNP minima are available, select the appropriate minima when loading the approach in
the FMS.
Some instrument approach procedures have a ball note indicating a lower RNP 0.3 minimum if a
non-standard missed approach climb gradient can be met. Use these lower RNP 0.30 minima.
NOTE: RNP AR – Operations requiring missed approach RNP less than 1.0 are not authorized.
CAUTION: RNP approach procedures must be extracted from the database in their
entirety and must match the current charted procedure. The only permissible
modifications are changes to altitude and/or airspeed waypoint constraints on
initial, intermediate, or missed approach segments (e.g., to comply with an ATC
clearance/instruction).
APPROACH SET UP AND ACTIONS
• Verify the navigation database will be current for the duration of the flight. When an
installed navigational database is not current prior to the beginning of a flight, or when a
database will expire in flight, notify the dispatcher as this may affect flight planning. The
database and stored procedures may still be utilized provided the flight crew compares
the database fixes to the fixes/routes on the current published Jeppesen procedure and

verifies there are no discrepancies or changes. When discrepancies are noted, the affected
RNP procedure cannot be flown.
• Use the current local altimeter setting. Remote altimeter settings are not authorized. The
PM must perform an altimeter crosscheck at the FAF. Altimeters must be within 100 ft of
each other and 100 ft of the published FAF altitude (allowing for temperature compensation)
while on GP or a missed approach is required.
• TERRAIN must be displayed on at least one MFD.
• Autopilot usage is mandatory to begin the approach and should remain on to decision
altitude (DA) unless aligned with the runway and in visual conditions. When the autopilot
fails or is disconnected during the approach, discretion is allowed if it is deemed safe
to continue the approach.
• Do not accept a clearance to join an approach on a RF leg. Comply with all charted
airspeeds.
CAUTION: Maintain the course centerline at all times, even in visual conditions.
Initiate a missed approach when navigation guidance is misleading, or
course deviation is excessive. Upon the initiation of a missed approach, it is
imperative to verify LNAV activates on the FMA, and that the aircraft remains
on the course centerline. Comply with any segment airspeed limitations and
monitor the lateral and vertical deviations.
CONTINGENCY PLAN – LOSS OF NAVIGATION
When the ability to see or trust the FMS generated navigation course is compromised, it is
imperative to have a simple plan.
• Initially, maintain current heading or bank angle, if on an RF leg.
• Steer away from terrain.
• Ask ATC for heading and altitude or climb to MSA. Consider declaring an emergency.
• Consider use of ground-based navaids.
RECORDING PROCEDURES
At the completion of the flight, the captain will record the approach during closeout in FliteView. A
satisfactory approach is one completed as planned without any navigation or guidance system
anomalies. A missed approach directed by ATC or due to weather conditions is not considered
an unsatisfactory approach.
Reasons for unsatisfactory approaches include:
• NO RNP message, navigation position error, or loss of navigation signal.
• Excessive lateral or vertical deviation.
• EGPWS or terrain warning.
• Autopilot system disconnect.
• Navigation database errors, and/or
• Any other navigation or guidance system anomaly.
NOTE: Pilots should fly three RNP AR approaches every 90 days to maintain proficiency.

ILS CAT I PROCEDURES
Approach Setup
• Verify the localizer frequency for the intended runway is active on PFD or RADIO page.
• Verify using the on-side navigation source.
• Verify the final approach course is set.
• Set BARO on the RA/BARO Minimums Selector.
Initially, it is preferable to descend on the approach using VNAV. This ensures compliance
with altitude constraints. After passing the penultimate waypoint and prior to the FAF ensure
the approach is armed (APP). Before passing the FAF, the navigation source should be the
localizer (LOC) and glide slope (GS).
ILS CAT II PROCEDURES
CAT II approaches use the same procedures described in the ILS CAT I section with some
additional considerations. The captain is the PF for all CAT II approaches. The first officer will
act as the PM.
CAT II logic is automatically activated whenever the RA / BARO Minimums Selector knob is set
to the RA position. A green APPR 2 annunciator on the FMA indicates the correct setting and a
white or amber APPR 1 ONLY annunciator indicates an incorrect setting.
REQUIRED EQUIPMENT
The “Dark and Quiet Cockpit” concept aids in identifying an abnormal aircraft system. With
inoperative equipment prior to flight, refer to the MEL to determine whether the failed equipment
affects CAT II operations. If an equipment failure occurs in flight, refer to the QRH Required
Equipment section.

APPROACH MINIMA
Minimum RVR required to begin the approach
TDZ (required) MID ROLLOUT
Only TDZ RVR
available
1400 not available not available
At least two RVR
required
1200 600 300
• All available RVR reports are controlling. Operative RVR cannot be disregarded.
• TDZ RVR is required.
• Far End RVR is advisory only but may be substituted for inoperative Rollout RVR. When a
substitution is made, Far End RVR must report RVR 300 or greater.
• “RA NOT AUTH” minima are not authorized.
After passing the FAF, the crew may continue to DH if RVR drops below minimums. At DH,
the pilot must have required visual reference.
During the approach, the PF places one hand on the throttles guarding the TOGA button
and other hand on the control column near or adjacent to the autopilot disconnect button. At
“APPROACHING MINIMUMS” callout the PF visually transitions outside. The PM maintains
altitude awareness and ensures “MINIMUMS” is made.
LANDING SPEEDS
Landing speeds are calculated in accordance with the LANDING SPEEDS section of this
chapter using either:
• ACARS, QRH or
• Performance Data
LIMITATIONS
• Manual CAT II approaches are prohibited.
• Landing configuration must be FLAPS 5.
• Lowest authorized published RA setting is 80 ft.
• DH is limited to 100 ft above TDZ.
• CAT II operations are not authorized using INNER MARKER derived DH.
• Crosswind component on the landing runway is limited to 12 kt (AOM).
• CAT II operations with one engine inoperative are authorized.
NOTE: Autopilot minimum use height (MUH) is 50 ft.

WIND COMPONENT
There are no wind limitations for the AFCS when conducting a CAT II approach.
The crosswind component for the landing runway is 12 kt (AOM).
ABNORMAL PROCEDURES
Hardover or Slowover
If any unusual acceleration or motion is noticed on the airplane flight path, disengage the
autopilot, and perform a Missed Approach Procedure.
Autopilot Malfunction
Before the FAF:
• If the autopilot disengages or has to be disengaged, re-engage it.
• If the autopilot disengages again perform a Missed approach Procedure.
After the FAF:
• When the autopilot disengages or has to be disengaged, do not re-engage the autopilot.
Perform a Missed Approach procedure.
Display Warnings
Initiate a Missed Approach Procedure for any of the following:
• Below 800 RA, APPR 1 ONLY displayed on the FMA
• APPR 2 NOT AVAIL EICAS message
• RALT FAIL EICAS message
• Any comparator flag displayed on the PFD
• Below 500 ft RA, excessive localizer and/or GS deviation (amber)
• Anytime any required airborne or associated ground-based equipment becomes inoperative,
or the captain determines it is necessary
Radar Altimeter Test
To test the radar altimeter performing the following steps on the MCDU:
• Push the LSK 1L (MISC).
• Push the LSK 4R (TEST).
• On the TEST 2/2, push the LSK 3L (RAD ALT) and check the MCDU showing RAD ALT
test ON and the radar altitude indication showing 50 ft on PFDs 1 and 2.
• Push the LSK 4R (TEST) again to stop the test.

CAT II DOWNGRADE / UPGRADE PROCEDURES
Each aircraft certified for CAT II operations has a placard displayed in full view of the flight crew
stating: “CAT II Certified.”
Anytime the aircraft is removed from CAT II status, an “INOP” sticker is placed on the placard.
CAT II Downgrade
An aircraft is downgraded from CAT II status any time there is a failure of any required component
or when any component or system exceeds the interval for required check/functional test.
Whenever an aircraft is on downgraded status, OCC makes a notation in the MEL section of
the dispatch release indicating the aircraft is not to be used for CAT II operations. The notation
on the dispatch release remains until the aircraft is upgraded.
CAT II Upgrade
An aircraft which has been downgraded from CAT II status may be upgraded by having a
qualified technician perform the required calibration tests.
The following statement must be placed in the Aircraft Maintenance Log (AML) when an aircraft
is upgraded:
“This aircraft has been inspected in accordance with applicable standards and is released for
service for CAT II landing minimums.”
The mechanic removes the INOP sticker.
An in-flight functional check may be used to upgrade the aircraft to CAT II status. In this case, a
notation is made by Maintenance in the AML as follows:
“In-flight check of ________ system to CAT II standards is required. Check is to be accomplished
under CAT I. Notify Maintenance upon completion of check.
In addition, a notation in the Comments section of the flight release notifies flight deck crews that
an in-flight check of systems is required at the first opportunity. This check may be accomplished
by the flight deck crew during revenue flight, as long as the check is conducted during operations
with no less than CAT I operations.
After the check is accomplished and is acceptable, the captain notifies OCC and Maintenance.
Under the direction of Maintenance, the following is entered:
“This aircraft has been flight checked to CAT II standards, and is upgraded to CAT II status per
________ (name of Maintenance Controller), Maintenance Control.”
The captain then enters their signature after this entry in the corrective action block.
After the entry is made, the INOP sticker is removed from the CAT II placard. OCC removes
the downgrade notice from the flight release.

MEL PROCEDURES
In addition to standard operations, any MEL item affecting CAT II operations has a large block
“CAT II” logo included to flag Maintenance and flight deck crews that the item may cause the
aircraft to be downgraded from CAT II status if it is deferred. Part of the deferral process of each
item is to cover the CAT II placard in the aircraft with an “INOP” sticker per the above procedures.
The removal of this sticker and restoring to CAT II status is done per the above procedures
independent of and after clearing of the DMI item.
RECORDING PROCEDURES
At the completion of the flight, the captain will record the approach during closeout in FliteView. A
satisfactory approach is one completed as planned without any navigation or guidance system
anomalies. A missed approach directed by ATC or due to low visibility weather conditions is
not considered an unsatisfactory approach.
Reasons for unsatisfactory approaches include:
• Any required airborne or associated ground-based equipment becomes inoperative.
• The approach becomes unstabilized.
• The approach exceeded the CAT II limitations listed in this chapter (except for weather below
minimums).
• Any of the CAT II abnormal procedures are experienced.
• The autopilot malfunctions requiring a missed approach.
• Any display warnings during final approach listed in this chapter.
• An excessive deviation warning resulting in a missed approach.
• The crosswind component at touchdown is expected to be greater than 12 kt.
• Any other abnormality resulting in a missed approach.
NOTE: SkyWest recommends flight deck crews fly three practice CAT II approaches every
90 days.

NON-PRECISION PROCEDURES WITH VERTICAL GUIDANCE
The procedures presented herein are applicable to VOR, GPS (GNSS), and RNAV approaches.
All approaches may be flown using the FMS as a primary source of navigation.
When a final path descent angle is defined on a non-precision approach, the use of VGP is
recommended.
Fly the intermediate approach conventionally, using HDG or NAV mode. Start the deceleration
segment approaching the airport in radar vectors or using own navigation. Plan to have flaps
2 when on the heading to intercept the final approach track.
When cleared for the approach, and on an intercept heading of less than 90º of the inbound
track, arm the applicable mode. Once on the final inbound course, before FAF (approximately 2
NM), extend landing gear and select flaps 3. At the FAF, select landing flaps and reduce to VAP.
FMS AS PRIMARY SOURCE
The FMS may be used to provide primary guidance for the following non-precision instrument
approach types: GPS (GNSS), RNAV (non-GPS and GPS required types), VOR, VOR-DME,
RNAV Visual, and FMS Visual.
Using the FMS as the navigation source for guidance on the final approach segment of an
ILS, LOC, LDA, Visual, and Radar final approaches is prohibited. These procedures may be
retrieved from the navigation database and inserted into the flight plan but cannot be used
to provide primary guidance using the FMS.
FMS DATABASE
The approach procedure to be flown must be retrieved from the FMS database. A thorough
verification of all waypoints, tracks, distances, glide path angles, altitude constraints, and
threshold crossing altitudes must be verified and cross-checked during the approach briefings.
NOTE: For VOR approaches, manually tune the primary navaid frequency. Set the final
approach course using the cyan preview needle. Monitor the course deviation during the final
approach segment and remain within one dot of the centerline.
VECTORS TO FINAL
When on vectors to final approach, using the FMS as a primary source of navigation, the
following applies:
→
• When vectored for approach, sequence the approach from the appropriate fix considering
lateral and vertical constraints.
• Arm LNAV through the APP or NAV button, only when cleared to intercept the final approach
course.

VNAV APPROACH
When using FMS, the vertical guidance is VNAV. The possible VNAV modes, when flying a
non-precision approach, are VPATH and VGP.
The TEMP COMP feature compensates approach altitudes from the FMS database, according
to the destination temperature inserted on the landing page.
A compensated MDA (DA) is assigned on the TEMP COMP page and must be used as current
MDA (DA).
VPATH
With VPATH mode engaged, the altitude selector must be set to MDA for continuous VNAV
descent. In this mode, it is not possible to set the missed approach altitude before reaching the
MDA. It is necessary to be at VALT mode to set the missed approach altitude; otherwise, the
airplane stops the descent, changes the vertical mode from VPATH to VFLCH, and starts an
immediate climb to the missed approach altitude, adding thrust on the final approach phase.
VGP
Whenever the VGP mode is available, all non-precision approaches are flown in this mode.
Perform the approach conventionally (i.e., non-precision approach profile), but fly the VGP as
a glide path and configure the aircraft the same as an ILS approach. When using an MDA for
approach minimums, add 50 ft to the MDA.
On the heading to intercept the final approach track, pressing the APP button on the GP arms
VGP mode. When the preview function is not presented while flying in HDG mode, pressing
the APP button also arms the LNAV mode.
Intercepting the final approach descent path engages the VGP mode. From this point, the
altitude selector may be set to the missed approach altitude, when a go-around is to be initiated
from the glide path at DA, rather than at the MDA.
When a holding pattern is performed, VGP only engages after the FMS EXITING HOLD is active
and with the airplane on the inbound course of the FAF.
VGP permits a glide path capture when the airplane is above the desired GP, when the
vertical mode is ALT. This condition may cause excessive descent angles that may lead to an
unstabilized approach, specifically when the VGP occurs at or in close proximity to the FAF.
For all other vertical modes, VGP behaves like an ILS and captures the glide path, only within
about 100 ft above or below the glide path.

(8) This callout is not made during CAT II operations.
MALFUNCTIONS
The decision to continue an approach in the case of any aircraft malfunction requiring crew
action, evaluated to determine whether the safest course of action is to continue or go-around.
The decision to continue an approach in the case of any failure affecting the FD approach mode
capability or approach criteria is made prior to 1,000 ft HAT.
Following an autothrottle failure below 1,000 ft, the approach may be continued, when the
airspeed is under control and stabilized.

ENGINE FAILURE DURING FINAL APPROACH
When landing with flaps 5, the airplane is already in the required configuration for an OEI landing.
A speed adjustment is the only thing necessary to continue the approach.
Ensure the runway distance is adequate to safely complete the landing. Take into account the
speed additive and the fact only one thrust reverser is available.
In most cases, a 15 kt (No Ice) or 10 kt (Ice) additive is enough to comply with the speed
requirement for an OEI approach (VREF FULL + 20 kt).
When landing with flaps FULL, a reasonable increase in workload occurs, including MCDU
settings (heads down), flaps retraction, and thrust increments to reach the target speed.
Typically, a go-around is recommended, unless there is plenty of time to reconfigure the airplane,
or the safest course of action is to continue the approach in the same configuration.

LOCALIZER ONLY APPROACH
Use this procedure for the following approaches:
• Localizer only.
• LDA with no glide slope.
GENERAL
• Both pilots display and monitor a cyan preview needle (LOC raw data).
• Use LNAV lateral mode to track the magenta course for the entire approach.
• Use VNAV PTH vertical mode to descend to minimums.
APPROACH SETUP
NOTE: Line select the LOC approach, not ILS, from the FMS database. Localizer and ILS
approaches often differ inside the FAF.
• Verify using the correct localizer frequency for the intended runway.
• Verify using the on-side localizer source (e.g., CA – LOC 1; FO – LOC2).
• Verify the final approach course is set.
• Set the derived approach minimum using the BARO Minimums Selector. The derived
minimum is the charted MDA rounded up to 100 ft increment.
CLEARED FOR THE APPROACH
When cleared for the approach:
• Arm NAV.
• Verify VNAV is active.
• Set the derived minimum in the altitude selector.
The aircraft descends in PTH mode to the derived minimum.
CAUTION: The cyan localizer is the primary NAVAID. A missed approach is required
when the lateral deviation exceeds one dot during the final approach segment.
CAUTION: Do not arm APP. Doing so arms GS mode.

Date: 11 DEC 23
RUNWAY IN SIGHT
When the runway is in sight and in an adequate position to land, accomplish the following items
in the order listed:
• Disconnect the autopilot.
• Clear the PF’s flight director.
• Set the missed approach altitude.
Clear the flight director prior to setting the missed approach altitude.
A common error is setting the missed approach altitude while in PTH mode. This makes FLCH
active, advances the thrust levers to the TOGA position, and automatically sets the speed bug to
a faster speed leading to an unstable approach.
NO RUNWAY IN SIGHT
At minimums, if the runway is not in sight, allow altitude capture (i.e., ALT active). Then, set the
missed approach altitude and execute the Missed Approach Procedure.
FLY VISUAL SEGMENTS
Occasionally, a fly visual segment follows the missed approach point (MAP) (e.g., DCA LDA
Y Rwy 19). The fly visual segment is represented by a discontinuity in the FMS flight plan.
When passing the MAP, the discontinuity produces an FD LATERAL MODE OFF caution EICAS
message.
When landing, clear the flight director to remove the message.
When executing a missed approach procedure, pressing the TOGA button sequences the
missed approach and activates LNAV and eliminates the message.

ONE ENGINE INOPERATIVE NON-PRECISION APPROACH
In case of an engine failure, use the same procedure described in the non-precision approach
section of this chapter. Flaps 5 is used for landing and approach.
CIRCLE-TO-LAND APPROACH
GENERAL
Circle-to-land approaches are IFR approaches conducted in accordance with Operations
Specification C075, Category I IFR Landing Minimums – Circle-to-Land Approach Maneuver.
Circling may require maneuvers at low altitude, at low airspeed, and in marginal weather
conditions. Pilots must use sound judgement, have an in-depth knowledge of their capabilities,
and fully understand the aircraft performance. Factors such as weather, unique airport design,
and the aircraft position, altitude, and airspeed must all be considered for a successful circling
approach.
The captain is the PF for all circle-to-land approaches. The first officer will act as the PM.
Use the charted Category D minima, but never less than 1,000 ft ceiling and 3 sm visibility.
A circling-to-land approach consists of an instrument approach followed by a visual circling
maneuver.
Published circling minimum provide obstacle clearance (300 ft) when pilots remain within the
appropriate protection area. During the circle-to-land maneuver, the aircraft must remain clear of
clouds. Remain at an altitude not less than the charted circle-to-land MDA or 1,000 ft above
the airport (HAA), whichever is higher, until the aircraft is in position for a normal descent to
the landing runway.
WARNING: COMPLY WITH ALL CIRCLING RESTRICTIONS ON THE APPROACH
CHART AND THE AIRPORT INFORMATION PAGE (10–7). THE CAPTAIN MUST
BRIEF TERRAIN AND OBSTACLES IN THE VICINITY OF THE AIRPORT IN THE
ARRIVAL BRIEFING.
INSTRUMENT APPROACH
Use any approved instrument approach.
Approach Setup
• Set up the intended approach.
• Set the derived circling minimums with the BARO Minimums Selector rounded up to the
nearest 100 ft increment. The lowest derived circling minimum is the higher of the charted
Category D circle-to-land minimum, or 1,000 ft height above airport.
• Use ice speeds for landing.

Before Circling
• Fully configured.
• Before Landing checklist complete.
• Stabilized approach criteria must be met with the exception of:
– On lateral and vertical profile by 500 ft.
Cleared for the Approach
When cleared for the approach:
• Arm NAV. Do not arm APP.
• Verify LNAV is active.
• Verify VNAV is active.
• Set the derived minimum in the altitude selector. (Not less than the charted MDA or 1,000 ft
above the airport (HAA), whichever is higher, rounded up to the next 100 ft increment.)
The aircraft descends in PTH mode to the derived minimum.
At minimums, allow altitude capture (i.e., ALT active), set the missed approach altitude and
begin the visual circling maneuver.
Before beginning the circling maneuver, the aircraft must be fully configured for landing with the
Before Landing Checklist complete.
No Runway in Sight
At minimums, if the runway is not in sight, allow altitude capture (i.e., ALT active). Then, set the
missed approach altitude and execute the Missed Approach Procedure.
VISUAL CIRCLING MANEUVER
The circling maneuver is initiated only after visual contact is established with the landing runway.
Use of the autopilot and autothrottle is recommended to minimize workload.
Adjust aircraft heading and timing so the aircraft ground track does not exceed the obstruction
clearance distance from the runway at any time during the circling maneuver.

STANDARD AND EXPANDED CIRCLING APPROACH RADII
The standard circling maneuvering airspace radius for Category D (165 kt maximum) is 2.3 NM
and can be identified by the absence of the symbol (negative-C) on the symbol (negative-C)
on the circling line of minima.
In 2012, the FAA has modified the criteria for circling approach areas. These distances,
dependent on aircraft category, are also based on the circling altitude which accounts for the
true airspeed increase with altitude. The expanded circling maneuvering airspace radius for
Category D can be identified by the presence of the symbol on the circling line of minima
and is a function of altitude.
MISSED APPROACH
If a missed approach is required while circling, make a climbing turn in the shortest direction
toward the landing runway. Continue the turn until established on an intercept heading to the
missed approach course corresponding to the instrument approach procedure just flown. This
may result in a turn greater than 180 degrees to intercept the missed approach course. Maintain
the missed approach flap setting until close-in maneuvering is completed. Different patterns may
be required to become established on the prescribed missed approach course. This depends on
aircraft position at the time the missed approached is started. The following figures illustrate
the maneuvering that may be required. This ensures the aircraft remains within the circling and
missed approach obstruction clearance areas.

GO-AROUND - MISSED APPROACH
No approach is initiated, unless the prevailing conditions are understood, briefed, and a landing
is anticipated without undue risk. Philosophically, all approaches are treated as approaches
followed by a missed approach. A landing is treated as an alternate procedure. This mindset
depends on a good approach briefing, on the knowledge of the missed approach procedure, and
on proper programming of the FMS.
Sufficient visual cues must exist to continue the approach below DA (DH) or MDA. When visual
cues are lost, initiate a go-around immediately and fly the published missed approach procedure.
Although the PF is initially responsible for the go-around callout, the PM may also make this
callout when it is not made in a timely manner. The PF’s immediate response to this callout by
the PM is the execution of a go-around/missed approach.
Conduct a missed approach as follows:
• When a go-around is required at any time while maneuvering in the traffic pattern and visual
conditions cannot be maintained, perform a climbing turn toward the runway of intended
landing
• When over the airport and climbing, turn (in the shortest direction) to the missed approach fix
and proceed with the published procedure

• To initiate the go-around, press TOGA, ensure go-around thrust is set (TOGA position) or
manually apply go-around thrust. Verify FMA annunciations (GA, TRACK and GA). The
TRACK mode is activated when TOGA is pressed and disengaged when another lateral
mode becomes active. The selection of another vertical mode deactivates the GA mode. An
automatic go-around cannot be initiated after touchdown.
• The GA pitch mode initially commands a go-around attitude and then transitions to speed as
the rate of climb increases. The GA roll mode maintains existing ground track.
• Select flaps according to the table and rotate to an initial pitch of 8° nose up when flying
manually or follow the FD guidance and monitor the AP
SLAT / FLAPS LANDING SLAT / FLAPS GO-AROUND
5 2
FULL 4
• With a positive rate of climb, retract the landing gear and maintain a minimum of VREF + 20 kt
• At 1,000 ft AGL or obstacle clearance altitude, whichever is higher, accelerate to flap retraction
speed by selecting VNAV, or another vertical mode, and then VFS. As the airspeed increases,
retract flaps according to “F” bug and complete the After Takeoff Checklist.
• Maintain mode awareness by monitoring the FMA to determine the engaged pitch and roll
modes are the correct modes that are selected and are reflected by the airplane behavior
When MISSED APPR is activated, the missed approach procedure from the database is inserted
into the FMS flight plan whenever the GA mode is active and the airplane’s position is between 2
NM outside the final approach fix (FAF), and the missed approach point (MAP).
When MISSED APPR is selected, the APPR annunciator shuts off and the FMS transitions
from the approach mode to the terminal mode.
MISSED APPROACH FUEL
Planned missed approach fuel burn: 700 lbs
Planned missed approach fuel burn is derived using FOQA data and based on:
• Executing one missed approach, and
• Completing a subsequent approach using average actual distances
NOTE: FOQA data shows average miles flown while conducting a missed and subsequent
approach procedure is 39.6. Distances will vary.

DISCONTINUED OR EARLY MISSED APPROACH / PRM
BREAKOUT
In the event an approach is discontinued early, pressing the TOGA button may not be the best
course of action and may not give the desired results. Generally, it commands TOGA thrust,
accelerates the airplane to VREF + 20 kt, and sequences the missed approach procedure in the
FMS (if the TOGA button is pressed too early, it does not sequence the FMS).
This procedure and flow is used to help manage the flight path during an early missed approach
/ PRM breakout. The flow begins by terminating the approach (APP), moves from left to right
across the guidance panel (lateral mode, airspeed, vertical mode), sequences the FMS, and
finally configures the aircraft.
CAUTION: As with any maneuver, flight path management is critical. Verify the
desired modes are displayed on the PFD, FMA, and FMS.
To discontinue an approach without pressing the TOGA button, use the callout “DISCONTINUE”
and perform the following actions:
1. Deselect APP, if armed. Verify/select desired lateral mode displayed on the FMA.
2. Set appropriate airspeed.
3. Set appropriate altitude and select FLCH.
4. Sequence the missed approach using the MISSED APPR prompt on FMS FLT PLAN page.
5. Select go-around flaps.
6. Select gear up.
7. Call, “CLIMB SEQUENCE, AFTER TAKEOFF CHECKLIST.”
NOTE: For RNP AR approaches with RF legs in the final approach segment, manually set 165
kt (Category D). This is faster than green dot when configured FLAPS 2.
With slight variations, this flow can be used for PRM breakout maneuvers as follows:
• Push HDG and set the heading bug
• Set an appropriate airspeed
• Set an appropriate altitude and select FLCH
• Select go-around flaps
• Select landing gear up
• Call “CLIMB SEQUENCE, AFTER TAKEOFF CHECKLIST.”

ONE ENGINE INOPERATIVE GO-AROUND
The one engine inoperative go-around procedure is similar to the AEO procedure.
Rotation must be done at a slower rate, slightly less than on a normal go-around. Maintain
neutral aileron input and use rudder and yaw trim to keep wings level, as high ailerons input
raises spoilers and increases drag.
Verify the GA pitch and roll modes are annunciated on the FMA and ADI. At a positive rate of
climb, retract landing gear. The FD commands a pitch attitude that maintains the selected speed.
The GA roll mode maintains a ground track.
Select HDG and BANK mode at or above 400 ft AGL.
Advise ATC and fly the published missed approach procedure or fly the runway specific Engine
Failure - Missed Approach procedure.
At VFS, with landing gear and flaps retracted, select thrust CON on the MCDU TRS page and
verify continuous thrust (CON) is displayed on the EICAS.

ENGINE INOPERATIVE GO-AROUND (cont'd.)
ACTIONS AND CALLOUTS
PF PM
Climbing to safe
altitude
• Climb at VFS to a safe altitude
• Captain assigns PF and PM
duties based on the Threat and
Error Application Model (TEAM)
recommendations
• Advise ATC
NOTE - This profile is predicated on utilizing FMS speeds.
(1) PF states “MISSED APPROACH” when conducting an instrument approach or
“GO-AROUND” when conducting a visual approach.
NO SLAT / FLAPS CONFIGURATION
The following items are valid for all abnormal slat/flaps configuration approaches and landings:
• Use of autopilot is recommended
• Check landing distance (select the longest runway available)
• Limit bank angle to 20° maximum when maneuvering for landing
• Establish a long final approach at recommended speed
• Attitude and speed are higher than normal
• Establish landing configuration early
• Plan touchdown at the 1,000 ft touchdown zone marker
• Flare just enough to reduce the rate of descent; do not float
• Over threshold, set thrust levers to IDLE
• After nosewheel touchdown, apply forward control column, reverse thrust, and brake without
delay

EGPWS
False EGPWS warnings may be generated when operating in the area of a new runway surface.
Do not ignore any EGPWS warning until verified it is erroneous. When a warning is verified to
be erroneous, no corrective action needs be taken, as long as the runway is in sight and the
aircraft is stabilized. A maintenance log write-up is not required, when an EGPWS warning is
received and verified as erroneous.
Unless verified to be erroneous, EGPWS aural warnings must be corrected as follows:
Enroute or initial approach
• Climb immediately until warning is canceled and terrain separation is achieved.
When corrections are not immediately apparent or effective, and/or warnings continue,
perform an EGPWS Escape Maneuver.
Final approach (VMC)
• Correct flight path to cancel warning or perform an EGPWS / CFIT Escape Maneuver.
When corrections are not immediately apparent or effective, and/or warnings continue,
perform an EGPWS Escape Maneuver.
Final approach (IMC)
• Above 500 ft AGL: Correct flight path to cancel warning, or perform an EGPWS / CFIT Escape
Maneuver
When corrections are not immediately apparent or effective, and/or warnings continue,
perform an EGPWS Escape Maneuver.
• Below 500 ft AGL: Immediately initiate an EGPWS / CFIT Escape Maneuver

EGPWS / CFIT ESCAPE MANEUVER
ACTIONS AND CALLOUTS
PF PM
Upon receiving
EPGWS Warning
• Disengage the autopilot
• Simultaneously advance thrust
levers to MAX position and set
pitch 20° to maintain pitch on PLI,
respecting stick shaker and buffet
• Retract spoilers (when required)
• Maintain present airplane
configuration (gear/flaps) until
terrain separation is achieved
• Climb to the MSA or MORA
• Clear of terrain, resume level flight
• Retract gear and flaps (when
required)
• Engage autopilot
• Resume normal speed
• Verify all required actions have
been performed
• Communicate with ATC
• Turn FSTN BELTS ON
• Scan for visual reference
• Call out radar altimeter
indications
• Read MFD terrain indications to
check obstacle height, guides
for the best course of action,
and indicates when obstacle
has been cleared
UPSETS
Refer to Chapter 05 EMERGENCY / ABNORMAL PROCEDURES, UPSETS.

BEFORE LANDING EXPANDED CHECKLIST
FLIGHT ATTENDANTS ..........................................................................................NOTIFIED PM
The PM pushes the Attendant Call button before extending the landing gear. This notifies
the FAs the landing is imminent as outlined in the Introduction of this SOPM.
LANDING GEAR .......................................................................................... DOWN 3 GREEN PF
PM selects the Landing Gear Lever down.
FLAPS................................................................................................................................ (__) PM
The PM selects Slat/Flaps Control Lever as directed by the PF. After final flap selection, the
PF calls for the Before Landing Checklist.
When possible, extend flaps at the green dot to optimize flap life and fuel consumption. The
green dot (GD) provides the minimum speed for the current configuration.
NOTE: In case of loss of IAS, loss of slat/flaps position, or position disagreement, the GD
is removed and the QRH Flaps Maneuvering Speeds table should be used for flaps
extension. During final approach, with the airplane configured for landing, VAP and VREF
can be lower than GD.

GENERAL
Monitor the final approach path using all references available and plan to touchdown in the
runway touchdown zone. As the airplane approaches the touchdown point, initiate the flare
approximately 20 to 30 ft (normally a 2° to 3° pitch change is enough for the flare), by reducing
the rate of descent and slowly reducing thrust levers to idle (thrust is at idle when the airplane
touches down). Do not allow the airplane to float in ground effect as this increases landing
distance and risks a tail strike.
NOTE: Performance values calculated by the CAFM take into account the threshold is crossed
at the screen height at VREF or VREF NEW, according to the entered reference speed.
CAUTION: Efforts to achieve a softer than normal landing can cause the aircraft to
delay or inhibit weight on wheels activation, ground lift dumping, braking, and thrust
reverser deployment. Using forward pressure on the yoke to force a floating aircraft
to land can allow the nose wheel to touch first and can contribute to this problem.
After the main wheels touchdown, allow autobrake to engage or apply manual braking as
required for the runway condition and length available, while easing the nosewheel onto the
runway. Use reverse thrust only as necessary and verify spoiler actuation. Autobrake is disarmed
by gradually pressing the brake pedals.
WARNING: AIRCRAFT AERODYNAMIC BRAKING IS NOT APPROVED AND IS NOT
USED DURING LANDING.
When using reverse thrust, apply consistent with runway conditions and modulate as required.
At 80 kt, smoothly reduce reverse thrust to MIN REV by 60 kt and idle thrust by 30 kt.
NOTE: Maximum reverse thrust is used when landing on wet, slippery, and contaminated
runways.
Thrust reversers are more effective at high speeds. The use of reverse below 60 kt increases the
chances of foreign object ingestion. The thrust reversers may be used until the airplane comes
to a complete stop, only as necessary to provide full stopping authority.
Rudder control is effective to approximately 60 kt. Rudder pedal steering is sufficient for
maintaining directional control during the rollout. Do not use the nosewheel steering tiller until
reaching taxi speed.
WARNING: THIS RECORD CONTAINS SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION THAT IS CONTROLLED UNDER 49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520. NO PART OF THIS RECORD MAY BE DISCLOSED TO PERSONS WITHOUT A "NEED TO KNOW", AS DEFINED IN

LANDING ROLLOUT ACTIONS AND CALLOUTS
LANDING ROLLOUT
ACTIONS AND CALLOUTS
PF PM
After main wheels
touchdown
• Verify autobrake or apply
manual braking, as required
• Use thrust reverse, as required
(1)
• Reduce thrust levers to MIN
REV, when thrust reversers
were used
• At 30 kt, set thrust levers to
IDLE (forward thrust), when
being used
• Verify ground spoiler
deployment
• Verify thrust reverser
deployment, when used :
“REVERSE, 2 GREEN”
• At 80 kt :
“80 KNOTS”
(1) When operating on wet/slippery/contaminated runway, apply MAX REV thrust.
BRAKE USAGE
AUTOBRAKE
The use of the autobrake system does not relieve the pilot of the responsibility to achieve a safe
stop within the available runway length, by using the brake pedals.
With autobrake set, verify the normal operation of the braking system and proper deceleration of
the airplane upon touchdown.
Press the brake pedals to disengage and use the manual braking as necessary to reach a
safe taxi speed.
When landing on a runway with degraded braking conditions, allow autobrake to remain engaged
until reaching a safe taxi speed.
CAUTION: During the landing roll, when deceleration is not suitable for the stopping
distance, manual braking must be used.

Page: 04-13.7 of 14
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
Avoid the tendency to increase braking when taking over manually and avoid excessive braking
to make a convenient turnoff.
Be alert for autobrake disengagement indications during the landing roll. When heavy braking
is required to stop the airplane, decelerate straight ahead, until reaching a normal taxi speed,
prior to initiating any turns.
MANUAL BRAKE
The following actions provides optimum manual braking for all runway conditions:
• Pilot seat and rudder pedals are adjusted to make it possible to apply maximum braking
with full rudder deflection.
• Apply the brakes with no delay after the main landing gear wheels have touched down. Move
directly to a single firm and steady brake application, holding pedal pressure until decelerated
to taxi speed.
• Do not pump the brakes.
• When the landing weight is limited by runway length, full reverse and brakes are used
simultaneously to stop the airplane.
EMERGENCY / PARKING BRAKE
Some emergency and abnormal procedures require the use of the Emergency/Parking Brake to
stop the airplane during landing. When this happens, the following protections are not available:
• Locked Wheel Protection,
• Antiskid Protection, and
• Touchdown Protection.
At high speeds, apply the Emergency/Parking Brake handle to stop the airplane, monitor the
Emergency/Parking Brake light, and maintain steady pressure. The brake system provides
sufficient pressure to start braking. As speed decreases, the required handle deflection increases
to maintain continuous braking.
If a tire bursts while applying the Emergency/Parking Brake, do not release the
Emergency/Parking Brake handle until the airplane stops.
→
LANDING ON WET, SLIPPERY, OR CONTAMINATED RUNWAYS
To minimize stopping distance on a wet, slippery, or contaminated runway (RCC 5 or less):
• Use autobrake setting MED
• Make a firm touchdown. Such technique avoids hydroplaning on wet runways and reduces the
strength of any ice bond that, during flight, might be formed on brake and wheel assemblies.
• Apply maximum reverse thrust. Do not use asymmetric reverse thrust on slippery or icy
runways.

Date: 11 DEC 23
• Apply firm and symmetrical brake pedal pressure; do not pump the brakes.
• If the airplane loses friction, do not apply the Emergency/Parking Brake, as it inhibits anti-skid
protection.
• Maintain runway centerline with rudder, while maintaining firm and symmetrical braking.
• Landing on a wet, slippery, or contaminated runway with tailwind is not recommended.
• Consider using Maximum Performance Landing techniques.
MAXIMUM PERFORMANCE LANDING
To minimize landing distance, consider the following techniques:
• Use FULL Flaps
• Cross the threshold at screen height of 50 ft and VREF
• Avoid extended flare
• Apply maximum thrust reverse. When necessary, the thrust reversers may be used until the
airplane comes to a complete stop.
• Immediately after the main landing gear wheels have touched down, apply firm and steady
maximum manual brakes and hold pedal pressure until the airplane decelerates to a safe taxi
speed within the runway
• Lower nosewheel immediately
NOTE: The same technique could be used for Flaps 5 configuration, but the landing
distance will increase accordingly.
CROSSWIND LANDING
The following method is used to land in a crosswind:
• Crab the airplane on final approach, and just prior to touchdown, apply downwind rudder to
align the airplane with runway centerline simultaneously with aileron control into the wind.
CAUTION: Wing tip or engine nacelle strike occurs when bank exceeds 18° at
touchdown (16° with gear struts compressed).

OPERATIONAL FACTORS AFFECTING LANDING DISTANCE
The required landing distance for dispatch is calculated considering the landing is performed
with the airplane crossing the runway threshold at the screen height of 50 ft and at VREF using
maximum manual braking.
The following operational factors, among others, have an effect on the airplane landing distance:
• Unstabilized approach
• Crossing runway threshold with airspeed above VREF
• Crossing runway threshold above the screen height
• Extended flare, touching down on the runway beyond the touchdown zone
• Improper application of the available deceleration devices (brakes, spoilers, and thrust
reverse)
If the airplane crosses the threshold with 10 kt above VREF, the landing distance increases
approximately 20%. Crossing the threshold at 100 ft, instead of the normal screen height,
increases the landing distance by approximately 35%. Extending the flare by 3 seconds
increases the landing distance by approximately 25%.
Combining all these factors; an unstabilized approach with the airplane crossing the threshold at
100 ft, 10 kt above VREF, and with 3 seconds of extended flare, the landing distance increases by
approximately 80%. In this situation, the distance necessary to stop the airplane exceeds the
operational margins provided by the dispatch-required landing distance.
WINDSHEAR
The most important way to deal with windshear is to avoid areas of known or potential occurrence.
Severe windshear may be defined as a rapid change in wind direction and/or velocity resulting in
airspeed changes greater than 15 kt or vertical speed changes greater than 500 ft/min.
Whenever windshear is suspected during landing, the following precautions are taken:
• During approach, maintain VAP with the applicable wind additive correction
• Select Flaps 5, unless limited by other landing performance considerations
• When possible, plan to land on the longest runway available with the lowest possibility of a
windshear encounter
• Monitor airspeed trends during the approach. With the first evidence of windshear, initiate a
go-around. When necessary, perform the windshear escape maneuver procedure.
• Develop an awareness of normal airspeed, attitude, and vertical speed. Closely monitor the
vertical flight path instruments and call out any deviations.

WINDSHEAR ESCAPE MANEUVER
The windshear escape maneuver is performed whenever the following happens:
• A warning windshear is annunciated during approach.
PFD: WSHEAR (red)
Voice message: “WINDSHEAR, WINDSHEAR, WINDSHEAR.”
• A caution windshear is annunciated during approach and the captain decides to perform
the windshear recovery technique
PFD: WSHEAR (amber)
Voice message: “CAUTION WINDSHEAR.”
• Whenever the captain decides to perform the recovery techniques due to the presence of
windshear cues without EGPWS announcement
Windshear escape maneuver due to EGPWS announcement
When a windshear warning is encountered:
• Advance thrust levers to MAX and press the TOGA button.
• Follow flight director guidance.
• Do not change flaps or landing gear configuration until out of the windshear condition and
terrain clearance assured.
• Reduce thrust to climb power, retract landing gear and slats/flaps when the airplane is out of
the windshear condition.
NOTE: PM states “CLIMBING” or “DESCENDING” based upon VSI indication until the
aircraft has returned to a safe airspeed and altitude.
When the Caution/Warning WSHEAR indicator on the PFD disappears, the PM states
“WINDSHEAR WARNING GONE” or “WINDSHEAR CAUTION GONE.”
When the radar altimeter indicates less than 1,000 ft AGL and the airplane is descending, the
PM calls “DESCENDING (___)” based upon the RA value observed.
For example:
“DESCENDING 700,” “DESCENDING 600.”
Windshear escape maneuver without EGPWS announcement
Disengage autopilot, advance thrust levers to maximum thrust, and maintain a pitch angle 20° or
PLI, whichever is lower. When advancing the thrust levers, press either GA switch. Maintain
the actual aircraft configuration (landing gear and flaps) until 1,500 ft AGL and terrain clearance
is assured.

SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
LANDING
Page: 04-13.11 of 14
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
REJECTED LANDING
The rejected landing procedure is similar to the go-around procedure. The spoilers retract
and autobrake disarms as the thrust levers are advanced for a rejected landing, initiated after
touchdown.
Attention must be given to the intended flight path, as the published missed approach procedure
may not be valid after passing the published missed approach point.
After reverse thrust is initiated, a full stop landing must be made, due to the time the reverser
requires to transition from reverse to forward thrust and the possibility it will not stow back into
the forward thrust position.
If the airspeed has dropped below VREF, initiate the go-around procedure, and retract flaps only
when the airspeed becomes greater than VREF.
BOUNCED LANDING
A bounced landing may occur with one or a combination of the following factors:
• Windshear
• Low-level turbulence
• Excessive rate of descent
• Excessive airspeed
• Thrust not at idle on touchdown
• Incorrect flare technique
Do not apply stabilizer trim during the flare.
Recovery from Light Bounce
When a light bounce occurs, maintain, or re-establish a normal landing attitude. Increasing pitch
can lead to a tail strike. Use thrust, as required, to soften the subsequent touchdown. Beware
of increased landing distance. It may be difficult to evaluate the landing distance remaining
along with the airplane energy.
WARNING: 10 DEGREES PITCH UP ON TOUCHDOWN WILL LIKELY RESULT IN A
TAIL STRIKE.
Recovery from a Severe Bounce
When a more severe bounce occurs, initiate the Rejected/Bounced Landing procedure. Do
not attempt to land.
Press the TOGA button and advance thrust levers to TO/GA. Hold the flare attitude until the
engines spool up. Reset stabilizer trim, then follow normal go-around procedures.
CAUTION: If the airspeed has dropped below VREF, retract flaps only after airspeed
becomes greater than VREF.

NOTE - Callouts are shown in bold text.
(1) PF states “MISSED APPROACH” when conducting an instrument approach or
“GO-AROUND” when conducting a visual approach.
TAIL STRIKE DURING LANDING
Tail strikes occur more frequently during landings than takeoffs by a factor of two to one. The
mistakes most commonly made are:
• Approach at improper pitch, speed, thrust, and glide path
• Prolonged flare
• Beginning flare too high
• Improper crosswind correction during flare
Of these commonly made mistakes, flaring the aircraft too high above the runway has the greatest
potential for a tail strike. When the flare begins too high, the airspeed can decrease below VREF,
creating the tendency to continue increasing pitch in an effort to arrest the excessive sink rate.
The correct action is to immediately lower the pitch attitude and fly the aircraft to the runway
before the airspeed dissipates any further. This corrective action prevents a tail strike.
OVERWEIGHT LANDING
Situations may require a decision to land overweight. Overweight landings may be safely
accomplished by using normal landing procedures and techniques. There are no adverse
handling characteristics associated with an overweight landing.
The decision to land overweight is a function of several factors:
• Runway and performance landing weight limits
• Approach weather and runway conditions
• Tire speed limit

SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
LANDING
Page: 04-13.14 of 14
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
• Status of aircraft (i.e., anti-skid, brakes, spoilers)
• Time and altitude needed to burn off fuel versus weather, traffic, and/or need to land
When making the decision to land overweight, select the longest available runway, when
feasible. Slope and wind effect are taken into account. Where possible, avoid landing with a
tailwind on runways with negative slope or runways with less than normal braking conditions.
Wind additives may be impacted by flap speeds. The final approach maximum wind correction
may be limited by the flap maneuvering speed.
Configure the aircraft early to have more time for the higher approach speeds. Do not carry
excess airspeed on final. Brake energy is not exceeded for flaps 5 or FULL landing at all gross
weights. Give special attention when landing on wet or slippery runways. Brake energy limits
may be exceeded, at speeds associated with non-routine procedures requiring flaps 5 or less.
The maximum rate of descent at touchdown is 300 fpm. The captain makes the landing, and
the first officer monitors the rate of descent. Make a smooth landing; don’t allow the aircraft to
float. Use maximum reverse thrust down to 60 kt. When the stopping capability is in doubt, use
reverse thrust until certain the aircraft stops within the runway limits. Autobrake may be used.
Consider a go-around when a long landing is likely to occur.
Any overweight landing requires a report to Maintenance in accordance with the maintenance
reporting procedures outlined in Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 06 Airworthiness.
This report and write-up must include the actual gross weight and descent rate at touchdown.
NOTE: Monitor brake temperature after landing and alert the ground crew when it becomes
too high.
NOTE: It is not necessary to declare an emergency, unless it is decided the condition warrants
such an action.

SINGLE ENGINE SHUTDOWN AFTER LANDING (FO)
Run the engines at IDLE thrust for two minutes to allow for engine thermal stabilization before
shutting down an engine. Time spent taxiing counts towards this requirement.
• Thrust Lever (as appropriate) . ........................................................................................... IDLE
• START / STOP Selector (as appropriate) . ....................................................................... STOP
NOTE: Normally the right engine is shutdown for single engine taxi. When the PIC elects to
shutdown the left engine, SYS 1 ELEC PUMP knob must be turned ON before shutting the
engine down. During the Shutdown Checklist, turn the SYS 1 ELEC PUMP selector knob
to AUTO.
CAUTION: Electrical power interruptions during Shutdown combined with APU start
may lead to momentary loss of steering and/or brakes.
To prevent power interruptions when APU is required:
• If APU is started before the engine shutdown, wait 10 seconds after APU parameters are
stabilized before shutting down the engine.
• If the engine is shutdown before starting the APU, wait 10 seconds after the engine shutdown
is commanded before starting the APU.

The Shutdown Checklist is only accomplished when either APU or external AC power has
been established. The two-engine shutdown flow is not performed until the parking brake is
set. Unless otherwise required, the flight deck door must remain locked until completion of the
Shutdown Checklist.
The emergency/parking brake must be set when parking at the gate. When on a quick turn
where brake temperatures are an issue, the parking brake may be released after chocks are
set, provided the aircraft remains powered.
Avoid leaving the parking brake set during long periods in cold weather, (e.g., an RON). This
decreases the chance of having brake stacks frozen together.
When on a through flight, quick turn, or crew change, the aircraft may remain powered, and the
Leaving the Airplane Checklist need not be completed. When leaving the aircraft unattended, or
an extended ground time is anticipated, complete the Leaving the Airplane Checklist.
NOTE: With the parking brake ON and waiting for GPU / APU power, the FSTN BELTS and
STERILE signs may be turned OFF.

• Engines must be operated at idle for a cool down period of at least 2 minutes, including
taxi, prior to shutdown.
EMERGENCY / PARKING BRAKE ..........................................................................................ON
• Set the Emergency/Parking Brake after the airplane has stopped. Verify the brake
temperature. When close to the cautionary range, verify the chocks are in and release
the Emergency/Parking Brake to reduce the brake cooling time.
NOTE: To prevent electrical power interruptions, it is recommended to wait 10 seconds
after the GPU button is pushed in or after APU is stabilized before shutting down one
of the engines. When one of the engines is shut down prior to using the APU, wait
10 seconds until the electrical system has stabilized on the remaining IDG before
starting the APU.
ELECTRICAL ........................................................................................................ ON GPU / APU
• When APU GEN is not available, an AC GPU should be plugged in. Check the AVAIL
light illuminated before pushing in the GPU button.
ENGINES ................................................................................................................................OFF
• The engines do not shut down with START / STOP Selectors unless the thrust levers are
first moved to IDLE. When STOP is selected before the Thrust Lever is retarded to IDLE,
momentarily cycle the START / STOP Selector to RUN and back to STOP.

INADVERTENT PARTIAL OR FULL SLIDE DEPLOYMENT
When a door handle is raised using the main handle while the door is armed and before escape
slide deployment, stop and step away from the door and adhere to the following:
• Ensure all personnel remain clear of the door.
• Ensure the door is not used for egress/deplaning.
• Immediately notify station operations of the door/slide status and to keep all ground personnel
clear of the door area.
• Arrange an alternate means of passenger and crew deplaning with station operations
(preferably L2).
• Notify and coordinate with flight attendants regarding the deplaning location and process.
• Notify Maintenance and request maintenance assistance to correct the door and/or slide
status. A maintenance write-up is required in accordance with maintenance reporting
procedures outlined in Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 06 Airworthiness.

POST FLIGHT
The PIC ensures the aircraft is properly secured, and all post flight duties complete. A post
flight external inspection is required.
NOTE: On international arrivals from non-precleared cities, local U.S. Customs requirements
may preclude the completion of a post flight external inspection.
A post flight external inspection may be waived under the following conditions:
• Both pilots must authenticate and close out the flight using the Close Out Flight button on the
FliteView app. Refer to FliteView User Guide, Flight Close Out.
• The outbound crew must be at the aircraft prior to the inbound crew commencing the Leaving
the Airplane Checklist
• The aircraft must remain powered
• The outbound crew must perform all preflight duties per SOPM
• The inbound crew is responsible for resolving all known discrepancies prior to leaving the
aircraft

EMERGENCY / ABNORMAL PROCEDURES
Page: 05-01.1 of 24
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
INTRODUCTION
This section describes the standard procedures used in response to emergency/abnormal
situations.
EMERGENCY AND ABNORMAL PROCEDURES
During emergencies or other abnormal situations, it is important to stay calm and be accurate in
your actions; fly the airplane first, then deal with the emergency.
Flight deck crewmembers must maintain situational awareness and ensure proper crew
coordination, communication, and judgment are utilized. The PF devotes total awareness to
the control and navigation of the airplane and uses automation, as appropriate, to help reduce
workload.
Procedures are written assuming oxygen masks are donned, and communications established
between crewmembers whenever oxygen deficiency or contamination is suspected.
As conditions permit, it is recommended to delay QRC or memory item procedures until the
aircraft has reached acceleration altitude, flaps retracted, and established in a stable climb at
VFS. Safety and terrain clearance is always the primary concern.
MINIMUM / EMERGENCY FUEL
Minimum and emergency fuel values are not related to FAR dispatch fuel requirements. Values
are calculated at maximum landing weight for ease of memory and standardization.
MINIMUM / EMERGENCY FUEL TABLE
MINIMUM 2,250 LB
EMERGENCY 1,500 LB
NOTE: It is not a violation of Company policy or FARs to land below the minimum/emergency
fuel standards.
Minimum Fuel
Minimum fuel provides 45 minutes of fuel endurance based upon 1,500 ft AFE / Green Dot /
Clean.
Declare minimum fuel when the estimated fuel remaining on touchdown, at the airport of
intended landing, is less than 45 minutes fuel endurance or as specified in “Minimum” column
of the Minimum/Emergency Fuel Table, when further deviation from the planned route of flight
could result in an emergency fuel situation.
WARNING: THIS RECORD CONTAINS SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION THAT IS CONTROLLED UNDER 49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520. NO PART OF THIS RECORD MAY BE DISCLOSED TO PERSONS WITHOUT A "NEED TO KNOW", AS DEFINED IN
49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520, EXCEPT WITH THE WRITTEN PERMISSION OF THE ADMINISTRATOR OF TRANSPORTATION SECURITY ADMINISTRATION OR THE SECRETARY OF TRANSPORTATION. UNAUTHORIZED RELEASE MAY RESULT IN CIVIL
