Submitted: 07 AUG 23
SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
EMERGENCY / ABNORMAL PROCEDURES
Page: 05-01.2 of 24
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
Emergency Fuel
Emergency fuel provides 30 minutes of fuel endurance based upon 1,500 ft AFE / Green Dot /
Clean.
Declare emergency fuel when the estimated fuel remaining on touchdown, at the airport of
intended landing, is less than 30 minutes fuel endurance or as specified in the "Emergency"
column of the Minimum/Emergency Fuel Table.
NOTE: When declaring emergency fuel, report fuel remaining in minutes to ATC and, time
permitting, to the dispatcher.
NOTE: The fuel indicators turn amber when fuel in the respective tank drops below 1,320 lb
(2,640 lb total). This amount of fuel does not directly equate to a specific fuel endurance
as it is a default value and does not reflect actual conditions. Refer to the flight release to
determine the exact fuel quantity that is associated with 45 minutes of fuel endurance for
an individual flight.
EMERGENCY AND ABNORMAL CHECKLISTS
It is not possible to develop checklists for every situation, especially when multiple failures
occur. The Emergency/Abnormal Checklists provided contain the procedural steps required to
resolve the situations on the ground or in flight.
QRH Immediate Action Item(s) are reviewed silently by the PM to ensure completion before
continuing the procedure. The remaining checklist items are read aloud to keep the other pilot
informed.
The QRH may state “Land at Nearest Suitable Airport.” Whenever this item is included, the
captain ensures an emergency is declared and the aircraft is turned without delay towards the
nearest suitable airport, in point of time, at which a safe landing can be made.
Some Emergency/Abnormal checklists state when a go-around is required, maintain VREF FULL
+ CORRECTION until the acceleration altitude is reached. In these cases, the FD guidance
(VREF corrected + 20 kt in AEO) may be used for go-around.
When more than one EICAS message appears at the same time, the crew performs the required
checklists according to the following message priority: WARNING (red), CAUTION (amber), and
ADVISORY (cyan). When more than one EICAS message within a category appears at the same
time, the crew analyzes and defines which one is dealt with first.
NOTE: Root EICAS messages are highlighted by a chevron “>.” These messages are possible
root causes for other failures and the corrective action, in most cases, is to perform the QRH
procedure for these messages first. Whenever a WARNING message is triggered together
with a root EICAS message, the procedure for the WARNING message must be performed
first. When multiple messages are displayed on EICAS, the possible failure is more likely to
be an electrical bus, MAU, or SPDA, in this order of priority.

EMERGENCY AND ABNORMAL PROTOCOL
The first crewmember recognizing a malfunction calls it out based on the identified EICAS
message or non-annunciated condition.
The PM cancels the Warning/Caution message.
GROUND
The captain calls for the checklist and the first officer reads and performs the actions.
IN FLIGHT
After the QRC or memory items are accomplished and when appropriate, the captain assigns,
delegates and manages workload duties based on TEAM. After the PM is designated, the PM
runs the checklist.
NOTE: Items referring to throttles, fire handles, IDG switches, and guarded switches are
confirmed by both pilots prior to being accomplished by the PM.
Unless the situation dictates otherwise, the priority for handling an emergency/abnormal situation
is:
• Memory items
• QRC items
• QRH items
• Normal checklists
When applicable, refer to the respective system synoptic page. Synoptic pages show detailed
information that can help identify aircraft system problems.
EMERGENCY QUICK REFERENCE CARD (QRC)
Pilots use the emergency QRC to complete immediate action items. Memory items are also
listed on the emergency QRC indicated by a black line box around the checklist procedure.
NON-ANNUNCIATED PROCEDURES
The Non-Annunciated Checklists describe procedures referring to a non-routine operation
requiring flight crew action, due to conditions that are not sensed by the airplane.
SMOKE PROCEDURES
The Smoke Checklists describe procedures associated with malfunctions leading to smoke in the
flight deck, cabin, or cargo compartments condition.

EMERGENCY DESCENT
This maneuver is designed to bring the airplane down to a safe altitude in the minimum amount
of time, when experiencing a rapid depressurization, or any other situation requiring immediate
and rapid loss of altitude. This procedure is accomplished using the QRC.
During an emergency descent, crew communication must be maintained in order to identify
possible pilot incapacitation.
When structural damage is suspected, use flight controls with care, limit speed as appropriate,
and evaluate the use of landing gear to expedite the descent. When turbulence is encountered,
reduce to turbulent air penetration speed.
LOSS OF ALTITUDE
When descending due to cabin depressurization, don oxygen masks and establish
communication immediately after verifying the cabin is depressurizing.
Once mask usage is not necessary, pilots must close the oxygen mask stowage box doors and
press the reset button, enabling hand/headset microphone booms.
DESCENDING WITH AUTOPILOT ENGAGED
Green FLCH mode is the recommended vertical mode due to airspeed and altitude protection, as
well as low crew workload.
Descend straight ahead or initiate a turn with a maximum of 30° bank. It is recommended to start
the turn before commencing the emergency descent.
When the cabin reaches 14,500 ft or above, set MASKS DEPLOY selector knob to OVRD
immediately.
The flight profile must be monitored, and TERRAIN set on MFD. At 1,000 ft to MEA or 10,000 ft
MSL , close the speed brakes and reduce to an appropriate speed.
CAUTION: During a cabin depressurization, the autopilot and autothrottle should be
engaged due to risk of pilot incapacitation.
DESCENDING WITH AUTOPILOT DISENGAGED
After selecting 10,000 ft MSL or MEA, whichever is higher, disconnect the autothrottle and set
thrust settings to idle. Then, smoothly open speed brake and turn FD OFF.
Smoothly lower the nose to an initial descent attitude (approximately 10 degrees nose
down). Approximately 10 kt before achieving target speed (VMO / MMO), slowly adjust pitch
attitude to maintain speed. When structural integrity is in doubt, especially after an explosive
depressurization, use flight controls with care, and maintain current cruise speed.
To better manage speed and thrust control, use the FLIGHT PATH VECTOR SPEED ERROR
TAPE and FPA ACCELERATION POINTER chevron.
When cabin reaches 14,500 ft, set MASKS DEPLOY selector knob to OVRD immediately.

EVACUATION
FLIGHT DECK EVACUATION
In the event of an evacuation, the flight deck crew exits the flight deck through the flight deck
door. When the door is jammed, the following procedure may be used:
JAMMED FLIGHT DECK DOOR PROCEDURE
Figure 7. JAMMED FLIGHT DECK DOOR PROCEDURE
DIRECT VIEW (DV) WINDOW EVACUATION PROCEDURE
When the flight deck door is unusable, the following procedure may be used:
1. Depress the lock button.
2. Pull the window handle in and back. Slide window aft.
3. Pull the escape rope from the overhead compartment.
4. Deploy the escape rope and exit the flight deck head first.
5. Carefully begin evacuation down the escape rope.
6. Using the escape rope loops, continue evacuating.
7. Upon reaching the ground, assist others, as necessary.

CABIN EVACUATION
The cabin evacuation is reproduced using the QRH and is found on the QRC. As the emergency
dictates, the PIC or SIC, when delegated, calls the flight attendant, and directs “ASSESS
AND WAIT FOR MY COMMAND” before initiating the emergency evacuation. At the PIC’s
discretion, an emergency evacuation may be initiated based on the environment created by
the emergency. Refer to Flight Operations Manual SP 3100 Chapter 11 Emergency/Irregular
Operations (Emergency Landing and Evacuation paragraph).

EMERGENCY LIGHTING
Emergency lighting consists of internal and external lights powered by dedicated Emergency
Lights Power Units (ELPU) connected to DC BUS 1. The battery charge is sufficient to supply all
emergency lights for approximately 10 minutes.
External emergency lighting is provided by:
• LED lights on each escape slide
Internal emergency lighting is provided by:
• Exit locator signs
• Exit marker signs
• Exit identifier signs
• Cabin/Flight deck emergency floodlights
• Passageway emergency exit floodlights
A flight deck light is located in the ceiling and provides general emergency illumination of the area.
Floor-level aisle emergency lighting is provided by photo-luminescent strips that must be charged
prior to the first flight of the day. Double red dots on the strips indicate the end of each exit
path. Exposure for 15 minutes in BRT mode or daylight provides sufficient luminescence for
emergency evacuation.

LEAST RISK BOMB LOCATION (LRBL)
The Least Risk Location is as close as possible to the center of the second from the last window
on the airplane left side.
ENHANCED LOCATOR TRANSMITTER (ELT)
A portable ELT is located in the flight deck.
UNRELIABLE AIRSPEED
Refer to Chapter 05 EMERGENCY / ABNORMAL PROCEDURES, UPSETS.
Unreliable airspeed indications can result from blocking or freezing of the pitot/static system, a
severely damaged or missing radome, or system malfunction. Early recognition of erroneous
airspeed indications require familiarity with the relationship of attitude, thrust setting, and
airspeed. A delay in recognition could result in loss of aircraft control. Should airspeed
indications become unreliable, pilots must be familiar with the approximate pitch attitude and
thrust setting for each phase of flight.
Pilots may recognize an unreliable airspeed indication by monitoring and crosschecking PFDs
and the IESS. IAS and ALT miscompare annunciations may identify an unreliable airspeed
condition.
When an unreliable airspeed indication is identified, the autopilot and autothrottle must be
disengaged. Immediately set a target pitch attitude and thrust setting for the phase of flight.
NOTE: The FD may also be unreliable and should not be followed.
The QRH Non-Annunciated section includes the UNRELIABLE AIRSPEED procedure. Tables
found in the QRH list pitch attitude and thrust settings for each phase of flight.
The use of speedbrakes should be avoided. The Flight Path Angle (FPA) on the PFD, altitude
and/or vertical speed indications may also be unreliable.
Radio altitude reference is available below 2,500 ft AGL. Ground speed indications are available
on the PFD. GPS altitude is available on the FMS by selecting NAV > POS SENSORS > GPS
STATUS.
Use electronic and visual glideslope indicators, where available, for approach and landing.
When the airplane is stabilized under correct pitch attitude and thrust setting, continue to
crosscheck the flight instruments in order to identify reliable instruments.

ENGINE VIBRATION
NOTE: When increased VIB levels can be attributed to ice build-up in flight due to operations
in IMC, proceed directly to the QRH procedure for ENGINE ABNORMAL VIBRATION.
When an amber LP VIB indication of 4.0 occurs during climb or cruise, the following techniques
should restore the VIB levels to the green range:
NOTE: Wait 10 seconds after attempting each technique before deciding on subsequent
course of action.
• Reduce Mach speed slightly,
• When LP VIB remains in the amber range at 4.0, disconnect the autothrottle and set the
associated thrust lever to IDLE. Advance the associated thrust lever to a minimum of 70% for
10 - 30 seconds. Then return the thrust lever to the position required for flight conditions and
reengage the autothrottle.
• When LP VIB is still in amber rate at 4.0, the following altitude reductions may be considered.
When above FL300, reduce altitude by at least 4,000 ft. When at or below FL300, reduce
altitude by at least 2,000 ft.
Prior to reducing altitude, the following elements should be considered:
– Terrain
– Fuel
– Weather
– Other relevant flight conditions
• If the throttle adjustment or altitude reduction brings fan VIBs back to the green range, QRH
procedures will no longer be necessary, and the flight can be continued.
• When LP VIB remains in the amber range at 4.0, accomplish the QRH procedure for ENGINE
ABNORMAL VIBRATION.
CAUTION: The procedures contained above are not applicable in any of the
following conditions:
• Vibration level is 4.1 or higher, or
• Vibration occurs in a flight phase other than climb or cruise.
When either of these conditions exist, accomplish the QRH procedure for
ENGINE ABNORMAL VIBRATION.
• Record the high VIB as a mechanical irregularity per Flight Operations Manual SP 3100
Chapter 06 Airworthiness (Mechanical Irregularities paragraph)
• Review Fan Vibration video created by GE located in SkyWest Online video library (Flight
Operations > Training > Training Home > Video eLibrary > ERJ 175 > Engine Vibration.

UPSETS
An upset is defined as unintentionally exceeding the parameters normally experienced in
line operations or training. In other words, the aircraft is not doing what it was commanded
or expected to do and is approaching unsafe parameters (i.e., stalls, roll upsets; nose high
and nose low attitudes). While specific values may vary among aircraft types, the following
unintentional conditions generally describe an aircraft upset:
• Pitch attitude greater than 25 degrees nose up,
• Pitch attitude greater than 10 degrees nose down,
• Bank angle greater than 45 degrees, or
• Flight within the above parameters, but at an airspeed inappropriate for the conditions.
During an upset, there may be a tendency to react too quickly and fixate on the situation. Pilots
must overcome the surprise and startle, analyze what the aircraft is doing, regain control, and
then determine and eliminate the cause of the upset.
Pilots must be prepared to use full flight control authority when the situation warrants. Smooth
control input is essential to avoid unacceptable load factors, secondary stalls, and overstressing
the aircraft.
Initial recovery should be accomplished using primary flight controls (ailerons, elevator).
Secondary control devices such as stabilizer trim, thrust and speedbrakes may be considered
incrementally to supplement primary flight control inputs.
Altitude loss is not the primary concern during any upset recovery.
STALLS
The aerodynamic stall is characterized by buffeting combined with some loss of lateral control. In
low-speed situations, an artificial stall warning is provided in advance of the aerodynamic stall in
the form of stick shaker. In icing conditions, an airplane may stall at much higher speeds and
lower angles of attack than normal.
IMPENDING STALL
An impending stall, or approach-to-stall, occurs when the angle of attack (AOA) causes a stall
warning either through inherent aerodynamics such as buffeting, or by synthetic means such as
a stick shaker or persistent aural-and-visual cues. It is a controlled flight condition and recovery
should be initiated at the first recognition of a stall warning.
WARNING: STICK SHAKER ACTIVATION AUTOMATICALLY DISENGAGES THE
AUTOPILOT. IN ICING CONDITIONS, THE AUTOPILOT MAY MASK HEAVY OR
ASYMMETRIC CONTROL FORCES DUE TO AIRFRAME ICING. THE AUTOPILOT
MAY EVEN DISCONNECT EARLIER BECAUSE OF EXCESSIVE ROLL RATES, ROLL
ANGLES, OR EXCESSIVE PITCH.
NOTE: The activation of the stick shaker and the pitch limiter indication and low speed
awareness indications are adjusted accordingly in icing conditions.
WARNING: THIS RECORD CONTAINS SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION THAT IS CONTROLLED UNDER 49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520. NO PART OF THIS RECORD MAY BE DISCLOSED TO PERSONS WITHOUT A "NEED TO KNOW", AS DEFINED IN
49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520, EXCEPT WITH THE WRITTEN PERMISSION OF THE ADMINISTRATOR OF TRANSPORTATION SECURITY ADMINISTRATION OR THE SECRETARY OF TRANSPORTATION. UNAUTHORIZED RELEASE MAY RESULT IN CIVIL

FULL STALL
A full stall is an out-of-control undesired state but should be recoverable given sufficient altitude.
It is identified by any one or a combination of the following characteristics:
• An uncommanded nose-down pitch that cannot be readily arrested that may be accompanied
by an uncommanded rolling motion.
• Buffeting of a magnitude and severity that becomes a strong and effective deterrent to
further increases in AOA.
• No further increase in pitch occurs when the pitch control is held at the full aft stop, leading to
an inability to arrest descent rate.
UPSET PREVENTION AND RECOVERY
To avoid the possibility of an upset, situational awareness and pilot monitoring must be
maintained at all times during flight. If airspeed is allowed to deteriorate below safe margins
and/or the nose attitude becomes too high, performance may become so limited the airplane
will not be capable of maintaining altitude and recovering airspeed. At high angles of attack,
the engines may experience surging or a compressor stall, possibly leading to loss of thrust,
flameout and/or core lock.
The upset recovery procedure is the same for all upsets, including an impending or full stall. The
emphasis of upset recovery is to effectively reduce the AOA. Minimizing altitude loss becomes
secondary to regaining normal flight.
Pilots must accept that reducing the airplane’s AOA will normally result in altitude loss. At high
altitudes, recovery will likely require the loss of several thousand feet or more in order to regain
adequate airspeed and return the airplane to normal flight. The amount of altitude loss will be
affected by the airplane’s operational environment (e.g., entry altitude, density altitude, airplane
weight, bank angle, airplane configuration, etc.).
Troubleshooting the cause of the upset is also secondary to initiating the recovery. For example, if
a terrain warning is encountered during an upset recovery, the upset recovery takes precedence.
In this example, once the recovery is complete, a CFIT escape maneuver would be executed.
Do not delay a recovery due to degrading airspeed or a stall event in order to obtain ATC
clearance to a lower altitude. Declare an emergency, as necessary.

UPSET RECOVERY PROCEDURE
Each step must be correctly applied and executed before moving on to the next step.
RECOGNIZE AND CONFIRM
Upon recognizing an upset situation either pilot will call out “UPSET.” Assess the energy state of
the aircraft. The upset is confirmed by cross checking the flight instruments, and if available, by
using outside references.
DISCONNECT
Disconnect the autothrottle. Do not follow the flight director during the recovery.
PUSH
Unload the aircraft. Although the PUSH step does not always require forward pressure, it will
almost always call for unloading the aircraft. Unloading the airplane improves roll effectiveness
even when beyond 90 degrees of bank. Unloading to 0.5 g, “feeling light in the seat,” maintains
positive g-load and decreases stall speed by 30%.
Manage the angle of attack. Apply smooth, deliberate, and positive control inputs to avoid
unacceptable load factors and secondary stalls. Avoid cyclical or oscillatory control inputs to
prevent exceeding the structural limits of the airplane.
PUSH is accomplished with neutral ailerons and rudder. AOA must be reduced prior to rolling.
Applying rudder during an upset, unless required for adverse yaw, may result in a spin, or lead
to structural failure.
Trim, as necessary.
WARNING: IF THE AIRPLANE IS STALLED, RECOVER FROM THE STALLED
CONDITION BEFORE CONTINUING WITH THE REMAINDER OF THE UPSET
RECOVERY PROCEDURE. MAINTAIN REDUCED AOA UNTIL STICK SHAKER
CEASES AND AIRSPEED IS GREATER THAN GREEN DOT.
WARNING: AT HIGH ALTITUDES, MAINTAIN NOSE DOWN -5 TO -10 DEGREES
UNTIL THE AIRCRAFT IS OUT OF THE STALL OR SLOW SPEED CONDITION. IN
EXTREME SITUATIONS, INCREASED NOSE DOWN ATTITUDE MAY BE REQUIRED.
ROLL
Manage the lift vector orientation by rolling toward the sky pointer the shortest direction to wings
level without pulling on the elevator. Avoid pulling altogether.
NOTE: During a nose high upset, if the elevator is ineffective at reducing pitch, roll aircraft to
a bank angle that starts the nose down. The angle of bank should not normally exceed 60
degrees.
WARNING: THIS RECORD CONTAINS SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION THAT IS CONTROLLED UNDER 49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520. NO PART OF THIS RECORD MAY BE DISCLOSED TO PERSONS WITHOUT A "NEED TO KNOW", AS DEFINED IN
49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520, EXCEPT WITH THE WRITTEN PERMISSION OF THE ADMINISTRATOR OF TRANSPORTATION SECURITY ADMINISTRATION OR THE SECRETARY OF TRANSPORTATION. UNAUTHORIZED RELEASE MAY RESULT IN CIVIL

THRUST
Manage aircraft energy. Use thrust and speedbrakes as required. Up to maximum thrust may
be necessary.
WARNING: HIGH ENERGY ATTITUDES MAY REQUIRE IMMEDIATE REDUCTION
OF THRUST.
NOTE: Adding thrust with underwing mounted engines will cause a pitching up moment,
increasing angle of attack and possibly aggravating the upset.
NOTE: In normal mode, the fly-by-wire functionalities automatically neutralize any pitch up
tendency caused by engine thrust. In this case, applying MAX thrust during the stall recovery
procedure should not result in a pitch up tendency. Additionally, setting the thrust levers
to MAX position automatically disengages the autothrottle and retracts the speedbrakes,
reducing the workload during the recovery.
NOTE: In direct mode, no fly-by-wire high-level functionality is available. More control column
input may be required to produce the same pitch change.
STABILIZE
Return to a normal flight path (i.e., applicable profile and configuration).
After stabilizing the aircraft, implement the Threat and Error Management Model (TEAM) as
applicable.
WARNING: EXCESSIVE USE OF PITCH TRIM OR RUDDER MAY AGGRAVATE THE
UPSET OR MAY RESULT IN EXCESSIVE G-LOADS.
WARNING: AT HIGH ALTITUDES SMOOTHLY RETURN TO NORMAL FLIGHT,
REMAINING AT, OR ABOVE GREEN DOT.

UPSET RECOVERY – ACTIONS AND CALLOUTS
PF PM
Recognize and Confirm
upset
Cross-check instruments. Use outside references, if able
The pilot first noticing the condition announces:
“UPSET”
Disconnect Disengage autopilot and
autothrottles
Push Reduce angle of attack by applying
nose down elevator (push) with
neutral ailerons
Roll Roll shortest direction toward sky
pointer to wings level
Thrust Use thrust and speedbrakes as
necessary
Monitor airspeed and altitude
throughout recovery (1)
Announce aircraft state (2)
Call out terrain (3)
Verify all required actions are
accomplished and call out any
omissions
Calls out “GREEN DOT”
When accelerating past green
dot.
Stabilize Reestablish desired flight path and
configuration
Notify ATC of need for altitude
and/or course change.
Declare emergency, as
necessary
NOTE - Callouts are shown in bold text.
(1) PM is responsible for monitoring all aspects of the upset recovery and must call out any
observed discrepancies or deviations, according to the Deviations and Callouts table,
specific to the phase of flight.
(2) PM states “CLIMBING” or “DESCENDING” based on the VSI indication until the aircraft
has returned to stabilized flight (applicable profile configuration, airspeed, and altitude).
(3) When the radar altimeter indicates less than 1,000 ft AGL and the airplane is descending,
the PM calls “DESCENDING (___)”, based on the RA value observed. Example,
“DESCENDING 700,” “DESCENDING 600.”

06 DEFINITIONS

Submitted: 07 AUG 23
SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
DEFINITIONS
Page: 06-01.2 of 12
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
DEFINITIONS
abnormal
When abnormal is used to describe a procedure or checklist, it refers to a non-routine
operation in which certain procedures or actions must be taken to maintain an acceptable
level of airworthiness for a continued safe flight and landing. When associated with
a caution condition message, it will indicate procedures that may result in damage to
equipment if not followed. The abnormal procedures have been written assuming oxygen
masks will be donned, and communications established between crewmembers whenever
oxygen deficiency or contamination is suspected.
above ground level; acronym AGL
The height an aircraft is above the ground (terrain elevation). Used in conjunction or
alternatively with Mean Sea Level (MSL) to calculate altitudes for aviators.
acceleration altitude
It is defined as Level Off Altitude where the climb configuration (flaps up, final segment
climb speed) is reached, with the critical engine inoperative, and takeoff thrust on the
operating engine. It is the 3rd takeoff segment and is used for level flight, acceleration,
and flap retraction at a height of 1,000 ft AFE, unless a different altitude is required by
a Special Departure Procedure.
advisory
Used to indicate a condition that requires crew awareness and may require subsequent or
future crew actions.
additional crewmember; acronym ACM
An individual who occupies the flight deck jumpseat.
aircraft operator employee
An individual employed and paid directly by the aircraft operator.
aircraft search checklist
Provided by Station operations. The checklist is designed to document the full aircraft
search and visual inspection. The forms are located on SkyWest Online > Info Center >
Documents > Forms > Safety tab > Forms > Security > Aircraft Search Checklist (applicable
partner).
WARNING: THIS RECORD CONTAINS SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION THAT IS CONTROLLED UNDER 49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520. NO PART OF THIS RECORD MAY BE DISCLOSED TO PERSONS WITHOUT A "NEED TO KNOW", AS DEFINED IN
49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520, EXCEPT WITH THE WRITTEN PERMISSION OF THE ADMINISTRATOR OF TRANSPORTATION SECURITY ADMINISTRATION OR THE SECRETARY OF TRANSPORTATION. UNAUTHORIZED RELEASE MAY RESULT IN CIVIL
PENALTY OR OTHER ACTION. FOR U.S. GOVERNMENT AGENCIES, PUBLIC DISCLOSURE IS GOVERNED BY 5 U.S.C. 552 AND 49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520.
ERJ 175 STANDARD OPERATING PROCEDURES
MANUAL
06 DEFINITIONS

Submitted: 07 AUG 23
SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
DEFINITIONS
Page: 06-01.3 of 12
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
areas of responsibility
Flight deck areas operated by a specific pilot. These areas exist to improve crew
coordination. A pilot must advise the other pilot if intending to operate something outside
their area of responsibility. Refer to the Normal Procedures section to determine what
areas are assigned to which pilot.
atmospheric pressure at a specific airfield; acronym QFE
Altimeter setting referenced to airport field elevation.
attended aircraft
An aircraft to which a SkyWest employee or authorized representative ensures access is
limited to authorized individuals and property.
authorized representative
A person who is not a direct employee of the aircraft operator but is authorized to act on the
aircraft operator’s behalf to perform measures required by the AOSSP.
callouts
Callouts are aids in maintaining awareness for the crew as to the status of given tasks. They
are extremely important in aiding situational awareness; made to indicate that a system has
deviated from the assigned parameters, or to describe tasks and events requiring a high
level of monitoring of highly dynamic and unstable events.
captain ; acronym CP
The pilot who, while on duty, is in command of the aircraft and its crewmembers; also known
as Pilot-in-Command (PIC). Responsible for the manipulation of the flight controls of an
aircraft underway which includes taxiing, takeoff, and landing. Must be properly qualified
and designated by the Company to serve as PIC and must hold a current effective airman’s
certificate authorizing the pilot to serve as such.
caution
Precautionary statement describing a potential hazard that may cause injuries or minor
damage. A caution graphic of an exclamation point in a yellow diamond is used in
conjunction with the caution.
WARNING: THIS RECORD CONTAINS SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION THAT IS CONTROLLED UNDER 49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520. NO PART OF THIS RECORD MAY BE DISCLOSED TO PERSONS WITHOUT A "NEED TO KNOW", AS DEFINED IN
49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520, EXCEPT WITH THE WRITTEN PERMISSION OF THE ADMINISTRATOR OF TRANSPORTATION SECURITY ADMINISTRATION OR THE SECRETARY OF TRANSPORTATION. UNAUTHORIZED RELEASE MAY RESULT IN CIVIL
PENALTY OR OTHER ACTION. FOR U.S. GOVERNMENT AGENCIES, PUBLIC DISCLOSURE IS GOVERNED BY 5 U.S.C. 552 AND 49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520.

06 DEFINITIONS

Submitted: 07 AUG 23
SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
DEFINITIONS
Page: 06-01.4 of 12
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
climb sequence
The act of increasing altitude by use of power.
The logical phase of a typical flight (climb phase or climbout) following takeoff and preceding
the cruise, during which an increase in altitude to a predetermined level is effected.
Computerized Airplane Flight Manual; acronym CAFM
A digital version of the Airplane Flight Manual (AFM) utilized for detailed aircraft
performance calculations.
contaminated runway
A runway is considered contaminated when more than 25% of the runway surface area
(whether in isolated areas or not), within the reported length and width being used, is
covered by more than 1/8 in (3 mm) of standing water or its equivalent in slush, wet snow,
dry snow, or any depth of ice or compacted snow.
customer service agent; acronym CSA
Aircraft operator employee or authorized representative who has the responsibility for
ensuring the aircraft is attended, monitored, or secured, conducting the full aircraft search,
sealing applicable doors and compartments, and completing their portion of an Aircraft
Search Checklist.
dark and quiet cockpit
A concept used to design and operate an airplane based on the assumption that in flight all
systems are normal when the overhead, main, glare-shield and control pedestal panels
have no lights on; and no aural warnings are taking place. This concept enforces the need
of a quiet environment inside the cockpit during most phases of flight.
derated takeoff thrust
A takeoff thrust level, less than the maximum takeoff thrust approved, for which a separate
and specific set of data exists. When operating with a derated takeoff thrust, the thrust
setting parameter used to establish thrust for takeoff is presented in the AFM, or as
determined by AeroData performance, and is considered an operating limit for that takeoff.
direct vision (DV) window
A window on each side of the windshield that can be opened for viewing outside or as
an emergency exit.

06 DEFINITIONS

Submitted: 07 AUG 23
SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
DEFINITIONS
Page: 06-01.5 of 12
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
dry runway
A runway is dry when it is neither wet nor contaminated.
emergency procedure or checklist
When emergency is used to describe a procedure or checklist, it refers to a non-routine
operation (warning) in which certain procedures or actions must be taken to protect the
crew, the passengers, or the airplane from a serious or potential hazard. When associated
with a warning EICAS message, an emergency will indicate procedures that may result
in personal injury or loss of life if not followed. The emergency procedures have been
written assuming oxygen masks will be donned, and communications established between
crewmembers whenever their use is required.
emergency
A serious situation that happens unexpectedly and demands immediate action.
expanded procedures
A description of sequential procedural steps, with detailed explanatory descriptions and/or
instructions accompanying each step.
first flight of the day; acronym FFOD
The first flight of the day for an aircraft.
first officer ; acronym FO
A pilot who is Second-in-Command (SIC) and any part of whose duty is to assist or relieve
the Captain in the manipulation of the aircraft controls including takeoff and landing of
such aircraft. A first officer must be properly qualified and hold a current effective airman's
certificate authorizing them to serve as such, and who is designated by the Company
to serve as a first officer.
flex takeoff thrust
A takeoff method, also known as assumed temperature takeoff thrust, using less thrust than
maximum takeoff thrust.
flight attendant; acronym FA
Individual who has successfully completed training requirements to ensure in-flight safety,
pursuant to FAA and Company regulations. This crewmember is responsible for the safety
and comfort of the passengers aboard an aircraft.
WARNING: THIS RECORD CONTAINS SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION THAT IS CONTROLLED UNDER 49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520. NO PART OF THIS RECORD MAY BE DISCLOSED TO PERSONS WITHOUT A "NEED TO KNOW", AS DEFINED IN
49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520, EXCEPT WITH THE WRITTEN PERMISSION OF THE ADMINISTRATOR OF TRANSPORTATION SECURITY ADMINISTRATION OR THE SECRETARY OF TRANSPORTATION. UNAUTHORIZED RELEASE MAY RESULT IN CIVIL
PENALTY OR OTHER ACTION. FOR U.S. GOVERNMENT AGENCIES, PUBLIC DISCLOSURE IS GOVERNED BY 5 U.S.C. 552 AND 49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520.

06 DEFINITIONS

Submitted: 07 AUG 23
SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
DEFINITIONS
Page: 06-01.6 of 12
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
Flight Mode Annunciator; acronym FMA
full aircraft search
A thorough physical search of the aircraft interior, accessible compartments in the
passenger cabin and flight deck that do not require special tools to open, exterior accessible
compartments, and the empty baggage compartment.
ground loading bridge; acronym GLB
Provides a means for passengers to transit from the ramp into and out of the aircraft cabin
without utilizing the main cabin door (MCD) steps.
ground security coordinator; acronym GSC
An airline employee or authorized representative of an airline who is designated to interface
with crewmembers, TSA, law enforcement officers, and others in matters of security.
Each aircraft operator must designate and use a GSC for each domestic and international
flight departure.
guarded
Dedicated personnel assigned to be physically present at the aircraft to prevent
unauthorized access. These personnel may not be assigned additional duties while
guarding an aircraft. This may occur when an airport threat level increases from Elevated to
Imminent in the United States or in international locations.
icing conditions
Conditions conducive to icing exist on the ground and for takeoff exist when the OAT (on
the ground/ takeoff) or TAT (in flight) is 10° C or less; and
• When operating on ramps, taxiways, or runways where surface snow, ice, standing
water, or slush may be ingested by the engines or freeze on engine nacelles or engine
sensor probes.
• Visible moisture in any form is encountered (e.g., clouds, rain, snow, sleet, or ice
crystals) except when the SAT is -40°C (40°F) or below.
Immediate Action
An action that must be taken so quickly in response to a nonroutine event so quickly that
reference to a checklist is not practical, because of a potential loss of aircraft control,
incapacitation of a crewmember, or damage to or loss of an airplane component or system–
which would make continued safe flight improbable.
WARNING: THIS RECORD CONTAINS SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION THAT IS CONTROLLED UNDER 49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520. NO PART OF THIS RECORD MAY BE DISCLOSED TO PERSONS WITHOUT A "NEED TO KNOW", AS DEFINED IN
49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520, EXCEPT WITH THE WRITTEN PERMISSION OF THE ADMINISTRATOR OF TRANSPORTATION SECURITY ADMINISTRATION OR THE SECRETARY OF TRANSPORTATION. UNAUTHORIZED RELEASE MAY RESULT IN CIVIL
PENALTY OR OTHER ACTION. FOR U.S. GOVERNMENT AGENCIES, PUBLIC DISCLOSURE IS GOVERNED BY 5 U.S.C. 552 AND 49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520.

06 DEFINITIONS

Submitted: 07 AUG 23
SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
DEFINITIONS
Page: 06-01.7 of 12
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
improvised explosive device; acronym IED
A device fabricated in an improvised manner that incorporates explosives, or destructive,
lethal, noxious, pyrotechnic, or incendiary chemicals in its design. Generally, an IED will be
composed of an explosive, a power supply, a switch or timer, and a detonator or initiator.
In-flight Security Coordinator; acronym ISC
The PIC of a flight who performs duties specified in the aircraft operator’s security program
(14 CFR Part 108.215).
land at the nearest suitable airport
Some checklists require landing at the nearest suitable airport. This statement may be
presented below the associated emergency/abnormal characterization or at the beginning
of a task that requires such. When it appears at the beginning of the checklist, it should be
the first task accomplished. Anytime the crew determines that a significant threat to safety
is present, they should always accomplish the earliest possible descent, land at the nearest
suitable airport, regardless of having this statement present in the checklist.
last flight of the day; acronym LFOD
Last flight of the day for the aircraft.
LDA offset
An angular offset of approximately 3° of the Localizer-type Directional Aid (LDA) from
the runway extended centerline in a direction away from the No Transgression Zone that
increases the normal operating zone width.
maximum performance landing
Maximum performance landing is a set of techniques that leads to stopping the aircraft
within a minimum landing distance.
memory items
Items on the checklist that must be memorized by the flight crew and whose execution
must be carried out immediately should the corresponding checklist become applicable
(immediate action).
mobile bridge adapter; acronym MBA
A device used to bridge the gap between the passenger loading bridge and a regional
aircraft’s main cabin door.

49 CFR PARTS 15 AND 1520, EXCEPT WITH THE WRITTEN PERMISSION OF THE ADMINISTRATOR OF TRANSPORTATION SECURITY ADMINISTRATION OR THE SECRETARY OF TRANSPORTATION. UNAUTHORIZED RELEASE MAY RESULT IN CIVIL

06 DEFINITIONS

Submitted: 07 AUG 23
SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
DEFINITIONS
Page: 06-01.8 of 12
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
monitored
To observe the area in or around an aircraft by either physically being in the area or via
closed circuit television (CCTV) to ensure there is no unauthorized access to the area being
observed, no unattended baggage/property, or any items that do not belong.
Multifunction Display Control Unit; acronym MDCU
non-annunciated
When non-annunciated is used to describe a procedure or checklist, it refers to a
non-routine operation requiring flight crew action, due to a condition not capable to be
sensed by the airplane avionics system (e.g., smoke, emergency descent).
passenger loading bridge; acronym PLB
A structure designed to allow passengers to enplane and deplane between the terminal
and aircraft.
Pilot-in-Command; acronym PIC
The pilot who:
(1) Has final authority and is responsible for the operation and safety of the flight,
(2) Has been designated as PIC before or during the flight, and
(3) Holds the appropriate category, class, and type rating, when appropriate, for the
conduct of the flight
pilot flying; acronym PF
The pilot who is controlling the path of the aircraft at any given time, whether or not the
aircraft is in flight or on the ground.
pilot incapacitation
A situation where one of the pilots is not able to perform their duties.
pilot monitoring; acronym PM
Pilot monitoring is the responsibility and role of both the PF and PM. It is a systematic, active
observation and participation that involves crosschecking and feedback, thus enabling
decision making, ensuring timely action, and increasing safety.
precision-like approach
A procedural and technical method of flying all non-precision approaches with a stabilized
vertical descent path to the runway end.

06 DEFINITIONS

Submitted: 07 AUG 23
SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
DEFINITIONS
Page: 06-01.9 of 12
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
Precision Radar Monitor; acronym PRM
Provides air traffic controllers with high precision secondary surveillance data for aircraft
on final approach to parallel runways that have extended centerlines separated by less
than 4,300 ft.
prohibited items
Items that cannot be transported in checked baggage, accessible property, or on an
individual’s person, and which are not permitted in the sterile area (see Prohibited Items
List on the TSA website at www.tsa.gov).
Items that may not be brought into a country from another country.
quick reference card; acronym QRC
A card in which information can be quickly accessed in case of an emergency situation.
Also known as a quick reference.
quick reference handbook; acronym QRH
Carried onboard an aircraft for crewmember guidance when an emergency or abnormal
event occurs.
Second-in-Command; acronym SIC
Responsible for the safe and effective operation of the aircraft as directed by the
Pilot-in-Command (PIC) and Company-approved procedures.
secured aircraft
An aircraft that is attended, monitored, or sealed to prevent unauthorized access.
security seal
A sticker seal used to secure exterior and interior compartments and equipment. The
sticker is a tamper-evident seal.
simultaneous offset instrument approaches; acronym SOIA
Simultaneous approaches to a set of parallel runways utilizing a straight-in ILS approach to
one runway and an LDA with a glide path instrument approach to the other runway.
situational awareness
Refers to the requirement that the crew must be conscious of the airplane's condition and
status under specific operational and environmental circumstances.

06 DEFINITIONS

Submitted: 07 AUG 23
SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
DEFINITIONS
Page: 06-01.10 of 12
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
speeds
Approach Climb Speed (VAC) - The speed to be used in case of a single engine go-around
with Go-around Flaps and Landing Gear retracted.
Approach Speed ( VAP) - The speed on the final approach, in landing configuration.
Decision Speed (V1) - The maximum speed during takeoff at which the pilot may abort the
takeoff to stop the airplane within the accelerate-stop distance available. V1 is also the
minimum speed at which the pilot can continue the takeoff and achieve the required height
above the runway or the clearway within the takeoff distance available when a failure of the
critical engine is identified immediately after V1.
Final Segment Speed (Vfs) - The speed to be achieved during the final takeoff segment,
with landing gear up and flaps retracted.
Landing Reference Speed (VREF) - A reference for VAP calculation. It is the minimum
recommended speed at 50 ft over the threshold. It is the speed used in the landing distance
calculations.
Rotation Speed (Vr) - The speed at which the pilot initiates action to raise the nose in
order to takeoff.
Takeoff Safety Speed (V2) - The speed to be attained at a height (e.g., 35 ft) when rotation
is initiated at Vr, following a failure of the critical engine at VEF.
stabilized approach
Criteria used to judge an approach according to the capability of the airplane to perform a
safe landing after a determined point at the approach procedure.
station sealing log
The document used to record the numbers of the seals applied to the aircraft.
status
Used to indicate a condition that requires flight deck indications, not necessarily as part
of the integrated warning system.
sterile area
A portion of an airport, defined in the airport security program that provides passengers
access to boarding aircraft and to which the access generally is controlled by TSA or by
an aircraft operator under 49 CFR Part 1544 or 49 CFR Part 1546 through the screening
of persons and property.

Submitted: 07 AUG 23
SENSITIVE SECURITY INFORMATION
DEFINITIONS
Page: 06-01.11 of 12
Revision: 11
Date: 11 DEC 23
supplementary procedures
Used to describe non-routine procedures that may be employed in addition to a normal
procedure. Infrequently used procedures should be performed by reference (read and do).
transition altitude
The altitude above sea level at which aircraft change from the use of altitude to flight levels.
Above the transition altitude, the altimeter setting is adjusted to the standard pressure
setting.
Aircraft Search for Flights To and From Canada; Canada Search
A visual inspection of the cabin and flight deck to remove items that do not belong for
flights involving Canada.
Performed:
(1) Prior to passenger boarding when departing the United States to Canada and
departing Canada to the United States.
(2) After passengers have deplaned upon landing at an intermediate stop within Canada.
warning
Major damage, major injury, or death is possible if the policy/procedure is not followed. A
warning graphic of an exclamation point in a red triangle is used in conjunction with the
warning.

Nature of emergency – Conditions causing emergency
landing or ditching.
Time – Available time for preparation before landing.
Signal – Signal used for evacuation or non-evacuation of the
aircraft once aircraft has landed.
Brace – Determine when brace position will be necessary.
Other – Any additional information (e.g., passenger
briefing).
